Pharmaceutical Analysis 
Spectroscopy 
Spectroscopy is broadly defined as the branch of science that studies the interaction of matter with light. 
Historically, it was understood as the "study of specific values or numbers" related to EMR. Alternatively, it 
can be defined as the study of the interaction of electromagnetic radiation (EMR) with matter. 
 
Electromagnetic Radiation consists of discrete packages of energy called photons. Each photon 
comprises an oscillating electric field (E) and an oscillating magnetic field (M), which are 
perpendicular to each other. 
It is important to differentiate between several related terms: 
 
Spectroscopy: The general study of the interaction between matter and radiant energy. 
 
Spectrometry: Involves the measurement of a spectrum where the spectrum is not necessarily 
formed by the emission or absorption of electromagnetic radiations, such as in Mass Spectrometry, 
which deals with fragments of masses. 
 
Spectrophotometry: The study of a portion of the spectrum formed by EMR, where there is emission 
or absorption of light in the UV, visible, or IR regions. 
Principle 
The principle of spectroscopy is based on the measurement of the spectrum of a sample containing 
atoms or molecules. A spectrum is typically presented as a graph of the intensity of absorbed or emitted 
radiation by a sample versus its frequency (ν) or wavelength (λ). The instrument designed for this 
measurement is called a spectrometer.  
A spectrophotometer specifically measures the amount of EMR absorbed, emitted, or transmitted upon 
interaction with matter and consists of two main components:  
1. A spectrometer (which generates EMR of a particular wavelength)  
2. A photometer (which measures the intensity of radiation after interaction with matter). 
There are two main types of spectroscopy based on the interaction measured: 
 
Absorption Spectroscopy: This analytical technique measures the absorption of electromagnetic 
radiation by a sample. Examples include UV/Visible Spectroscopy (UV: 190-400 nm, Visible: 400-800 
nm) and IR Spectroscopy (0.76-15 μm). 
 
Emission Spectroscopy: This technique involves the dispersion of emission (of a particle or 
radiation) according to some property of the emission, and the amount of dispersion is measured. 
An example is ICP Spectroscopy. 
Spectroscopic Methods 
1. Atomic Absorption and Emission Spectroscopy 
These techniques are used primarily for the detection and quantification of metals and metalloids.

--- OCR TEXT ---
Pharmaceutical Analysis
Spectroscopy

Spectroscopy is broadly defined as the branch of science that studies the interaction of matter with light.
Historically, it was understood as the "study of specific values or numbers" related to EMR. Alternatively, it
can be defined as the study of the interaction of electromagnetic radiation (EMR) with matter.

e Electromagnetic Radiation consists of discrete packages of energy called photons. Each photon
comprises an oscillating electric field (E) and an oscillating magnetic field (M), which are
perpendicular to each other.

It is important to differentiate between several related terms:
e Spectroscopy: The general study of the interaction between matter and radiant energy.

e Spectrometry: Involves the measurement of a spectrum where the spectrum is not necessarily
formed by the emission or absorption of electromagnetic radiations, such as in Mass Spectrometry,
which deals with fragments of masses.

e Spectrophotometry: The study of a portion of the spectrum formed by EMR, where there is emission
or absorption of light in the UV, visible, or IR regions.

Principle

The principle of spectroscopy is based on the measurement of the spectrum of a sample containing
atoms or molecules. A spectrum is typically presented as a graph of the intensity of absorbed or emitted
radiation by asample versus its frequency (v) or wavelength (A). The instrument designed for this
measurement is called a spectrometer.

A spectrophotometer specifically measures the amount of EMR absorbed, emitted, or transmitted upon
interaction with matter and consists of two main components:

1. Aspectrometer (which generates EMR of a particular wavelength)
2. Aphotometer (which measures the intensity of radiation after interaction with matter).
There are two main types of spectroscopy based on the interaction measured:

+ Absorption Spectroscopy: This analytical technique measures the absorption of electromagnetic
radiation by a sample. Examples include UV/Visible Spectroscopy (UV: 190-400 nm, Visible: 400-800
nm) and IR Spectroscopy (0.76-15 um).

+ Emission Spectroscopy: This technique involves the dispersion of emission (of a particle or
radiation) according to some property of the emission, and the amount of dispersion is measured.
An example is ICP Spectroscopy.

Spectroscopic Methods
1. Atomic Absorption and Emission Spectroscopy

These techniques are used primarily for the detection and quantification of metals and metalloids.

============================================================
PAGE 8
============================================================
1.1 
Atomic Absorption Spectroscopy (AAS) 
I. 
Introduction 
AAS measures the concentrations of metals and metalloids by utilizing the absorption of ultraviolet or 
visible light by these elements. The technique was first introduced in 1955 by Walsh. 
II. 
Principle 
When a beam of electromagnetic radiation of a particular wavelength passes through atoms in a vaporized 
form, ground state atoms are excited due to the absorption of radiation. This causes electrons to 
transition to higher electronic energy levels. The decrease in the intensity of radiation is directly related to 
the number of ground state atoms in the sample. The analyte's concentration is determined from the 
amount of absorption, following the relationship that Absorbance is proportional to concentration. The 
change in energy involved in this transition can be calculated by the equation ΔE = E  - E  = hν = hc/λ, 
λ is 
wavelength. 
III. 
Instrumentation  
An Atomic Absorption Spectrometer typically consists of five principal components: 
 
Light Source (Hollow Cathode Lamp): This lamp contains a tungsten anode and a hollow 
cylindrical cathode made of the specific element to be measured, sealed in a glass tube with an 
inert gas like neon or argon. When a potential difference is applied, gas atoms ionize, bombard the 
cathode, and eject metal atoms (sputtering). Some sputtered atoms emit characteristic radiation as 
they return to the ground state. This radiation is concentrated into a beam that passes through the 
sample. Different elements absorb characteristic wavelengths, so a lamp containing a specific 
element (e.g., lead) emits light that is absorbed by lead atoms in the sample. 
 
Beam Chopper: Positioned between the hollow cathode lamp and the atomizer (flame), it rotates to 
convert the steady light into intermittent, pulsating light. 
 
Atomizer: Its role is to convert the sample into a gaseous, free atomic state where atoms are 
available to absorb radiation.  
o Flame Atomization: This method is used for liquid and solution samples. The sample is 
introduced into a nebulizer, which disperses it into tiny droplets. This fine mist is then mixed 
with fuel (e.g., acetylene) and an oxidant (e.g., nitrous oxide) and burned. The flame's 
temperature is crucial as it influences the distribution of atoms. Processes occurring in the 
flame include nebulization (liquid to spray), desolvation (solid atoms mixed with gaseous 
fuel), and volatilization (solid atoms converted to vapor). 
o Graphite Furnace Atomization: This technique is employed when sample size is small or 
greater sensitivity is required. It can analyze liquid, solid, semi-solid, and solution samples. 
The sample is dried, burned to ash, and then atomized by heating in the graphite furnace. 
 
Monochromator: This critical component selects the specific wavelength of light absorbed by the 
sample, ensuring that other wavelengths are excluded. This selectivity allows for the determination 
of a chosen element even in the presence of others.

--- OCR TEXT ---
1.1 Atomic Absorption Spectroscopy (AAS)
I. Introduction

AAS measures the concentrations of metals and metalloids by utilizing the absorption of ultraviolet or
visible light by these elements. The technique was first introduced in 1955 by Walsh.

Il. Principle

When a beam of electromagnetic radiation of a particular wavelength passes through atoms in a vaporized
form, ground state atoms are excited due to the absorption of radiation. This causes electrons to
transition to higher electronic energy levels. The decrease in the intensity of radiation is directly related to
the number of ground state atoms in the sample. The analyte’s concentration is determined from the
amount of absorption, following the relationship that Absorbance is proportional to concentration. The
change in energy involved in this transition can be calculated by the equation AE = E, - E, =hv=he/A,
where E, is excited energy, Eo is ground state energy, his Planck's constant, c is the velocity of light, and A is
wavelength.

If. Instrumentation
An Atomic Absorption Spectrometer typically consists of five principal components:

e Light Source (Hollow Cathode Lamp): This lamp contains a tungsten anode and a hollow
cylindrical cathode made of the specific element to be measured, sealed in a glass tube with an
inert gas like neon or argon. When a potential difference is applied, gas atoms ionize, bombard the
cathode, and eject metal atoms (sputtering). Some sputtered atoms emit characteristic radiation as
they return to the ground state. This radiation is concentrated into a beam that passes through the
sample. Different elements absorb characteristic wavelengths, so a lamp containing a specific
element (e.g., lead) emits light that is absorbed by lead atoms in the sample.

¢ Beam Chopper: Positioned between the hollow cathode lamp and the atomizer (flame), it rotates to
convert the steady light into intermittent, pulsating light.

e Atomizer: Its role is to convert the sample into a gaseous, free atomic state where atoms are
available to absorb radiation.

o Flame Atomization: This method is used for liquid and solution samples. The sample is
introduced into a nebulizer, which disperses it into tiny droplets. This fine mist is then mixed
with fuel (e.g., acetylene) and an oxidant (e.g., nitrous oxide) and burned. The flame's
temperature is crucial as it influences the distribution of atoms. Processes occurring in the
flame include nebulization (liquid to spray), desolvation (solid atoms mixed with gaseous
fuel), and volatilization (solid atoms converted to vapor).

o Graphite Furnace Atomization: This technique is employed when sample size is small or
greater sensitivity is required. It can analyze liquid, solid, semi-solid, and solution samples.
The sample is dried, burned to ash, and then atomized by heating in the graphite furnace.

e¢ Monochromator: This critical component selects the specific wavelength of light absorbed by the
sample, ensuring that other wavelengths are excluded. This selectivity allows for the determination
of a chosen element even in the presence of others.

============================================================
PAGE 9
============================================================
 
Detector and Read out Device: A photomultiplier tube is typically used to convert the selected light 
signal into an electrical signal proportional to the light intensity. This electrical signal is then 
amplified and displayed or sent to a data station for printout. 
IV. 
Working of AAS 
In practice, the instrument is calibrated to zero absorbance with a blank (unionized water) sprayed into 
the flame, and unsaturated light from the hollow cathode lamp passes to the readout device. When a 
solution containing the absorbing species is introduced, a portion of the light is absorbed, decreasing 
the light intensity reaching the detector and causing a deflection. 
 
Atomic absorption spectrum: Shows a series of dark lines in a continuous band. 
 
Absorption bands: Regions in the spectrum where radiation has been absorbed by the substance. 
 
Resonance spectral lines: Stable, intense radiations that should be narrow compared to the width 
of absorption bands/lines. A calibration curve is used to determine unknown concentrations. The 
instrument is calibrated with solutions of known concentrations, measuring the absorbance of each. 
A plot of concentration versus absorbance is then created, and the unknown concentration is 
calculated from this curve. 
Relationship of AAS with UV-Visible Spectroscopy: AAS shares similarities with UV-Visible 
Spectroscopy in that both have a similar basic principle of promoting electrons from a lower energy 
level to a higher energy level, and both use similar steps to interpret results. However, they differ in the 
part of the emission spectrum they use: AAS uses the 'visible' part, while UV-Vis uses the 'ultraviolet' 
part. 
V. 
Interferences  
Factors that affect the ground state atom population or the instrument's ability to read this parameter can 
cause interference: 
 
Absorption of Source Radiation: Other elements may absorb the same wavelength as the analyte. 
 
Ionization Interference: Formation of ions rather than atoms reduces absorption. This is overcome 
 
 
Self-Absorption: Atoms of the same kind absorb more radiation at the center of the line, altering 
line shape and intensity. 
 
Background Absorption: Caused by incomplete atomization; can be reduced by increasing flame 
temperature. 
 
Physical Interference: Differences in physical properties (e.g., viscosity, surface tension) between 
sample and standard can affect results. 
 
Spectral Interference: Overlapping radiations with the characteristic lines of the test element, 
which can be overcome by selecting different spectral lines. 
 
Chemical Interference: Formation of strong, stable bonds between analyte ions and other species 
(e.g., Ca²⁺ and PO ⁻). Solutions include increasing temperature, adding releasing agents (like 
LaO ), or chelating agents (like EDTA). 
 
Background Correction: Running a blank and subtracting its readings from the samples.

--- OCR TEXT ---
Detector and Read out Device: A photomultiplier tube is typically used to convert the selected light
signal into an electrical signal proportional to the light intensity. This electrical signal is then
amplified and displayed or sent to a data station for printout.

IV. Working of AAS

In practice, the instrument is calibrated to zero absorbance with a blank (unionized water) sprayed into
the flame, and unsaturated light from the hollow cathode lamp passes to the readout device. When a
solution containing the absorbing species is introduced, a portion of the light is absorbed, decreasing
the light intensity reaching the detector and causing a deflection.

Atomic absorption spectrum: Shows a series of dark lines in a continuous band.
Absorption bands: Regions in the spectrum where radiation has been absorbed by the substance.

Resonance spectral lines: Stable, intense radiations that should be narrow compared to the width
of absorption bands/lines. A calibration curve is used to determine unknown concentrations. The
instrument is calibrated with solutions of known concentrations, measuring the absorbance of each.
A plot of concentration versus absorbance is then created, and the unknown concentration is
calculated from this curve.

Relationship of AAS with UV-Visible Spectroscopy: AAS shares similarities with UV-Visible
Spectroscopy in that both have a similar basic principle of promoting electrons from a lower energy
level to a higher energy level, and both use similar steps to interpret results. However, they differ in the

part of the emission spectrum they use: AAS uses the 'visible' part, while UV-Vis uses the 'ultraviolet'
part.

Interferences

Factors that affect the ground state atom population or the instrument's ability to read this parameter can
cause interference:

Absorption of Source Radiation: Other elements may absorb the same wavelength as the analyte.

lonization Interference: Formation of ions rather than atoms reduces absorption. This is overcome
by adding ionization suppressors like CsCl).

Self-Absorption: Atoms of the same kind absorb more radiation at the center of the line, altering
line shape and intensity.

Background Absorption: Caused by incomplete atomization; can be reduced by increasing flame
temperature.

Physical Interference: Differences in physical properties (e.g., viscosity, surface tension) between
sample and standard can affect results.

Spectral Interference: Overlapping radiations with the characteristic lines of the test element,
which can be overcome by selecting different spectral lines.

Chemical Interference: Formation of strong, stable bonds between analyte ions and other species
(e.g., Ca? *and PO,*). Solutions include increasing temperature, adding releasing agents (like
LaQ,), or chelating agents (like EDTA).

Background Correction: Running a blank and subtracting its readings from the samples.

VI. 
Advantages 
 
Provides precise and accurate results. 
 
Highly sensitive, capable of detecting concentrations as low as a few parts per million (µg/L). 
 
Very specific, as set wavelengths are strongly absorbed only by the particular metal ion. 
 
Requires only a small quantity of sample (1-2 mL). 
 
It is a non-destructive technique. 
 
Has a large linear dynamic range. 
VII. 
Disadvantages 
 
It is a cost-effective technique. 
 
Flame atomic absorption spectroscopy can only analyze liquid samples. 
 
Graphite furnace AA may have disadvantages related to sample size and precision if flame AA would 
suffice. 
 
Low sensitivity compared to some other detectors, which limits its use with WCOT columns. 
VIII. 
Applications 
AAS is widely applied in various fields, including  
 
Clinical Analysis: Detecting deficiencies or excessive amounts of metals in biological fluids (blood, 
urine). 
 
Environmental Analysis: Monitoring metal ion pollution in soil, air, and water, including levels in 
rivers, seawater, and drinking water. 
 
Pharmaceuticals: Determining trace metal catalyst quantities in final pharmaceutical products 
(e.g., in vitamin preparations). 
 
Industry: Raw material analysis, ensuring major elements are present and toxic impurities are 
below specified levels (e.g., lead in concrete). 
 
Food Industry: Trace element analysis in food and drinks to track harmful metals. 
 
Cosmetics Industry: Trace element analysis in cosmetics, such as identifying allergic metals. 
 
Petroleum Industry: Analyzing metals in engine oils (e.g., anti-knocking agent tetraethyl lead). 
 
Mining: Determining metal amounts (e.g., gold) in rocks to assess mining viability.

1.2. Atomic Emission Spectroscopy (AES)
I. Introduction

AES is an analytical technique that measures the concentrations of elements in samples by making use of
the emission of light by these elements.

Il. Principle

In AES, the sample is atomized, and the analyte atoms are excited to higher energy levels by sufficient
energy from high-temperature atomization sources. As these excited atoms decay back to lower energy

============================================================
PAGE 11
============================================================
levels, they emit light at characteristic wavelengths. This emitted radiation is then passed through 
monochromators or filters to photomultiplier tubes for detection. The analyte concentration is determined 
from the amount of emission, with Emission being proportional to Concentration. The energy change is 
given by ΔE = E  - E  = hν = hc/λ
 
III. 
Types of Emission Spectrum  
When an element is heated in a flame, the absorption of thermal energy by ground state electrons leads to 
excitation, and subsequent return to the ground state emits characteristic radiation. The collection of 
spectral lines produced by an excited atom is called an emission spectrum and is characteristic of that 
atom. There are three types: 
1. Line Emission Spectrum: Consists of sharply defined, often widely and irregularly spaced individual 
lines of a single wavelength, characteristic of an element (also called atomic spectrum). 
2. Band Emission Spectrum: Consists of groups of lines, each with a single wavelength, becoming 
closely spaced towards the end of the band (also called molecular spectrum). 
3. Continuous Emission Spectrum: Obtained when solids are heated to incandescence, characterized 
by the absence of sharp lines. 
IV. 
Instrumentation 
The instrumentation for AES is similar to AAS, but notably, it does not require a separate radiation source 
because the sample itself emits light when excited. The main components are: 
1. Atomizer: The primary energy sources for excitation include:  
o Graphite Furnace: Used when sample size is small or greater sensitivity is needed. The 
sample is dried, burned to ash, and then atomized at high temperatures (e.g., 2700°C). 
o Flame: Useful for elements with low excitation energies (e.g., sodium, potassium, calcium), 
operating at temperatures between 1700°C and 3150°C. 
o Inductively Coupled Plasma (ICP): Operates at very high temperatures (6000°C  8000°C), 
useful for elements with high excitation energies. 
o Electric Arc/Spark: Sample heated by an electric arc or excited in a high-voltage spark. 
2. Monochromator: Used to select the specific wavelength of light emitted by the sample, 
excluding other wavelengths. This allows for the determination of the selected element in the 
presence of others. Like AAS, a good monochromator is vital for detection limits. 
3. Detector and Read out Device: Similar to AAS, a photomultiplier tube converts the light signal into 
an electrical signal proportional to intensity, which is then amplified and displayed or printed. 
Calibration Curve: Similar to AAS, a calibration curve plotting concentration versus emission is used to 
determine unknown concentrations.

--- OCR TEXT ---
levels, they emit light at characteristic wavelengths. This emitted radiation is then passed through
monochromators or filters to photomultiplier tubes for detection. The analyte concentration is determined
from the amount of emission, with Emission being proportional to Concentration. The energy change is
given by 4E = E, -E, =hv=he/A, where E, is excited energy and E, is the ground state.

III. Types of Emission Spectrum

When an element is heated in a flame, the absorption of thermal energy by ground state electrons leads to

spectral lines produced by an excited atom is called an emission spectrum and is characteristic of that

1. Line Emission Spectrum: Consists of sharply defined, often widely and irregularly spaced individual
lines of a single wavelength, characteristic of an element (also called atomic spectrum).

2. Band Emission Spectrum: Consists of groups of lines, each with a single wavelength, becoming
closely spaced towards the end of the band (also called molecular spectrum).

3. Continuous Emission Spectrum: Obtained when solids are heated to incandescence, characterized
by the absence of sharp lines.

IV. Instrumentation

The instrumentation for AES is similar to AAS, but notably, it does not require a separate radiation source
because the sample itself emits light when excited. The main components are:

1. Atomizer: The primary energy sources for excitation include:

o Graphite Furnace: Used when sample size is small or greater sensitivity is needed. The
sample is dried, burned to ash, and then atomized at high temperatures (e.g., 2700°C).

o Flame: Useful for elements with low excitation energies (€.g., sodium, potassium, calcium),
operating at temperatures between 1700°C and 3150°C.

o Inductively Coupled Plasma (ICP): Operates at very high temperatures (6000°C - 8000°C),
useful for elements with high excitation energies.

o Electric Arc/Spark: Sample heated by an electric arc or excited in a high-voltage spark.

excluding other wavelengths. This allows for the determination of the selected element in the
presence of others. Like AAS, a good monochromator is vital for detection limits.

3. Detector and Read out Device: Similar to AAS, a photomultiplier tube converts the light signal into
an electrical signal proportional to intensity, which is then amplified and displayed or printed.

Calibration Curve: Similar to AAS, a calibration curve plotting concentration versus emission is used to
determine unknown concentrations.

2. Molecular Fluorescence Spectroscopy 
Definition: Molecular Fluorescence Spectroscopy (FS) is an analytical technique that measures the 
concentration of a fluorescent substance in a sample by utilizing the phenomenon of fluorescence, which is 
the emission of light by a substance after it absorbs light or electromagnetic radiation. 
Principle: This technique is based on the emission of light by molecules that have become electronically 
excited after absorbing electromagnetic radiation. It typically uses UV-visible spectra along with some part 
of the IR region of the electromagnetic spectrum. It is a highly sensitive technique, capable of detecting 
samples in the parts per million (ppm) to parts per trillion (ppt) range. 
Jablonski Diagram: The process is explained by the Jablonski Diagram: an electron in its 
either non-radiatively (losing energy as heat during interconversion) or radiatively, producing fluorescence. 
Properties of Fluorescence: 
 
The emitted light usually has a longer wavelength (lower energy) than the absorbed exciting light, 
because some energy is lost as heat. 
 
The emitted light is composed of many wavelengths, forming a fluorescence spectrum. 
 
Fluorescence is a specific property of each molecule and depends on its auxochrome.

2. Molecular Fluorescence Spectroscopy

Definition: Molecular Fluorescence Spectroscopy (FS) is an analytical technique that measures the
concentration of a fluorescent substance in a sample by utilizing the phenomenon of fluorescence, which is
the emission of light by a substance after it absorbs light or electromagnetic radiation.

Principle: This technique is based on the emission of light by molecules that have become electronically
excited after absorbing electromagnetic radiation. It typically uses UV-visible spectra along with some part
of the IR region of the electromagnetic spectrum. It is a highly sensitive technique, capable of detecting
samples in the parts per million (ppm) to parts per trillion (ppt) range.

Jablonski Diagram: The process is explained by the Jablonski Diagram: an electron in its ground state (So)
absorbs light (EMR) and gets excited to a high intermediate energy level. According to Kasha’s rule, it moves
to a stable energy level (S,). From this stable excited state, the electron then returns to its ground state (So)
either non-radiatively (losing energy as heat during interconversion) or radiatively, producing fluorescence.

Properties of Fluorescence:

e The emitted light usually has a longer wavelength (lower energy) than the absorbed exciting light,
because some energy is lost as heat.

e The emitted light is composed of many wavelengths, forming a fluorescence spectrum.
e Fluorescence is a specific property of each molecule and depends on its auxochrome.
Types of Fluorescence:

e Prompt Fluorescence: Immediate release of electromagnetic energy from the singlet state (S, > Sp
+hv).

e Delayed Fluorescence: Results from two intersystem crossings (Sp > S; > T; > S; > So + hv). This can
be E-type (thermal energy interconversion) or P-type (collision energy interconversion).

e Phospholuminescence: A delayed release of electromagnetic energy from the triplet state (S, > S, >
T, >So + Av). Its intensity is lower as the electron loses energy during intersystem crossing.

Instrumentation: A typical fluorescence spectrometer includes:

============================================================
PAGE 13
============================================================
 
Light Source: Commonly mercury-arc lamps or xenon-arc lamps, which provide a steady and 
homogeneous supply of light to energize electrons from the ground state to an excited state. 
 
Excitation Monochromator: Used to select the specific wavelength of light to fall on the sample, 
ensuring that the sample is excited at the desired energy. 
 
Sample Holder: A cuvette or quartz cell, which must be transparent from all sides to allow the 
produced fluorescence to be observed by the detector. 
 
Emission Monochromator: A second monochromator is placed at a right angle to the excitation 
monochromator. It filters and directs the emitted fluorescence towards the detector, allowing only 
the desired wavelengths to be observed. 
 
Detector: Observes the produced fluorescence and generates an electrical signal, which is then 
amplified. Common detectors include phototubes and photomultiplier tubes. 
 
Read Out Devices: A computer reads the detected signal and plots a graph (spectrum). 
Applications: While not all substances fluoresce, FS has several important applications: 
 
Qualitative and Quantitative Analysis: Used for both identifying and quantifying fluorescent 
substances. 
 
Protein Identification: Achieved by tagging a fluorescent molecule within the protein and 
observing its fluorescence. 
 
Assay of Medications: Used for drugs that exhibit fluorescence (e.g., Amphotericin B, 
Diphenhydramine, Flurazepam, Reserpine). 
 
Quality Control of Crude Drugs: Powdered crude drugs can be analyzed for fluorescent properties 
under visible and UV light for quality control. 
 
Quantitative Analysis of Specific Compounds: Such as riboflavin, chloroquine, quinine, 
adrenaline, reserpine, phenobarbitone, and retinol. 
 
Derivatization: For compounds that are not inherently fluorescent, derivatization methods (direct 
labeling or changing solvent/medium) can be employed to make them fluorescent. 
 
Quenching and Replacement Techniques: Used when derivatization is not feasible to analyze 
fluorescent inactive compounds or separate multiple solutes. 
3. Flame Photometry 
Introduction: Flame photometry, or more accurately, Flame Atomic Emission Spectrometry, is an analytical 
technique developed in the 1980s. It measures the emission of radiation by neutral atoms after a sample 
is introduced into a flame. A photoelectric flame photometer specifically determines the concentration of 
certain metal ions, including sodium, potassium, calcium, and lithium. 
I. 
Principle 
The technique is based on the principle that "Matter absorbs light at the same wavelength at which it emits 
light". When a metal salt solution is introduced into a hot flame, the atoms absorb thermal energy, causing 
their orbital electrons to become unstable at higher energy levels. As these unstable excited atoms return to 
their ground state, they release energy as photons of a particular wavelength, producing a characteristic 
bright line emission. The intensity of the emitted light is directly proportional to the concentration of

--- OCR TEXT ---
e Light Source: Commonly mercury-arc lamps or xenon-arc lamps, which provide a steady and
homogeneous supply of light to energize electrons from the ground state to an excited state.

e Excitation Monochromator: Used to select the specific wavelength of light to fall on the sample,
ensuring that the sample is excited at the desired energy.

e¢ Sample Holder: A cuvette or quartz cell, which must be transparent from all sides to allow the
produced fluorescence to be observed by the detector.

e Emission Monochromator: A second monochromator is placed at a right angle to the excitation
monochromator. It filters and directs the emitted fluorescence towards the detector, allowing only
the desired wavelengths to be observed.

e Detector: Observes the produced fluorescence and generates an electrical signal, which is then
amplified. Common detectors include phototubes and photomultiplier tubes.

e Read Out Devices: A computer reads the detected signal and plots a graph (spectrum).
Applications: While not all substances fluoresce, FS has several important applications:

¢ Qualitative and Quantitative Analysis: Used for both identifying and quantifying fluorescent
substances.

e Protein Identification: Achieved by tagging a fluorescent molecule within the protein and
observing its fluorescence.

e Assay of Medications: Used for drugs that exhibit fluorescence (e.g., Amphotericin B,
Diphenhydramine, Flurazepam, Reserpine).

¢ Quality Control of Crude Drugs: Powdered crude drugs can be analyzed for fluorescent properties
under visible and UV light for quality control.

¢ Quantitative Analysis of Specific Compounds: Such as riboflavin, chloroquine, quinine,
adrenaline, reserpine, phenobarbitone, and retinol.

e Derivatization: For compounds that are not inherently fluorescent, derivatization methods (direct
labeling or changing solvent/medium) can be employed to make them fluorescent.

¢ Quenching and Replacement Techniques: Used when derivatization is not feasible to analyze
fluorescent inactive compounds or separate multiple solutes.

3. Flame Photometry

Introduction: Flame photometry, or more accurately, Flame Atomic Emission Spectrometry, is an analytical
technique developed in the 1980s. It measures the emission of radiation by neutral atoms after a sample
is introduced into a flame. A photoelectric flame photometer specifically determines the concentration of
certain metal ions, including sodium, potassium, calcium, and lithium.

I. Principle

The technique is based on the principle that "Matter absorbs light at the same wavelength at which it emits
light". When a metal salt solution is introduced into a hot flame, the atoms absorb thermal energy, causing
their orbital electrons to become unstable at higher energy levels. As these unstable excited atoms return to
their ground state, they release energy as photons of a particular wavelength, producing a characteristic
bright line emission. The intensity of the emitted light is directly proportional to the concentration of

============================================================
PAGE 14
============================================================
the element in the sample under constant and controlled conditions. The specific wavelength (color) 
indicates the element qualitatively, while the color's intensity indicates its quantity. 
II. 
Working  
The process involves: 
1. Preparing standard and sample solutions in distilled water. 
2. Calibrating the photometer's flame by adjusting air and gas, allowing it to stabilize. 
3. Inserting appropriate color filters and adjusting the galvanometer reading to zero using distilled 
water. 
4. Adjusting sensitivity with the most concentrated standard solution. 
5. Spraying each standard and then the sample solution into the flame, recording galvanometer 
readings (typically three times). 
6. Calculating the mean of the readings and plotting a graph of concentration versus galvanometer 
reading to determine the unknown concentration. 
III. 
Instrumentation 
Key components of a flame photometer include: 
While specific configurations vary, common components for flame-based spectroscopic methods include: 
 
Burner System:  
o Burner/Flame: Provides the high temperature needed for atomization and excitation. The 
flame consists of various zones: preheating, primary reaction, interconal (where 
measurements are taken due to maximum temperature), and secondary reaction zones.  
 
Fuel and Oxidants: If the temperature is too high, elements may convert into ions 
instead of neutral atoms. If too low, atoms may not reach an excited state.  The 
flame temperature is crucial, as it influences atom distribution and can be adjusted 
by altering the oxidant and fuel ratio.  These are required to produce the flame and 
convert the sample into neutral atoms, which then get excited by heat energy. 
Combinations (e.g., acetylene and nitrous oxide) are chosen to achieve the desired 
stable flame temperature. 
 
Burner Types: Pre-mixed burners (mix sample, fuel, oxidant before burning, for 
uniform intensity) and total consumption burners (extract sample by high pressure of 
fuel/oxidant, consume entire sample). 
o Flame Structure: The flame can be divided into zones:  
 
Preheating Zones: Combustion mixture is heated to ignition temperature. 
 
Primary Reaction Zone (Inner Zone): About 0.1 mm thick, with no thermodynamic 
equilibrium and high concentrations of ions and free radicals. This region is not 
typically used for flame photometry. 
 
Interconal Zone: Can extend considerably, achieving maximum temperature just 
above the inner zone. This zone is used for flame photometry.

--- OCR TEXT ---
the element in the sample under constant and controlled conditions. The specific wavelength (color)
indicates the element qualitatively, while the color's intensity indicates its quantity.

Il. Working
The process involves:
1. Preparing standard and sample solutions in distilled water.
2. Calibrating the photometer's flame by adjusting air and gas, allowing it to stabilize.

3. Inserting appropriate color filters and adjusting the galvanometer reading to zero using distilled
water.

4. Adjusting sensitivity with the most concentrated standard solution.

5. Spraying each standard and then the sample solution into the flame, recording galvanometer
readings (typically three times).

6. Calculating the mean of the readings and plotting a graph of concentration versus galvanometer
reading to determine the unknown concentration.

While specific configurations vary, common components for flame-based spectroscopic methods include:
e Burner System:

o Burner/Flame: Provides the high temperature needed for atomization and excitation. The
flame consists of various zones: preheating, primary reaction, interconal (where
measurements are taken due to maximum temperature), and secondary reaction zones.

= Fuel and Oxidants: If the temperature is too high, elements may convert into ions
instead of neutral atoms. If too low, atoms may not reach an excited state. The
flame temperature is crucial, as it influences atom distribution and can be adjusted
by altering the oxidant and fuel ratio. These are required to produce the flame and
convert the sample into neutral atoms, which then get excited by heat energy.
Combinations (e.g., acetylene and nitrous oxide) are chosen to achieve the desired
stable flame temperature.

= Burner Types: Pre-mixed burners (mix sample, fuel, oxidant before burning, for
uniform intensity) and total consumption burners (extract sample by high pressure of
fuel/oxidant, consume entire sample).

o Flame Structure: The flame can be divided into zones:

« Preheating Zones: Combustion mixture is heated to ignition temperature.

» Primary Reaction Zone (Inner Zone): About 0.1 mm thick, with no thermodynamic
equilibrium and high concentrations of ions and free radicals. This region is not
typically used for flame photometry.

«  Interconal Zone: Can extend considerably, achieving maximum temperature just
above the inner zone. This zone is used for flame photometry.

============================================================
PAGE 15
============================================================
 
Secondary Reaction Zone: Combustion products burn to stable molecular species 
with surrounding air. 
 
Sample Delivery System: Includes a nebulizer which converts the liquid sample into a fine mist of 
droplets, and an aerosol modifier which removes larger droplets, allowing only smaller ones to reach 
the flame. 
 
Monochromators: Filters or monochromators are used to isolate the specific wavelength of light 
emitted by the analyte from the rest of the flame's light. In flame photometry, simple filters are often 
sufficient for studying elements like Ca, Na, K, and Li, with a filter wheel used to select the appropriate 
filter for the element being analyzed. 
 
Detectors: Similar to spectrophotometry, operating in the visible region (400-700nm). Photovoltaic 
cells, phototubes, or photomultiplier tubes are used to convert the emitted radiation into an electrical 
signal. This electrical signal is then processed by a signal amplifier and displayed for readout or fed into 
a data station for printout.  
o A calibration curve is typically generated by measuring the absorbance or emission of 
standards with known concentrations to determine the unknown concentration of an element in 
a sample. 
 
Recorder and Display: Modern instruments often use microprocessor-controlled electronics to display 
and print data, minimizing operator errors. 
IV. 
Interferences 
Various factors can affect the accuracy of flame spectroscopic measurements: 
 
Spectral Interferences:  
o Occur when other elements or background flame emissions overlap with the characteristic 
radiation of the test element. This can be overcome by selecting different spectral lines. 
o Background absorption of source radiation by incomplete atomization particles can also 
occur, which can be mitigated by increasing flame temperature. 
 
Chemical Interferences:  
o Analyte atoms may form strong bonds with other elements, creating stable molecular 
species that do not readily atomize (e.g., Ca+2 and Mg+2 with PO4-3 forming Ca3 (PO4)2 and 
Mg3 (PO4)2). 
o These can be overcome by increasing the flame temperature to promote bond 
dissociation, or by adding releasing agents (e.g., LaO2) or chelating agents (e.g., EDTA). 
o Cation-cation interference can also occur, where one cation interferes with the emission of 
another (e.g., aluminum with calcium and magnesium). 
 
Ionization Interference:  
o Occurs when the formation of ions rather than neutral atoms lowers the absorption or 
emission signal. 
o This is often problematic for elements that ionize easily at low temperatures (e.g., Na+, K+).

--- OCR TEXT ---
« Secondary Reaction Zone: Combustion products burn to stable molecular species
with surrounding air.

e Sample Delivery System: Includes a nebulizer which converts the liquid sample into a fine mist of
droplets, and an aerosol modifier which removes larger droplets, allowing only smaller ones to reach
the flame.

e Monochromators: Filters or monochromators are used to isolate the specific wavelength of light
emitted by the analyte from the rest of the flame's light. In flame photometry, simple filters are often
sufficient for studying elements like Ca, Na, K, and Li, with a filter wheel used to select the appropriate
filter for the element being analyzed.

e Detectors: Similar to spectrophotometry, operating in the visible region (400-700nm). Photovoltaic
cells, phototubes, or photomultiplier tubes are used to convert the emitted radiation into an electrical
signal. This electrical signal is then processed by a signal amplifier and displayed for readout or fed into
a data station for printout.

o Acalibration curve is typically generated by measuring the absorbance or emission of
standards with known concentrations to determine the unknown concentration of an element in
asample.

e Recorder and Display: Modern instruments often use microprocessor-controlled electronics to display
and print data, minimizing operator errors.

IV. Interferences
Various factors can affect the accuracy of flame spectroscopic measurements:
¢ Spectral Interferences:

o Occur when other elements or background flame emissions overlap with the characteristic
radiation of the test element. This can be overcome by selecting different spectral lines.

o Background absorption of source radiation by incomplete atomization particles can also
occur, which can be mitigated by increasing flame temperature.

e« Chemical Interferences:

o Analyte atoms may form strong bonds with other elements, creating stable molecular
species that do not readily atomize (e.g., Ca+2 and Mg+2 with PO4-3 forming Ca3 (PO4)2 and
Mg3 (PO04)2).

o These can be overcome by increasing the flame temperature to promote bond
dissociation, or by adding releasing agents (e.g., LaO2) or chelating agents (e.g., EDTA).

o Cation-cation interference can also occur, where one cation interferes with the emission of
another (e.g., aluminum with calcium and magnesium).

e lonization Interference:

o Occurs when the formation of ions rather than neutral atoms lowers the absorption or
emission signal.

o This is often problematic for elements that ionize easily at low temperatures (e.g., Na+, K+).

============================================================
PAGE 16
============================================================
o Adding ionization suppressors or non-ionizing agents (e.g., CsCl2) can overcome this 
problem. 
 
Physical Interference:  
o Physical properties of the sample, such as viscosity, surface tension, vapor pressure, and 
density, should be similar to those of the standards used for calibration to ensure consistent 
nebulization and transport into the flame. 
 
Self-Absorption:  
o Atoms of the same kind that are absorbing radiation will absorb more at the center of the 
spectral line than at the wings, changing the shape and intensity of the line. 
 
Background Correction:  
o To compensate for interferences, a blank is run in the instrument and its reading is 
subtracted from the sample readings. 
V. 
Advantages 
Flame photometry offers several benefits: 
 
Precise and Accurate Results: Can yield highly accurate and precise data. 
 
High Sensitivity: Capable of detecting very low concentrations, sometimes as small as parts per 
million (µg/L) to parts per billion (ppb). 
 
Specificity: Generally very specific, as the set wavelength is strongly absorbed/emitted by the 
particular metal ion being analyzed, with minimal interference from other components. 
 
Small Sample Volume: Only a small quantity of sample (e.g., 1-2 mL) is required. 
 
Economical: Flame photometry, in particular, is noted for being a simple and economical method of 
analysis. 
 
Quick and Convenient: Provides quick, convenient analysis. 
 
Qualitative and Quantitative: Can be used for both qualitative (identifying elements) and 
quantitative (determining concentration) analysis 
VI. 
Limitations 
 
Limited number of elements can be analyzed, primarily alkali and alkaline earth metals due to the 
low temperature of the natural gas and air flame. 
 
Sample must be in solution form, making analysis of insoluble compounds difficult. 
 
Small sample amounts may be wasted due to volatilization. 
 
Impurity mixing with solvents can lead to errors. 
 
Cannot detect inert gases, carbon, hydrogen, or halides due to their non-radiating nature. 
 
Results can be susceptible to interference and instability of flame/aspiration conditions. 
VII. 
Applications 
Flame spectroscopy techniques are widely applied across various fields:

problem.

VII. Applications

Flame spectroscopy techniques are widely applied across various fields:

============================================================
PAGE 17
============================================================
 
Clinical Analysis: Used to detect deficiencies or excessive amounts of certain metals in biological 
fluids like blood and urine. 
 
Environmental Analysis: Essential for monitoring metal ion pollutants in soil, air, and water (e.g., 
rivers, seawater, drinking water, petrol). 
 
Pharmaceuticals: Determines trace quantities of catalysts (often metals) in final pharmaceutical 
products, such as in vitamin preparations. Also used for quality control of pharmaceutical reagents. 
 
Industry: Used for raw material analysis (e.g., checking major elements and toxic impurities in 
concrete). 
 
Food Industry: Trace element analysis in food and drinks to track harmful metals. 
 
Cosmetics Industry: Trace element analysis in cosmetics, for example, to analyze specific allergic 
metals. 
 
Petroleum Industry: Analyzes metals in engine oils, such as anti-knocking agent tetraethyl lead 
(TEL). 
 
Mining: Determines metal content (e.g., gold in rocks) to assess mining viability. 
 
General Element Estimation: Routinely used for the determination of sodium, potassium, calcium, 
and lithium levels in various samples. 
4. IR Spectroscopy 
IR Spectroscopy is an analytical technique used to study the absorption of different infrared (IR) 
frequencies when a sample is placed in the path of an IR beam. This method helps to identify functional 
groups within molecules and is often referred to as "fingerprinting" a molecule. 
Here's a detailed guide to IR Spectroscopy: 
I. 
Principle 
IR Spectroscopy is based on the fact that molecules of a chemical substance exhibit selective 
absorption in the IR region of the electromagnetic spectrum. When absorbed, this radiation causes 
the bonds within the molecules to stretch or bend or vibrate at various rates. The specific 
wavelength of radiation absorbed is characteristic of the bond absorbing it. Absorption in the IR 
region leads to changes in the rotational and vibrational energy levels of molecules. After 
absorbing IR radiation, molecules form the absorption spectrum. The technique focuses on the 
region of the electromagnetic spectrum specifically a frequency range of 500 cm⁻¹ to 670 cm⁻¹ (IR 
Region).  
In IR spectroscopy, the changes in the vibrational energy depends upon 
a. Mass of atoms present in a molecule. 
b. Strength of bonds 
c. Arrangement of atoms within the molecules 
II. 
Regions of IR 
The IR spectrum is generally divided into three main regions: 
 
Very Near IR: 0.8 µm to 2.5 µm.

--- OCR TEXT ---
¢ Clinical Analysis: Used to detect deficiencies or excessive amounts of certain metals in biological
fluids like blood and urine.

e Environmental Analysis: Essential for monitoring metal ion pollutants in soil, air, and water (e.g.,
rivers, seawater, drinking water, petrol).

e« Pharmaceuticals: Determines trace quantities of catalysts (often metals) in final pharmaceutical
products, such as in vitamin preparations. Also used for quality control of pharmaceutical reagents.

e Industry: Used for raw material analysis (e.g., checking major elements and toxic impurities in
concrete).

e Food Industry: Trace element analysis in food and drinks to track harmful metals.

¢ Cosmetics Industry: Trace element analysis in cosmetics, for example, to analyze specific allergic
metals.

e Petroleum Industry: Analyzes metals in engine oils, such as anti-knocking agent tetraethyl lead
(TEL).

e Mining: Determines metal content (e.g., gold in rocks) to assess mining viability.

e General Element Estimation: Routinely used for the determination of sodium, potassium, calcium,
and lithium levels in various samples.

IR Spectroscopy

IR Spectroscopy is an analytical technique used to study the absorption of different infrared (IR)
frequencies when a sample is placed in the path of an IR beam. This method helps to identify functional
groups within molecules and is often referred to as "fingerprinting" a molecule.

Here's a detailed guide to IR Spectroscopy:

I. Principle

IR Spectroscopy is based on the fact that molecules of a chemical substance exhibit selective
absorption in the IR region of the electromagnetic spectrum. When absorbed, this radiation causes
the bonds within the molecules to stretch or bend or vibrate at various rates. The specific
wavelength of radiation absorbed is characteristic of the bond absorbing it. Absorption in the IR
region leads to changes in the rotational and vibrational energy levels of molecules. After
absorbing IR radiation, molecules form the absorption spectrum. The technique focuses on the
region of the electromagnetic spectrum specifically a frequency range of 500 cm 7 to 670. cm 7 (IR
Region).

In IR spectroscopy, the changes in the vibrational energy depends upon
a. Mass of atoms present ina molecule.
b. Strength of bonds
c. Arrangement of atoms within the molecules
II. Regions of IR
The IR spectrum is generally divided into three main regions:

e Very Near IR: 0.8 um to 2.5 um.

============================================================
PAGE 18
============================================================
 
Near IR region: 2.5 µm to 15 µm. 
 
Far IR: 75 µm to 200 µm.  
III. 
Interpretation of IR Spectrum: 
The spectrum is further often divided into two primary parts for interpretation: 
 
Functional Group Region: This region spans from 4000 cm⁻¹ to 1600 cm⁻¹. It is often the first 
region examined because most compounds exhibit strong, characteristic stretching vibrations 
from their functional groups.  
I. 
Examples: C-H, O-H, and C-N bonds absorb in the 3700-2500 cm⁻¹ range due to stretching 
vibrations. Triple bonds (C≡C, C≡N) show absorption between 2300-2100 cm⁻¹. Double bonds 
(C=C, C=O) absorb in the 1900-1600 cm⁻¹ range. 
II. 
It is crucial to confirm functional groups by checking other regions of the spectrum as well. 
For example, an aliphatic acetate absorbs at 1740 cm⁻¹ (C=O stretch) but also at 1240 cm⁻¹ 
(C-O stretch). Aromatic rings have characteristic bands between 1600 cm⁻¹ and 1450 cm⁻¹, 
but also show absorption bands in the 900-700 cm⁻¹ range (out-of-plane bending 
vibrations). 
 
Fingerprint Region: This region covers the range from 1600 cm⁻¹ to 625 cm⁻¹. It provides a 
unique set of absorption bands for each compound, acting as a "fingerprint" for identification 
purposes. The structural information is derived from the presence or absence of characteristic 
absorption bands of various functional groups. 
IV. 
Types of Absorption Bands 
Three main types of absorption band occur in IR spectra: 
1. Fundamental bands 
2. Overtone bands  
3. Combinational bands 
 Fundamental band: Transition from grounded state to first excited state by absorbing light 
strongly in the IR region, give rise to intense band known as fundamental band. These bands in 
the IR spectra are due to various stretching and bending vibrations. 
 Overtone (multiples of given frequency): Overtone occur when a vibrational mode is excited 
from ground state v=0 to higher energy state v=2, this is known as first overtone. 
o 
(Fundamental) 
o 
(First Overtone) 
o 
(Second Overtone)

(Third Overtone) 
o 
(Fourth Overtone)   
 Combinational: Combinational bands are observed when two or more fundamental vibration 
are excited simultaneously. 
V. 
Modes of Vibration 
There are two main types of molecular vibrations that absorb IR radiation:

--- OCR TEXT ---
e Near IR region: 2.5 um to 15 um.
e Far IR: 75 um to 200 um.
Ill. Interpretation of IR Spectrum:

The spectrum is further often divided into two primary parts for interpretation:

e Functional Group Region: This region spans from 4000 cm * to 1600 cm +. It is often the first
region examined because most compounds exhibit strong, characteristic stretching vibrations
from their functional groups.

|. | Examples: C-H, O-H, and C-N bonds absorb in the 3700-2500 cm 7 range due to stretching
vibrations. Triple bonds (C=C, C=N) show absorption between 2300-2100 cm 7. Double bonds
(C=C, C=O) absorb in the 1900-1600 cm 7 range.

ll. It is crucial to confirm functional groups by checking other regions of the spectrum as well.
For example, an aliphatic acetate absorbs at 1740 cm + (C=O stretch) but also at 1240 cm 7
(C-O stretch). Aromatic rings have characteristic bands between 1600 cm + and 1450cm 7,
but also show absorption bands in the 900-700 cm * range (out-of-plane bending
vibrations).

e Fingerprint Region: This region covers the range from 1600 cm * to 625 cm 7. It provides a
unique set of absorption bands for each compound, acting as a "fingerprint" for identification
purposes. The structural information is derived from the presence or absence of characteristic
absorption bands of various functional groups.

IV. Types of Absorption Bands
Three main types of absorption band occur in IR spectra:
1. Fundamental bands
2. Overtone bands
3. Combinational bands

e Fundamental band: Transition from grounded state to first excited state by absorbing light
strongly in the IR region, give rise to intense band known as fundamental band. These bands in
the IR spectra are due to various stretching and bending vibrations.

e Overtone (multiples of given frequency): Overtone occur when a vibrational mode is excited
from ground state v=0 to higher energy state v=2, this is known as first overtone.

o V0 V1 (Fundamental)

o V0-+V2 (First Overtone)

o V0 V3 (Second Overtone)
o V0 V4 (Third Overtone)

o V0-+V5 (Fourth Overtone)

e Combinational: Combinational bands are observed when two or more fundamental vibration
are excited simultaneously.

V. Modes of Vibration

There are two main types of molecular vibrations that absorb IR radiation:

============================================================
PAGE 19
============================================================
a. Stretching Vibration 
In this mode, the distance between two atoms increases or decreases along the bond axis, but the 
atoms remain on the same bond axis. 
 
Symmetric Stretching: The movement of atoms with respect to a particular central atom in the 
same molecule is in the same direction. 
 
Asymmetric Stretching: One atom approaches the central atom while the other moves away 
from it. 
b. Bending Vibration 
In this mode, the position of atoms changes with respect to the original bond axis. Stretching 
absorptions typically appear at higher frequencies (higher energy) compared to bending 
absorptions. There are four types of bending vibrations: 
 
Scissoring: Two atoms joined to a central atom move towards and away from each other, 
similar to a scissor. 
 
Rocking: Two atoms joined to a central atom move back and forth in the plane of the molecule 
in the same direction. 
 
Wagging: The structural unit moves back and forth out of the plane of the molecule (e.g., two 
atoms moving up and below the plane relative to the central atom). 
 
Twisting: The structural unit rotates around the bond connecting it to the rest of the molecule 
(e.g., one atom moves up the plane while the other moves down the plane relative to the central 
atom). 
VI. 
Factors affecting Vibrational Frequency: 
1) Symmetry: Symmetrical compounds does not possess dipole moment and are optically 
inactive. e.g. symmetrical acetylene 
2) Fermi resonance: When a fundamental vibration coupled with overtone or combinational band 
it result in a different vibration, this coupled vibration is known as Fermi resonance. 
 The phenomenon can be observed whenever two fundamental or a fundamental and 
overtone bands have nearly the same energy. 
 As a result, two strong bands are observed in the spectrum, instead of expected strong and 
weak bands. 
 In the figure, the bands in the top represents two fundamental vibrations without fermi 
resonance, whereas the bands in the bottom represent two fundamental bands with fermi 
resonance. 
 The fermi resonance result in the increased in energy of first band and decrease in the 
energy of second band thus known as "Fermi Doublet". 
3) Hydrogen Bonding: 
Hydrogen bonding bring about remarkable energy shift. It changes the shape and position of IR 
band. 
Stronger the hydrogen bonding, greater the absorption shift. 
a. Intermolecular - broad band

4) Coupling: There are so many factors which cause coupled vibration in IR spectrum and it will 
influence the intensity and shape of absorption bands. 
 Normally the band due to C-C bond is around at 1650cm-1 but due to mechanical coupling of 
two C-C systems in allenes it give two bands at 1960cm-1 and 1970cm-1. 
5) Electronic effect: Electronic effect such as inductive, mesomeric and field effect cause shift in 
the absorption band due to changes in the absorption frequency. E.g. inductive - acetone 
(1715cm-1) and chloroacetone (1725cm-1), mesomeric - acetophenone (1693cm-1) and p-amino 
acetophenone (1677cm-1). 
6) Bond angle: Difference in the bond angle also leads to changes in the absorption bands. 
VII. 
Instrumentation of IR Spectrophotometer 
An IR spectrophotometer typically consists of four main components: 
1. IR Source: Provides continuous infrared radiation.  
o Nernst Glower
1500 2200 K. At the end of the cylinder platinum wires are attached and a current is 
passed through the cylinder. It is fragile and requires preheating to be conductive. 
o Globar Source: A silicon carbide rod electrically heated to about 1500 K. It requires water 
cooling for electrical contacts and has a larger output at shorter wavelengths compared 
to the Nernst Glower. It is less intense then Nernst glower. 
o Carbon Dioxide Laser: A tunable laser used for monitoring atmospheric pollutants and 
determining absorbing species in aqueous solutions. 
o Mercury Arc: Used for the far IR region (< 200 cm⁻¹), emitting an intense continuum in 
this range. 
o Incandescent lamp: An incandescent light bulb or lamp is an electric light which 
produces light with a wire filament heated to a high temperature by an electric current 
passing through it, until it glow. The hot filament is protected from oxidation with a glass 
or quartz bulb that is filled with inert gas. 
2. Monochromator: Separates polychromatic radiation from the source into desired wavelengths.  
o Prism: Often made of salt like NaCl, which is transparent over the region from 4000 cm⁻¹ 
 650 cm⁻¹. Metal halide prisms (e.g., KBr, LiF) are also used. They are susceptible to 
mechanical, thermal instability, and water solubility. 
o Grating: A series of parallel lines ruled on materials like glass or quartz. Gratings 
generally offer better resolution at high frequencies than prisms and are not affected by 
moisture. 
3. Detector: Measures the intensity of transmitted infrared radiations.  
o Thermal Detectors: When IR radiation fall on these detectors, they cause heating which 
give rise to potential difference. This potential difference is depend upon amount of 
radiation.

spectrophotometry. It consists of two pieces of dissimilar metal fused together at

VII. Instrumentation of IR Spectrophotometer

An IR spectrophotometer typically consists of four main components:
1. IR Source: Provides continuous infrared radiation.

o Nernst Glower: A cylinder made of rare earth oxides (ZrO , Y.0;) electrically heated to
1500-2200 K. At the end of the cylinder platinum wires are attached and a current is
passed through the cylinder. It is fragile and requires preheating to be conductive.

o Globar Source: A silicon carbide rod electrically heated to about 1500 K. It requires water
cooling for electrical contacts and has a larger output at shorter wavelengths compared
to the Nernst Glower. It is less intense then Nernst glower.

o Carbon Dioxide Laser: A tunable laser used for monitoring atmospheric pollutants and
determining absorbing species in aqueous solutions.

o Mercury Arc: Used for the far IR region (< 200 cm *), emitting an intense continuum in
this range.

o Incandescent lamp: An incandescent light bulb or lamp is an electric light which
produces light with a wire filament heated to a high temperature by an electric current
passing through it, until it glow. The hot filament is protected from oxidation with a glass
or quartz bulb that is filled with inert gas.

2. Monochromator: Separates polychromatic radiation from the source into desired wavelengths.

o Prism: Often made of salt like NaCl, which is transparent over the region from 4000 cm
-650cm +. Metal halide prisms (e.g., KBr, LiF) are also used. They are susceptible to
mechanical, thermal instability, and water solubility.

o Grating: A series of parallel lines ruled on materials like glass or quartz. Gratings
generally offer better resolution at high frequencies than prisms and are not affected by
moisture.

3. Detector: Measures the intensity of transmitted infrared radiations.

o Thermal Detectors: When IR radiation fall on these detectors, they cause heating which
give rise to potential difference. This potential difference is depend upon amount of
radiation.

= Thermocouples: It is the most widely used detector used in IR
spectrophotometry. It consists of two pieces of dissimilar metal fused together at

============================================================
PAGE 21
============================================================
the end. When heated, these two pieces heat at different temperature, this 
difference in temperature create a potential difference, which is directly 
proportional to the degree of heating. 
 
Bolometer: Bolometer is made in one arm of the Wheatstone bridge and a similar 
strip of metal is used as balancing arm of the bridge. When no IR radiation fall on 
the bolometer, the bridge remains balanced. When IR radiation fall on the 
bolometer, the bridge becomes unbalanced due to the changes in the electrical 
resistance and thus the electric current flows through the galvanometer. Gives an 
electrical signal from the change in resistance of a metallic conductor with 
temperature. 
 
Golay Cells: Utilize the expansion of gas upon IR absorption to deform a 
diaphragm, which alters a light signal on a photocell. 
 
Pyroelectric Detectors: It consists of pyroelectric (ceramic, lithium, tentalate) 
material which get polarized in the presence of electric field. The degree of 
polarization depends upon the temperature of crystal. It is fast responding, 
sensitive and used as FTIR detector. 
o Photoelectric Detectors: (e.g., Mercury cadmium telluride detector) consist of a 
semiconducting film where IR absorption promotes electrons to a conducting state, 
decreasing electrical resistance and generating current/voltage. They have better 
response than pyroelectric detectors and are used in FT-IR. 
Mode of Operation (Working) 
The IR instrument operates by: 
 Splitting the light from the IR source [A] into a sample beam [B] and a reference beam. 
 Reflecting these two beams onto a rotating segmented mirror called a Chopper [C], which 
then directs them to the monochromator grating [D]. 
 The grating slowly rotates, transmitting individual frequencies to the detector (e.g., 
thermopile [E]), which converts IR energy into electrical energy. 
 If the sample absorbs a specific frequency, the detector receives an alternating signal 
(intense reference beam, weaker sample beam), generating an AC current [F]. 
 This imbalanced signal triggers a servomotor [G], which moves an optical wedge [H] into 
the reference beam until equal intensity is restored at the detector. 
 The movement of the wedge (attenuator) is coupled to an ink pen recorder [J], which 
produces the absorption bands (signals) on a printed chart. 
4. Recorder: Displays the absorption spectrum. 
5. Determination of IR Spectrum (Sample Preparation)

or gas): 
Handling samples in Infrared (IR) spectroscopy depends on the physical state of the sample, 
whether it is a gas, liquid, or solid. 
Here is a detailed, structured guide on sample handling:

(intense reference beam, weaker sample beam), generating an AC current [F].

the reference beam until equal intensity is restored at the detector.

e The movement of the wedge (attenuator) is coupled to an ink pen recorder [J], which
produces the absorption bands (signals) on a printed chart.

5. Determination of IR Spectrum (Sample Preparation)

Methods for preparing samples for IR analysis depend on the sample's physical state (solid, liquid,
or gas):

Handling samples in Infrared (IR) spectroscopy depends on the physical state of the sample,
whether it is a gas, liquid, or solid.

Here is a detailed, structured guide on sample handling:

============================================================
PAGE 22
============================================================
I. Gases 
 
Gas Cell: Gases are typically handled using a gas cell. 
o These cells are equipped with infrared transparent windows that allow them to be 
mounted directly into the instrument. 
 
Internal Mirrors: Internal mirrors are used to reflect the IR beam multiple times through the 
sample, which helps to increase sensitivity. 
 
Vapor Phase Considerations: In the vapor phase, rotational changes in molecules can occur 
freely, and low-frequency processes can modulate the higher energy vibrational bands. 
 
Gas analysis using this method is not commonly used due to a lack of sensitivity. 
II. Liquids 
 
Thin Films: Liquid samples are usually prepared as thin films. 
 
This involves squeezing the sampling liquid between two infrared transparent windows, such as 
NaCl flats. 
 
The salt plates or rock salt flats used must be optically polished and cleaned immediately 
after use. 
 
Solvents like Toluene and Chloroform are used to clean these plates. 
 
Handling Precaution: They should be handled only by their edges and kept dry. 
III. Solids 
Solid samples can be prepared using a few different techniques: 
a) KBr Disks 
 
Preparation: KBr disks are prepared by grinding the sample with KBr (100 mg of dried KBr for 
1mg of sample) using an agate mortar and pestle or commercial ball mills. A particle size of 
2µm must be achieved to avoid scattering. Poorly ground mixtures can result in disks that 
scatter light rather than transmit it, affecting the spectrum. 
o Carefully place the sample mixture into the processing chamber of a mold (dye), 
ensuring it is held between the polished surfaces of the bottom and top processing dyes. 
o Attach the chamber to a vacuum line and activate the vacuum pump. 
o Initially, apply a slight negative pressure to compact the powder, then gradually 
increase the pressure to less than 15mm Hg for 30 seconds. Finally, enhance the pressing 
force to 10,000 pounds/inch² for 1-2 minutes. 
o Release the pressure, remove the dyes, and place the resulting disc onto the sample 
holder for analysis. 
 
Moisture Prevention: KBr must be kept dry, and grinding should be carried out under an 
infrared lamp to avoid condensation of atmospheric moisture, which can lead to broad 
absorptions in the spectrum. 
 
Special Dies: Special dies are used, from which air can be evacuated by hydraulic compression 
(up to 10 tonnes load). 
 
Ease of Handling: Disks formed this way are easy to handle.

--- OCR TEXT ---
I. Gases

Gas Cell: Gases are typically handled using a gas cell.

o These cells are equipped with infrared transparent windows that allow them to be
mounted directly into the instrument.

Internal Mirrors: Internal mirrors are used to reflect the IR beam multiple times through the
sample, which helps to increase sensitivity.

Vapor Phase Considerations: In the vapor phase, rotational changes in molecules can occur
freely, and low-frequency processes can modulate the higher energy vibrational bands.

Gas analysis using this method is not commonly used due to a lack of sensitivity.

. Liquids

Thin Films: Liquid samples are usually prepared as thin films.

This involves squeezing the sampling liquid between two infrared transparent windows, such as
NaCl flats.

The salt plates or rock salt flats used must be optically polished and cleaned immediately
after use.

Solvents like Toluene and Chloroform are used to clean these plates.

Handling Precaution: They should be handled only by their edges and kept dry.

Material: Sample cells (holders) for solids must be made of IR transparent material, such as KBr

b) Mulls 
 
Preparation: Mulls are pastes prepared by grinding the sample with a drop of oil. The mull is

this is never perfectly true.

 
Components: This method uses NaCl plates as transparent windows.

solvent onto NaCl plates and allowing the solvent to evaporate, leaving a thin film on the flat 
surface. 


providing excellent spectra.

signal. 
6. Strengths of IR Spectroscopy 
 
Provides a complex fingerprint unique to the compound, making identification reliable when 
matched against standards. 
 
Computer control allows for ready matching of spectra to standard fingerprints. 
 
Can characterize samples in solid and semi-solid states (e.g., creams, tablets). 
 
Useful as a fingerprint test for films, coatings, and packaging plastics. 
 
Can detect polymorphs of drugs, which is important for manufacturing and bioavailability. 
 
Used as a preliminary check for compound identity in synthetic chemistry, especially for 
carbonyl groups. 
7. Limitations of IR Spectroscopy 
 
Rarely used as a quantitative technique due to difficulties in sample preparation and complex 
spectra. 
 
Generally can only detect gross impurities in samples. 
 
Sample preparation requires skill, particularly for KBr discs. 
 
Lacks robustness, as sample handling can affect the spectrum.

8. Applications of IR Spectrophotometer

IR Spectroscopy has diverse applications in pharmaceutical and chemical analysis:

============================================================
PAGE 24
============================================================
 
Qualitative Analysis: Identifies functional groups and aromatic rings in organic compounds 
and drugs (e.g., benzoic acid, paracetamol). Used for identity checks of raw materials and 
synthetic products (e.g., presence/absence of carbonyl groups). 
 
Material Characterization: Can characterize solid and semi-solid samples (creams, tablets) 
and act as a fingerprint test for films, coatings, and packaging plastics. 
 
Polymorph Detection: Can detect different crystal forms of drugs, which is important for 
manufacturing and bioavailability. 
 
Quantitative Analysis: Can determine the percentage purity of drugs by comparing IR spectra 
with standard drugs. 
 
Hydrogen Bonding and Structure Elucidation: Used to determine hydrogen bonding in organic 
molecules and study the relative proportion of saturated and unsaturated rings in 
hydrocarbons. 
 
Tautomerism Studies: Helps in studying tautomeric equilibria (e.g., keto-enol) by identifying 
characteristic frequencies of groups like C=O, O-H, N-
 
 
Chromatographic Separation Monitoring: Spectra of selected fractions can monitor 
chromatographic separation processes. 
 
Identification of Groups and Bonds: Identifies various functional groups and bonds based on 
their specific frequency ranges. 
9. Fourier Transform Infrared Spectrometers (FTIR) 
FTIR is the preferred method for infrared spectroscopy, developed to overcome the slow scanning 
limitations of dispersive instruments. 
 
Principle: In FTIR, IR radiation passes through a sample, and the measured signal is an 
interferogram (a plot of intensity vs. time). A Fourier transform is then performed on this data 
to convert it into a spectrum identical to that from conventional IR spectroscopy. This process 
involves an interferometer, which divides the beam from the source into two halves, reflects 
them off fixed and movable mirrors, and recombines them to produce interference patterns. 
 
Advantages over Dispersive IR:  
o More energy enters the sample because dispersion elements are not necessary. 
o Faster spectrum recording (< 1s). 
o High resolution (up to 0.01 cm⁻¹). 
o Improved frequency resolution and reproducibility. 
o Higher energy throughput and faster operation. 
o Computer-based, allowing for spectrum storage and processing. 
o Easily adapted for remote use (e.g., GC-FT-IR). 
5. Mass Spectroscopy (MS) 
Mass Spectrometry (MS) is a powerful analytical technique primarily used for structural determination 
(elucidation) and quantifying metals and metalloids. Unlike other spectroscopic methods, MS does not 
involve the absorption or emission of electromagnetic radiation (EMR); instead, it focuses on the 
breakdown of molecules into different fragments.

5. Mass Spectroscopy (MS)

Mass Spectrometry (MS) is a powerful analytical technique primarily used for structural determination
(elucidation) and quantifying metals and metalloids. Unlike other spectroscopic methods, MS does not
involve the absorption or emission of electromagnetic radiation (EMR); instead, it focuses on the
breakdown of molecules into different fragments.

============================================================
PAGE 25
============================================================
I. 
Basic Theory and Principle 
The fundamental principle involves bombarding organic molecules with electrons to convert them into 
highly energetic positively charged ions, known as molecular ions or parent ions. These molecular ions 
can then break up into smaller fragment ions or daughter ions. These fragment ions are subsequently 
separated by deflection in a magnetic field based on their mass and charge, ultimately yielding a mass 
spectrum. 
The basic principle dictates that if a large molecule is present, too many fragments will be formed. This is 
why MS is often used in combination with other techniques like Gas Chromatography (GC), High-
Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC), Liquid Chromatography (LC), and Thin Layer 
Chromatography (TLC), which first isolate compounds before MS performs fragmentation and 
identification. 
II. 
Mass Spectrum 
The mass spectrum is a graphical representation where the relative abundance of ions is plotted against 
their mass-to-charge ratio (m/e). It is used for the detection of isotopes based on their mass; ions with a 
high mass-to-charge ratio correspond to heavier isotopes, and vice versa. 
 
The highest peak is recorded as 100% and is called the base peak, representing the highest 
quantity of a fragment. 
 
Other peaks are recorded relative to this base peak. 
 
The mass of the substance is inversely proportional to the distance in the spectrum. 
 
It's important to note that if too many compounds are introduced to MS, they may mix, making it 
difficult to identify the original parent compound. 
Instrumentation 
A mass spectrometer is composed of several key components that work in concert: 
1. Sample Inlet System 
2. Ionization Source (or Ionisation Chamber) 
3. The Electrostatic Accelerating System 
4. The Magnetic Field 
5. The Ion Separator (Mass Analyzer) 
6. The Ion Collector (Detector) 
7. The Vacuum System 
1. Sample Inlet System 
The type of inlet system used depends on the nature of the sample: 
 
Solid samples with low vapor pressure are directly inserted into the ionization chamber, with 
volatilization controlled by heating the probe. 
 
Liquids are handled via hypodermic needle injection through a silicon rubber dam.
