1. Sample Inlet System
The type of inlet system used depends on the nature of the sample:

¢ Solid samples with low vapor pressure are directly inserted into the ionization chamber, with
volatilization controlled by heating the probe.

e Liquids are handled via hypodermic needle injection through a silicon rubber dam.

============================================================
PAGE 26
============================================================
 
Gases and samples with high pressure are leaked directly into the ionization chamber with the 
help of a mercury manometer. 
2. Ionization Source 
The ionization source is responsible for the production of ions. Ionization of the sample occurs in the 
ionization chamber. Molecular ions begin to form when the electron beam energy reaches 10-15 eV, 
while fragmentation typically occurs at higher bombardment energies, around 70 eV. Ionization 
methods are broadly categorized into two types: 
 
Gas Phase Ionization (for gases and vapors): Samples are volatilized outside the ion source. 
o Electron Impact (EI) Ionization: This is the most common method. An electrically heated 
filament (tungsten or rhenium) serves as the source of an electron beam. Electrons are 
drawn towards an anode plate (electron trap) and travel across the chamber, guided by 
collimating magnets. The sample, volatilized in a separate chamber to pressures of 10⁻   
10⁻  torr, enters the ionization chamber where the electron beam bombards it, removing 
electrons to create radical cations. The produced ions then move towards the mass 
analyzer. 
o Chemical Ionization (CI): A reagent gas (e.g., methane, ammonia, nitric acid) is introduced 
into a high-pressure source (0.1  1.0 torr) and ionized by electron bombardment. A small 
amount of the sample in vapor phase then reacts with these reagent ions through gas-phase 
processes, such as proton transfer, to produce sample ions. 
o Field Ionization (FI): This method uses very strong electric fields (typically 10-20 kV) to 
produce ions from gas-phase molecules. It is particularly suited for the analysis of synthetic 
or man-made polymers. 
 
Desorption Technique (for liquids and solids): Samples are ionized directly inside the ion source. 
Energy is introduced into the solid or liquid samples, causing the direct formation of gaseous ions. 
This technique is suitable for non-volatile substances. 
o Field Desorption (FD): An electrode coated with a solution of the sample is mounted on a 
probe and inserted into the sample compartment. Ionization occurs upon the application of 
a high potential. 
o Fast Atom Bombardment (FAB): This method is used for large biological molecules that 
are difficult to get into the gas phase. FAB typically causes little fragmentation and usually 
yields a large molecular ion. The sample is dissolved in a viscous liquid, often glycerol, and 
then bombarded by energetic (fast-moving) xenon or argon atoms. These energetic atoms 
are generated by accelerating xenon or argon ions from an ion source (gun) at 10⁻  torr, 
which then pick up an electron in collisions with neutral atoms to form a high-energy atomic 
beam. 
o Laser Desorption (LD): This technique involves the interaction of a laser beam with sample 
particles, leading to both ionization and vaporization. 
o Matrix Assisted Laser Desorption Ionization (MALDI): This technique is based on 
bombarding sample molecules with laser light for ionization. The sample is pre-mixed with a 
highly absorbing matrix compound, such as nicotinic acid, to optimize the energy at the

--- OCR TEXT ---
e Gases and samples with high pressure are leaked directly into the ionization chamber with the
help of a mercury manometer.

highly absorbing matrix compound, such as nicotinic acid, to optimize the energy at the

============================================================
sample/matrix surface when the laser beam is fired. This process causes larger molecules to 
ionize, allowing them to be extracted through the mass spectrometer. MALDI is a widely used 
ionization method for protein identification. 
3. Mass Analyzer 
The mass analyzer is often referred to as the "Heart" of the mass spectrometer. Its primary function is to 
take ionized masses and separate them based on their charge-to-mass ratios (m/e) using magnetic and 
electric fields. It is also known as an ion separator. The two main functions of a mass analyzer are: 
 
To resolve the ion beam into its component ions. 
 
To maximize the resolved ions intensities. The mass resolving power (R) measures the analyzer's 
ability to separate ions with different mass-to-charge ratios. 
Here are the types of mass analyzers: 
 
Magnetic Sector Mass Analyzer: These analyzers rely on the effect of a magnetic field on moving 
ions to separate them. The ionic beam is directed between the poles of an electromagnet, which 
causes the beam to assume a curved path. This curved path separates the ions according to their 
m/e ratio.  
o Single Focusing Mass Analyzer: This type consists of a horse-shoe shaped glass tube, an 
inlet, an electron bombardment source, accelerated plates at one end, and a collector slit at 
the other. Ions are passed between the poles of a magnet. An ion with charge "e" repelled by 
an ionic source with voltage "V" gains potential energy (eV), which is converted into kinetic 
energy (½ mv²). Under the influence of the magnetic field, ions follow a curved path with 
radius 'r', experiencing a centrifugal force (mv²/r) that equals the centripetal force (eHv) at 
equilibrium. This leads to the relationship: m/e ∝ r², meaning the mass-to-charge ratio 
depends on the magnetic field strength (H), voltage (V), and radius of curvature (r). Single 
focusing analyzers have lower mass ranges. 
o Double Focusing Mass Analyzer: This analyzer uses a combination of magnetic and 
electric fields to focus and sort ions. It provides both directional and velocity focusing, 
differentiating ions based on their kinetic energy before they enter the magnetic field. These 
analyzers offer higher mass ranges compared to single focusing mass spectrometers. 
 
Time of Flight (TOF) Analyzer: This method measures the time an ion takes to travel from the ion 
source to the detector. A burst of electrons produces ions between the first and second electrodes. 
An extraction potential (E) is applied briefly to focus the ions, followed by an accelerating potential 
(V) applied for an even shorter period to simultaneously accelerate all ions. The accelerated ions 
then travel through a field-free drift tube (about one meter long) before being collected by a sensing 
electrode. 
o Benefits: Fastest MS analyzer, well-suited for pulsed ionization (e.g., MALDI), high ion 
transmission, highest practical mass range. 
o Limitations: Requires pulsed ionization, fast digitizers can have limited dynamic range. 
o Applications: Most MALDI systems, very fast GC/MS systems. 
o Typical flight times are 1-30 µs. TOF analyzers generally have lower resolution power (less 
than 1000) and lower mass ranges.

--- OCR TEXT ---
sample/matrix surface when the laser beam is fired. This process causes larger molecules to
ionize, allowing them to be extracted through the mass spectrometer. MALDI is a widely used
ionization method for protein identification.

3. Mass Analyzer

The mass analyzer is often referred to as the "Heart" of the mass spectrometer. Its primary function is to
take ionized masses and separate them based on their charge-to-mass ratios (m/e) using magnetic and
electric fields. It is also known as anion separator. The two main functions of a mass analyzer are:

To resolve the ion beam into its component ions.

To maximize the resolved ions intensities. The mass resolving power (R) measures the analyzer's
ability to separate ions with different mass-to-charge ratios.

Here are the types of mass analyzers:

Magnetic Sector Mass Analyzer: These analyzers rely on the effect of a magnetic field on moving
ions to separate them. The ionic beam is directed between the poles of an electromagnet, which
causes the beam to assume a curved path. This curved path separates the ions according to their
m/e ratio.

fe}

Single Focusing Mass Analyzer: This type consists of a horse-shoe shaped glass tube, an
inlet, an electron bombardment source, accelerated plates at one end, and a collector slit at
the other. lons are passed between the poles of a magnet. An ion with charge "e" repelled by
an ionic source with voltage "V" gains potential energy (eV), which is converted into kinetic
energy (2 mv2). Under the influence of the magnetic field, ions follow a curved path with
radius 'r', experiencing a centrifugal force (mv2/r) that equals the centripetal force (eHv) at
equilibrium. This leads to the relationship: m/e or?, meaning the mass-to-charge ratio
depends on the magnetic field strength (H), voltage (V), and radius of curvature (r). Single
focusing analyzers have lower mass ranges.

Double Focusing Mass Analyzer: This analyzer uses a combination of magnetic and
electric fields to focus and sort ions. It provides both directional and velocity focusing,
differentiating ions based on their kinetic energy before they enter the magnetic field. These
analyzers offer higher mass ranges compared to single focusing mass spectrometers.

Time of Flight (TOF) Analyzer: This method measures the time an ion takes to travel from the ion
source to the detector. A burst of electrons produces ions between the first and second electrodes.
An extraction potential (E) is applied briefly to focus the ions, followed by an accelerating potential
(V) applied for an even shorter period to simultaneously accelerate all ions. The accelerated ions
then travel through a field-free drift tube (about one meter long) before being collected by a sensing
electrode.

fe}

Benefits: Fastest MS analyzer, well-suited for pulsed ionization (e.g., MALDI), high ion
transmission, highest practical mass range.

Limitations: Requires pulsed ionization, fast digitizers can have limited dynamic range.
Applications: Most MALDI systems, very fast GC/MS systems.

Typical flight times are 1-30 us. TOF analyzers generally have lower resolution power (less
than 1000) and lower mass ranges.

============================================================
PAGE 28
============================================================
 
Quadrupole Mass Analyzer: These analyzers are constructed from four rods with a hyperbolic or 
circular cross-section. Opposite pairs of rods are charged either positively or negatively, with both 
DC and RF (radiofrequency) potentials applied. Accelerated ions entering the analyzer oscillate in a 
complex manner based on their m/e ratio and the applied RF/DC ratio. Only ions with a specific m/e 
value can pass through to the collector, while others strike the poles and are discharged. By varying 
the RF/DC ratio continuously, the entire mass spectrum can be recorded.  
o Benefits: Classical mass spectra, good reproducibility, relatively small and low-cost. 
o Limitations: Limited resolution, peak heights can vary, not well-suited for pulsed ionization. 
o Applications: Majority of benchtop GC/MS and LC/MS systems, triple quadrupole MS/MS 
systems. 
 
Ion Trap Mass Analyzer: Ions from an electron impact or chemical ionization source enter a 
specially designed chamber. Ions with an appropriate m/e value circulate in an orbit. As the 
radiofrequency voltage increases, the orbit of heavier ions becomes stabilized, while lighter ions 
become destabilized or trapped and move towards the detector.  
o Types: Three-dimensional quadrupole ion traps and ion cyclotron resonance mass 
spectrometers (FTICR). 
o Benefits (FTICR): Highest recorded mass resolution, powerful for ion chemistry and MS/MS, 
well-suited for pulsed ionization. 
o Benefits (Quadrupole Ion Traps): High sensitivity, multi-stage mass spectrometry, 
compact. 
o Limitations: Can lead to artifacts ("non-classical mass spectra"), poor quantitation for 
FTICR, limited dynamic range for ion traps, strict low-pressure requirements. 
o Applications: Ion chemistry, high-resolution MALDI/electrospray for high-mass analytes 
(FTICR); benchtop GC/MS, LC/MS, target compound screening (ion traps). 
 
Ion Cyclotron Resonances: In a magnetic field, ions move in circular orbits that are characteristic of 
their m/z values. If energy is supplied at a frequency equal to their precession frequency, and in a 
direction perpendicular to their plane of precession, the ions absorb this energy, enabling their 
detection. 
4. Detector 
The detector is an instrument that detects the ions coming from the analyzer. Common types include: 
 
Faraday Cup Detector: This is a metallic cup maintained at a potential to capture ions. Incident 
ions striking the dynode surface emit electrons, inducing a current that is then amplified and 
recorded. 
 
Photographic Plates Detector: Photographic plates coated with silver bromide emulsion are 
sensitive to energetic ions. Ions falling on these plates cause an impression, and this detector is well-
suited for a wide range of m/e values. 
 
Electron Multiplier Detector: This detector consists of a series of electrodes called dynodes. When 
an ion strikes the first dynode, it releases a large number of electrons. These electrons then hit the 
second dynode, releasing even more electrons, and this process continues through approximately

--- OCR TEXT ---
Quadrupole Mass Analyzer: These analyzers are constructed from four rods with a hyperbolic or
circular cross-section. Opposite pairs of rods are charged either positively or negatively, with both
DC and PF (radiofrequency) potentials applied. Accelerated ions entering the analyzer oscillate ina
complex manner based on their m/e ratio and the applied RF/DC ratio. Only ions with a specific m/e
value can pass through to the collector, while others strike the poles and are discharged. By varying
the RF/DC ratio continuously, the entire mass spectrum can be recorded.

o Benefits: Classical mass spectra, good reproducibility, relatively small and low-cost.
o Limitations: Limited resolution, peak heights can vary, not well-suited for pulsed ionization.

o Applications: Majority of benchtop GC/MS and LC/MS systems, triple quadrupole MS/MS
systems.

lon Trap Mass Analyzer: lons from an electron impact or chemical ionization source enter a
specially designed chamber. lons with an appropriate m/e value circulate in an orbit. As the
radiofrequency voltage increases, the orbit of heavier ions becomes stabilized, while lighter ions
become destabilized or trapped and move towards the detector.

o Types: Three-dimensional quadrupole ion traps and ion cyclotron resonance mass
spectrometers (FTICR).

o Benefits (FTICR): Highest recorded mass resolution, powerful for ion chemistry and MS/MS,
well-suited for pulsed ionization.

o Benefits (Quadrupole Ion Traps): High sensitivity, multi-stage mass spectrometry,
compact.

o Limitations: Can lead to artifacts ("non-classical mass spectra"), poor quantitation for
FTICR, limited dynamic range for ion traps, strict low-pressure requirements.

o Applications: lon chemistry, high-resolution MALDI/electrospray for high-mass analytes
(FTICR); benchtop GC/MS, LC/MS, target compound screening (ion traps).

lon Cyclotron Resonances: In a magnetic field, ions move in circular orbits that are characteristic of
their m/z values. If energy is supplied at a frequency equal to their precession frequency, andina
direction perpendicular to their plane of precession, the ions absorb this energy, enabling their
detection.

 
Scintillation Type Detector: Here, the dynode consists of a substance (scintillators) that emits

Recorder: Modern systems typically use an online computer (data system) to record and print the mass

Strengths and Limitations of MS 
Strengths: 
 
Rapid identification of trace impurities, especially when coupled with chromatographic 
separation and high-resolution MS. 
 
High sensitivity and selectivity for monitoring drugs and their metabolites in biological fluids. 
 
The re-emergence of time-of-flight MS and electrospray mass spectrometry makes it highly useful 
for quality control of therapeutic antibodies and peptides. 
Limitations: 
 
Currently not routinely used in quality control (QC), but rather in research and development (R&D) 
for specific problem-solving. 
 
Expensive instrumentation requiring highly trained personnel and regular maintenance, although 
open-access instruments are becoming more common. 
Applications of Mass Spectrometry 
Mass spectrometry has extensive applications, both qualitative and quantitative: 
Qualitative Applications 
1. Determination of molecular weight: It is considered the best technique for determining or 
confirming the molecular weight of compounds that can be easily volatilized. 
2. Determination of molecular formula: This requires identifying the molecular ion peak and its exact 
mass. 
3. Determination of structure of compounds: MS helps determine structure by analyzing the 
fragmentation pattern of ions. By breaking the molecule into smaller charged fragments and 
separating them by m/z ratio, the resulting mass spectrum provides structural information. 
Quantitative Applications 
1. Determination of isotope abundance: Despite small differences in isotope masses, MS can 
determine the isotopic composition of molecules. This information is valuable for:  
o Tracer studies with isotopes. 
o Determination of atomic weights of compounds. 
o Determination of the age of rocks and minerals. 
o Studies of the origin and nature of the solar system.

Applications of Mass Spectrometry
Mass spectrometry has extensive applications, both qualitative and quantitative:
Qualitative Applications

1. Determination of molecular weight: It is considered the best technique for determining or
confirming the molecular weight of compounds that can be easily volatilized.

2. Determination of molecular formula: This requires identifying the molecular ion peak and its exact
mass.

3. Determination of structure of compounds: MS helps determine structure by analyzing the
fragmentation pattern of ions. By breaking the molecule into smaller charged fragments and
separating them by m/z ratio, the resulting mass spectrum provides structural information.

Quantitative Applications

1. Determination of isotope abundance: Despite small differences in isotope masses, MS can
determine the isotopic composition of molecules. This information is valuable for:

o Tracer studies with isotopes.
o Determination of atomic weights of compounds.
o Determination of the age of rocks and minerals.

o Studies of the origin and nature of the solar system.

============================================================
PAGE 30
============================================================
2. Determination of isotope ratio: MS is used to determine isotope ratios, which helps ascertain the 
concentration of individual components in complex mixtures. 
3. Differentiation between Cis and Trans isomers: While both isomers yield similar spectra, they can 
be differentiated by the intensity of their molecular ion peaks; the trans isomer's molecular ion 
peak is more intense than that of the cis isomer. 
4. Mass spectrometry in thermodynamics:  
o Determination of heat of vaporization: Data from the spectrum of high-temperature 
compounds is collected, and a graph plotting ion intensities (Y-axis) against temperature (X-
axis) is used. 
o Determination of heat of sublimation: Vapors of sublimed solids are passed into the 
ionization chamber, and the recorded spectrum's peak intensities are directly proportional 
to the sample's vapor pressure (VP). 
5. Measurement of ionization potential: This refers to the minimum energy required by bombarding 
electrons to produce molecular ions from a molecule. 
6. Determination of ion-molecule reactions: MS is used to study reactions between fragment ions 
and unionized molecules. 
7. Detection of impurity: Impurities, even in low concentrations (parts per million), can be detected by 
spectrometry, provided their molecular weights differ significantly from the major components. 
8. Identification of unknown compounds: This is achieved by recording the spectrum of the unknown 
compound and comparing it with the spectrum of a standard compound. 
9. Identification of proteins: MS is a valuable tool in the study of protein structure and function, 
especially the analysis of proteins digested by proteases like trypsin (proteomics). Electro spray 
ionization (ESI) and Matrix-Assisted Laser Desorption Ionization (MALDI) are commonly used 
ionization methods for this purpose. 
6. NMR Spectroscopy 
Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy is a powerful analytical technique that exploits the 
magnetic properties of certain atomic nuclei to study the physical, chemical, and biological properties of 
matter. It is particularly effective for characterizing organic molecules by identifying their carbon-hydrogen 
frameworks. 
I. 
Principle of NMR Spectroscopy 
NMR spectroscopy is based on the absorption of electromagnetic radiation in the radiofrequency region by 
atomic nuclei with non-zero spin (e.g., 1H, 13C, 15N, 31P) placed in the presence of a magnetic field. This 
absorption causes a change in the orientation of the spinning nucleus within the applied magnetic field.  
II. 
Types of NMR 
Two common types of NMR spectroscopy are used to characterize organic structures: 
 
1H NMR: Used to determine the type and number of hydrogen atoms in a molecule. 
 
13C NMR: Used to determine the type of carbon atoms in the molecule.

peak is more intense than that of the cis isomer.

6. NMR Spectroscopy

Nuclear Magnetic Resonance (NMR) spectroscopy is a powerful analytical technique that exploits the
magnetic properties of certain atomic nuclei to study the physical, chemical, and biological properties of
matter. It is particularly effective for characterizing organic molecules by identifying their carbon-hydrogen
frameworks.

I. Principle of NMR Spectroscopy

NMR spectroscopy is based on the absorption of electromagnetic radiation in the radiofrequency region by
atomic nuclei with non-zero spin (e.g., 1H, 13C, isN, 31P) placed in the presence of a magnetic field. This
absorption causes a change in the orientation of the spinning nucleus within the applied magnetic field.

Il. Types ofNMR

Two common types of NMR spectroscopy are used to characterize organic structures:
e¢ 1HNMR: Used to determine the type and number of hydrogen atoms in a molecule.

¢ 13C NMR: Used to determine the type of carbon atoms in the molecule.

============================================================
PAGE 31
============================================================
III. 
Basic Theory 
1. Spinning Nucleus: When a charged particle, such as a proton, spins around its axis, it generates a 
magnetic field, causing the nucleus to behave like a tiny bar magnet. 
2. Behavior under Applied Magnetic Field (B0): Normally, these tiny nuclear magnets are randomly 
oriented. However, in the presence of an external magnetic field (B0), they align either with (α-spin 
state) or against (β-spin state) this field. The α-spin state is a lower energy state, making it more 
populated. The energy difference between these two states is very small (less than 0.1 cal). 
3. Spin Flipping & Resonance: The transition of a proton from the lower energy α-spin state to the 
higher energy β-spin state occurs by the absorption of radiofrequency electromagnetic radiation. 
This phenomenon is referred to as "spin flipping". When radiofrequency radiation matches the 
energy difference between α and β states (known as the Larmor frequency), the nucleus absorbs 
energy and transitions to the higher-energy state. This resonance condition is detected as an NMR 
signal. 
 
Nuclear Spin: The nuclear spin quantum number (I) determines the number of possible spin 
states, given by 2I + 1. For example:  
o Nuclei with even numbers of protons and neutrons (e.g., 12C, 16O) have I = 0 and produce 
no NMR signal. 
o Nuclei with an odd number of protons or neutrons (e.g., 1H, 13C) have half-integer spins (I 
= 1/2, 3/2, etc.) and are NMR-active. 
o Nuclei with odd numbers of both protons and neutrons (e.g., 2H, 14N) have integer spins (I 
= 1, 2, etc.) and are also NMR-active. 
 
Field and Frequency Relationship: NMR signals can be generated using two methods:  
o Field Sweep: The radiofrequency (ν) is kept constant, and the magnetic field (B0) is 
varied. 
o Frequency Sweep: The magnetic field is constant, and the radiofrequency is varied. 
4. NMR Signals: NMR signals are characterized by two variables:  
o Applied magnetic field (B0), measured in tesla (T). 
o Frequency of radiation (ν) used for resonance, measured in hertz (Hz) or megahertz (MHz). 
These variables are proportionally related (ν ∝ B0), meaning a stronger magnetic field leads 
to a larger energy difference and thus a higher frequency needed for resonance. NMR signals 
can be observed by keeping one variable constant and changing the other (field sweep or 
frequency sweep). 
o Note: Only nuclei with odd mass numbers (e.g., 1H, 13C, 19F, 31P) or odd atomic numbers 
(e.g., 1H, 7N) give rise to NMR signals. 
IV. 
NMR Spectrum 
An NMR spectrum is a graphical representation plotting the intensity of a peak against its chemical shift, 
measured in parts per million (ppm). 
 
NMR absorptions typically appear as sharp peaks.

--- OCR TEXT ---
III. Basic Theory

1. Spinning Nucleus: When a charged particle, such as a proton, spins around its axis, it generates a
magnetic field, causing the nucleus to behave like a tiny bar magnet.

2. Behavior under Applied Magnetic Field (BO): Normally, these tiny nuclear magnets are randomly
oriented. However, in the presence of an external magnetic field (BO), they align either with (a-spin
state) or against (6-spin state) this field. The a-spin state is a lower energy state, making it more
populated. The energy difference between these two states is very small (less than 0.1 cal).

3. Spin Flipping & Resonance: The transition of a proton from the lower energy a-spin state to the
higher energy 6-spin state occurs by the absorption of radiofrequency electromagnetic radiation.
This phenomenon is referred to as "spin flipping". When radiofrequency radiation matches the
energy difference between a and 6 states (known as the Larmor frequency), the nucleus absorbs
energy and transitions to the higher-energy state. This resonance condition is detected as an NMR
signal.

e Nuclear Spin: The nuclear spin quantum number (I) determines the number of possible spin
states, given by 21 + 1. For example:

o Nuclei with even numbers of protons and neutrons (e.g., 120, 160) have | = 0 and produce
no NMR signal.

o Nuclei with an odd number of protons or neutrons (e.g., 1H, :3C) have half-integer spins (I
= 1/2, 3/2, etc.) and are NMR-active.

o Nuclei with odd numbers of both protons and neutrons (e.g., 2H, :4N) have integer spins (I
= 1,2, etc.) and are also NMR-active.

e Field and Frequency Relationship: NMR signals can be generated using two methods:

o Field Sweep: The radiofrequency (v) is kept constant, and the magnetic field (Bo) is
varied.

o Frequency Sweep: The magnetic field is constant, and the radiofrequency is varied.
4. NMR Signals: NMR signals are characterized by two variables:
o Applied magnetic field (BO), measured in tesla (T).

o Frequency of radiation (v) used for resonance, measured in hertz (Hz) or megahertz (MHz).
These variables are proportionally related (v «BO0), meaning a stronger magnetic field leads
to alarger energy difference and thus a higher frequency needed for resonance. NMR signals
can be observed by keeping one variable constant and changing the other (field sweep or
frequency sweep).

o Note: Only nuclei with odd mass numbers (e.g., 1H, 13C, 19F, 31P) or odd atomic numbers
(e.g., 1H, 7N) give rise to NMR signals.
IV. NMR Spectrum

An NMR spectrum is a graphical representation plotting the intensity of a peak against its chemical shift,
measured in parts per million (ppm).

e« NMRabsorptions typically appear as sharp peaks.

============================================================
PAGE 32
============================================================
 
The chemical shift increases from right to left, with "upfield" referring to the right (lower chemical 
shift) and "downfield" referring to the left (higher chemical shift). 
 
NMR absorptions are measured relative to a reference peak at 0 ppm on the δ-scale, usually from 
tetramethyl silane (TMS). 
The spectrum provides critical structural information through: 
 
Number of Signals: Indicates the number of distinct proton or carbon environments in a molecule. 
 
Position of Signals: Reflects the chemical environment (e.g., aromatic, aliphatic, primary, 
secondary, or tertiary protons) based on chemical shift. 
 
Signal Intensity: Proportional to the number of nuclei contributing to the signal. 
V. 
Features of 1H NMR Spectrum 
The 1H NMR spectrum provides critical information about a compound's structure through: 
1. Number of Protons: 
o The number of NMR signals corresponds to the number of different types of protons in a 
compound. 
o Equivalent protons give the same NMR signal, while protons in different environments give 
distinct signals. For cycloalkanes and alkenes, all bonds to hydrogen should be drawn to 
determine equivalency, especially noting cis and 
 relationships. 
2. Positions of Protons (Chemical Shift): 
Definition: The difference in resonance frequency of a nucleus relative to TMS, expressed in ppm. It 
is independent of the spectrometer frequency, making it a universal scale. 
The chemical shift refers to the shift in the absorption position of an NMR signal. This shift arises due 
to the shielding or deshielding of protons by electrons. 
o Chemical Shift to the Right (Upfield / Shielding): When a nucleus is surrounded by an 
electron cloud, the applied magnetic field (B0) induces the electrons to circulate, creating 
their own magnetic field that opposes B0. This reduces the effective magnetic field 
experienced by the nucleus (Beffective = B0 - Binduced). The nucleus is said to be shielded, a 
phenomenon called diamagnetic shielding. Shielded nuclei require a lower frequency to 
achieve resonance, shifting their absorption upfield (to the right), towards a lower chemical 
shift. 
o Chemical Shift to the Left (Downfield / Deshielding): Conversely, if a nucleus is less 
shielded, it experiences more of the effective magnetic field. Such a deshielded nucleus 
requires a higher frequency for resonance, shifting its absorption downfield (to the left), 
towards a higher chemical shift. Protons near electronegative atoms are typically 
deshielded and absorb downfield. 
The effective field experienced by different nuclei can vary due to this shielding effect, leading to 
slightly different resonance energies. 
Factors Affecting Chemical Shift:

--- OCR TEXT ---
The chemical shift increases from right to left, with "upfield" referring to the right (lower chemical
shift) and "downfield" referring to the left (higher chemical shift).

NMR absorptions are measured relative to a reference peak at 0 ppm on the 6-scale, usually from
tetramethyl silane (TMS).

The spectrum provides critical structural information through:

Number of Signals: Indicates the number of distinct proton or carbon environments in a molecule.

Position of Signals: Reflects the chemical environment (e.g., aromatic, aliphatic, primary,
secondary, or tertiary protons) based on chemical shift.

V. Features of 1H NMR Spectrum

The 1H NMR spectrum provides critical information about a compound's structure through:

1.

Number of Protons:

o Thenumber of NMR signals corresponds to the number of different types of protons ina
compound.

o Equivalent protons give the same NMR signal, while protons in different environments give
distinct signals. For cycloalkanes and alkenes, all bonds to hydrogen should be drawn to
determine equivalency, especially noting cis and Tran’s relationships.

2. Positions of Protons (Chemical Shift):

Definition: The difference in resonance frequency of a nucleus relative to TMS, expressed in ppm. It
is independent of the spectrometer frequency, making it a universal scale.

The chemical shift refers to the shift in the absorption position of an NMR signal. This shift arises due
to the shielding or deshielding of protons by electrons.

o Chemical Shift to the Right (Upfield / Shielding): When a nucleus is surrounded by an
electron cloud, the applied magnetic field (BO) induces the electrons to circulate, creating
their own magnetic field that opposes BO. This reduces the effective magnetic field
experienced by the nucleus (Beffective = BO - Binduced). The nucleus is said to be shielded, a
phenomenon called diamagnetic shielding. Shielded nuclei require a lower frequency to
achieve resonance, shifting their absorption upfield (to the right), towards a lower chemical
shift.

o Chemical Shift to the Left (Downfield / Deshielding): Conversely, if a nucleus is less
shielded, it experiences more of the effective magnetic field. Such a deshielded nucleus
requires a higher frequency for resonance, shifting its absorption downfield (to the left),
towards a higher chemical shift. Protons near electronegative atoms are typically
deshielded and absorb downfield.

The effective field experienced by different nuclei can vary due to this shielding effect, leading to
slightly different resonance energies.

Factors Affecting Chemical Shift:

============================================================
PAGE 33
============================================================
Several factors can influence the extent of shielding or deshielding, thereby affecting the chemical 
shift: 
 
Electronegativity: As the electronegativity of nearby atoms increases, electrons are drawn 
away from the proton, decreasing shielding and causing a deshielded (downfield) shift. 
 
Hydrogen Bonding: An increase in hydrogen bonding generally leads to increased deshielding 
and a downfield shift. 
 
Solvent Effect: The choice of solvent can dramatically alter chemical shifts. Aromatic solvents 
(like benzene) often cause deshielding, while acidic solvents also cause deshielding. Non-
aromatic solvents tend to cause shielding. 
 
Magnetic Anisotropic Effect: This effect relates to the geometry of the molecule. A proton's 
orientation rela
can lead to additional shielding or deshielding. For example, in benzene, the induced magnetic 
field reinforces the applied field for protons, causing deshielding and a downfield shift. In other 
cases, it can oppose the field, causing shielding and an upfield shift. 
 
Addition of Lanthanide Shift Reagents: These reagents form complexes with the sample, 
altering the chemical shift of protons and are used to simplify complex NMR spectra. 
 
Van der Waals Deshielding: Steric hindrance in complex molecules can reduce electron density, 
causing deshielding. 
Calculation of Chemical Shift: 
The chemical shift is measured relative to a reference peak and is expressed in parts per million 
(ppm). The formula is: 
Chemical Shift (ppm) = [ν(sample) - ν(TMS)] / ν  
Where: 
 
ν(sample) = Frequency of the sample (in Hertz, Hz) 
 
ν(TMS) = Frequency of the Tetramethylsilane (TMS) reference (in Hertz, Hz) 
 
ν  = Frequency of the instrument (in MegaHertz, MHz) 
The use of ppm allows chemical shifts to be independent of the strength of the applied magnetic 
field. Most protons typically absorb between 0-10 ppm. 
Reference Standard for NMR 
To accurately determine absorption positions, a standard reference is used. NMR absorptions are 
measured relative to a reference peak at 0 ppm on the delta scale. 
 
Tetramethyl silane (TMS): A volatile, inert compound with a low boiling point (27°C), giving a 
single peak upfield, commonly used for organic compounds. 
 
Dimethyl silapentane sulfonic acid (DSS): More often used in protein experiments in water. 
Properties of a good standard: Chemically inert, miscible with most organic compounds, low 
melting point, and assumed to be free of shielding effect. 
Why Tetramethylsilane (TMS) is used as a Reference:

--- OCR TEXT ---
Several factors can influence the extent of shielding or deshielding, thereby affecting the chemical
shift:

e Electronegativity: As the electronegativity of nearby atoms increases, electrons are drawn
away from the proton, decreasing shielding and causing a deshielded (downfield) shift.

e Hydrogen Bonding: An increase in hydrogen bonding generally leads to increased deshielding
and a downfield shift.

¢ Solvent Effect: The choice of solvent can dramatically alter chemical shifts. Aromatic solvents
(like benzene) often cause deshielding, while acidic solvents also cause deshielding. Non-
aromatic solvents tend to cause shielding.

e¢ Magnetic Anisotropic Effect: This effect relates to the geometry of the molecule. A proton's
orientation relative to an induced magnetic field (caused by circulating electrons in m systems)
can lead to additional shielding or deshielding. For example, in benzene, the induced magnetic
field reinforces the applied field for protons, causing deshielding and a downfield shift. In other
cases, it can oppose the field, causing shielding and an upfield shift.

e Addition of Lanthanide Shift Reagents: These reagents form complexes with the sample,
altering the chemical shift of protons and are used to simplify complex NMR spectra.

e Van der Waals Deshielding: Steric hindrance in complex molecules can reduce electron density,
causing deshielding.

Calculation of Chemical Shift:

The chemical shift is measured relative to a reference peak and is expressed in parts per million
(ppm). The formula is:

Chemical Shift (ppm) = [v(sample) - v(TMS)] / vp
Where:
e v(sample) = Frequency of the sample (in Hertz, Hz)
e v(TMS) = Frequency of the Tetramethylsilane (TMS) reference (in Hertz, Hz)
¢ Vp =Frequency of the instrument (in MegaHertz, MHz)

The use of ppm allows chemical shifts to be independent of the strength of the applied magnetic
field. Most protons typically absorb between 0-10 ppm.

Reference Standard for NMR

To accurately determine absorption positions, a standard reference is used. NMR absorptions are
measured relative to a reference peak at 0 ppm on the delta scale.

¢ Tetramethyl silane (TMS): A volatile, inert compound with a low boiling point (27°C), giving a
single peak upfield, commonly used for organic compounds.

e Dimethyl silapentane sulfonic acid (DSS): More often used in protein experiments in water.

Properties of a good standard: Chemically inert, miscible with most organic compounds, low
melting point, and assumed to be free of shielding effect.

Why Tetramethylsilane (TMS) is used as a Reference:

============================================================
PAGE 34
============================================================
Tetramethylsilane (TMS) is the most commonly used reference standard in NMR spectroscopy. It is 
assigned a chemical shift of 0 ppm on the δ-scale. Its suitability as a reference standard stems from 
several key properties: 
 
Single, Intense Peak: All twelve protons in TMS are chemically equivalent, resulting in a single, 
strong absorption peak. This makes it easily identifiable and prevents confusion with sample 
signals. 
 
Highly Shielded Protons: The silicon atom in TMS is less electronegative than carbon, and it is 
bonded to four methyl groups. This arrangement makes the protons in TMS highly shielded, 
meaning their signal appears at a very high field (upfield) relative to most other organic 
compounds. This ensures that the TMS peak usually does not overlap with signals from the 
analyte, appearing "free of shielding effect". 
 
Chemical Inertness: TMS is chemically inert, meaning it does not react with the sample or the 
solvent. 
 
Miscibility: It is miscible with most organic compounds, allowing it to be easily dissolved in the 
sample solution as an internal reference. 
 
Low Boiling Point and Volatility: TMS has a low boiling point (27°C), making it easy to remove 
from the sample after analysis, especially if the sample needs to be recovered. 
 
Low Toxicity: It has relatively low toxicity, making it safe for laboratory use. 
 
Reasonable Solubility: It has reasonable solubility in common NMR solvents. 
 
Non-toxicity: TMS is non-toxic. 
3. Spin-Spin Splitting or Spin-Spin Coupling: 
o The splitting of resonance bands in NMR into doublets, triplets, quartets, etc., is known as 
spin-spin splitting or spin-spin coupling. It describes the magnetic interaction between 
neighboring non-equivalent nuclei. 
o Mechanism: Protons on adjacent carbons influence each other's magnetic environment. For 
example, two adjacent protons (Ha and Hb) can align with or against the applied magnetic 
field, causing the absorbing proton to experience slightly different magnetic fields, resulting 
in a split signal (e.g., a triplet with a 1:2:1 intensity ratio for two adjacent protons). 
o Significance: Coupling helps in identifying the types, number, and neighboring protons of 
each type. 
o Coupling Constant (J): The distance between the centers of adjacent peaks in a multiplet is 
a constant, known as the coupling constant (J), expressed in Hertz (Hz) or cycles/sec. It is 
characteristic of the structural relationship between coupled protons and is independent of 
the applied field and solvent. It helps distinguish between different types of multiplets (e.g., 
singlet vs. doublet, 1 quartet vs. 2 doublets). 
o Rules of Splitting:  
 
n+1 Rule: The splitting is into one more peak than the number of H's on the adjacent 
carbon(s).

--- OCR TEXT ---
Tetramethylsilane (TMS) is the most commonly used reference standard in NMR spectroscopy. It is
assigned a chemical shift of 0 ppm on the 6-scale. Its suitability as a reference standard stems from
several key properties:

¢ Single, Intense Peak: All twelve protons in TMS are chemically equivalent, resulting in a single,
strong absorption peak. This makes it easily identifiable and prevents confusion with sample
signals.

e Highly Shielded Protons: The silicon atom in TMS is less electronegative than carbon, and it is
bonded to four methyl groups. This arrangement makes the protons in TMS highly shielded,
meaning their signal appears at a very high field (upfield) relative to most other organic
compounds. This ensures that the TMS peak usually does not overlap with signals from the
analyte, appearing "free of shielding effect”.

¢ Chemical Inertness: TMS is chemically inert, meaning it does not react with the sample or the
solvent.

e Miscibility: It is miscible with most organic compounds, allowing it to be easily dissolved in the
sample solution as an internal reference.

e Low Boiling Point and Volatility: TMS has a low boiling point (27°C), making it easy to remove
from the sample after analysis, especially if the sample needs to be recovered.

e Low Toxicity: It has relatively low toxicity, making it safe for laboratory use.
e Reasonable Solubility: It has reasonable solubility in common NMR solvents.
e Non-toxicity: TMS is non-toxic.

. Spin-Spin Splitting or Spin-Spin Coupling:

o The splitting of resonance bands in NMR into doublets, triplets, quartets, etc., is known as
spin-spin splitting or spin-spin coupling. It describes the magnetic interaction between
neighboring non-equivalent nuclei.

o Mechanism: Protons on adjacent carbons influence each other's magnetic environment. For
example, two adjacent protons (Ha and Hb) can align with or against the applied magnetic
field, causing the absorbing proton to experience slightly different magnetic fields, resulting
in a split signal (e.g., a triplet with a 1:2:1 intensity ratio for two adjacent protons).

o Significance: Coupling helps in identifying the types, number, and neighboring protons of
each type.

o Coupling Constant (J): The distance between the centers of adjacent peaks in a multiplet is
aconstant, known as the coupling constant (J), expressed in Hertz (Hz) or cycles/sec. It is
characteristic of the structural relationship between coupled protons and is independent of
the applied field and solvent. It helps distinguish between different types of multiplets (e.g.,
singlet vs. doublet, 1 quartet vs. 2 doublets).

o Rules of Splitting:

= _n+1 Rule: The splitting is into one more peak than the number of H's on the adjacent
carbon(s).

============================================================
PAGE 35
============================================================
 
Relative Intensities: Intensities are in proportion to a binomial distribution, given by 
Pascal's Triangle. 
 
Equivalent Protons: Equivalent protons do not split each other. 
 
Distance: Protons farther than two carbon atoms apart generally do not split each 
other. Splitting is not typically observed between protons separated by more than 
three sigma bonds. 
 
Complex Splitting: For more than two interacting groups, the number of lines is 
(nB+1)(nC+1). 
VI. 
Instrumentation and Data Analysis 
A typical NMR spectrometer consists of seven major parts: 
1. Sample Tube: A borosilicate glass tube (15 cm long, 5 mm diameter) that holds the sample (0.5 ml 
of 15% solution with a reference standard like TMS or DSS). 
2. Magnet: Must be strong, stable, and produce a homogeneous field. Three types are used:  
o Permanent Magnets: Simple, cheaper, and provide a homogeneous field (up to 20,000 
Gauss) but lack flexibility and are heavy (~350 kg). 
o Electromagnets: Offer flexibility and homogeneous flux density (up to 100 MHz) but are 
heavy (~900-1000 kg) and require cooling systems. 
o Superconducting Magnets: Most powerful, offer high resolution, and are used in 
instruments with frequencies above 100 MHz, requiring Helium cooling. Operate at very low 
temperatures (e.g., -265.3°C in liquid helium) and provide high stability and field strength. 
3. Field Sweep Coil: Wrapped around or placed between magnet poles, it stabilizes and homogenizes 
the magnetic field and can vary its strength by altering current. 
4. Radiofrequency Transmitter or Generator: Transmits controlled frequency radiations to the 
sample, perpendicular to the magnetic field, to induce transitions. 
5. Radiofrequency Receiver: A coil wrapped around the sample tube, perpendicular to both the 
applied field and transmitter coil, it receives signals from the sample. 
6. Amplifier & Readout Device: Weak signals from the receiver coil are amplified and recorded, 
typically mechanically or on pre-calibrated charts with respect to reference compounds. 
7.  Solvents: Common solvents include deuterated chloroform (CDCl3), carbon tetrachloride (CCl4), 
deuterated benzene (C6D6), and heavy water (D2O). These are chosen for their chemical inertness, 
low viscosity, and lack of interfering proton signals (e.g., CDCl3 lacks ordinary protons, avoiding 
interference in 1H NMR). 
VII. 
Solvents Used in NMR 
Good NMR solvents should be: 
 
Cheap and easily available. 
 
Non-viscous. 
 
Chemically inert to the sample and holder.

VIII. 
Working of NMR Spectrometer 
1. A 0.5 ml, 15% sample solution (in a suitable solvent) is prepared in a test tube, with a few drops of 
reference standard (DSS or TMS) added. 
2. The sample tube is placed between the poles of the magnets. 
3. Radiofrequency radiations are directed onto the sample by the radiofrequency generator. 
4. The field strength is increased by varying the current in the sweep coil, causing the professional 
frequency of each set of protons to increase until resonance with the radiofrequency source occurs. 
5. The detector produces a signal when resonance is achieved. 
6. The signal is then amplified and recorded. 
IX. 
Applications of NMR Spectrometer 
NMR spectroscopy has diverse applications across multiple fields: 
 
Qualitative Analysis:  
o Structure Elucidation: Determines the molecular structure by analyzing the number, 
position, and splitting of signals (e.g., distinguishing methyl groups in a mixture based on 
chemical shifts and multiplicities). 
o Hydrogen Bonding: Identifies hydrogen-bonded protons, which appear downfield due to 
deshielding. 
o Conformational Analysis: Studies molecular conformations by analyzing signal patterns. 
o Optical Purity: Determines the enantiomeric composition of chiral compounds. 
 
Quantitative Analysis:  
o Measures the ratio of compounds in a mixture (e.g., integration of 1H NMR signals). 
o Assays drugs (e.g., corticosteroids) and determines iodine values. 
o Detects and quantifies impurities. 
 
Biological Applications:  
o Analyzes biomolecular interactions and structures (e.g., protein folding, ligand binding). 
 
Medical Applications:  
o Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI): Uses NMR principles for non-invasive imaging of 
tissues, detecting brain tumors, and distinguishing normal from malignant tissue. 
o Measures tissue perfusion and blood flow (angiography). 
 
Industrial Applications:  
o In the petroleum industry, NMR measures rock porosity and fluid permeability.

Limitations of NMR 
NMR spectroscopy, while powerful, has some limitations: 
 
Cost: Requires expensive instrumentation and specialized operators, though automation is 
available for routine analyses. 
 
Sample Requirements: Needs 5 mg for 1H NMR and 20 mg for 13C NMR, which may be challenging 
for scarce samples. 
 
Sensitivity: 13C NMR is less sensitive due to the low natural abundance of 13C, requiring larger 
samples or longer acquisition times. 
7. UV/Visible Spectroscopy 
UV/Visible (UV-Vis) Spectroscopy deals with the absorption of electromagnetic radiations in the 
visible and UV region. It is also known as Electronic Spectroscopy because the absorption of UV/Visible 
radiations primarily results in transitions among the electronic energy levels of the molecules. 
I. 
Principle 
When a beam of EMR passes through a compound, certain radiations are absorbed. The absorbed 
energy causes electronic excitation, promoting electrons to higher quantum levels within the 
molecule. A graph plotting the amount of radiation absorbed against the wavelength (the absorption 
spectrum) consists of bands with peaks of maximum intensity, with the overall range typically from 180 
nm to 850 nm. 
The more loosely the electrons are held in the bonds of molecules, the longer the wavelength (lower 
energy) is absorbed. 
II. 
Wavelength Regions 
 
The UV radiation region extends from 10 nm to 400 nm. 
o ◦ Near UV Region (Quartz region): 200 nm to 400 nm. UV-active compounds typically 
absorb in this region. 
o ◦ Far UV Region: below 200 nm. 
o ◦ Vacuum UV region: 100 nm  200 nm. This region cannot be studied by conventional UV 
spectrometers because oxygen in the air absorbs strongly here. Expelling oxygen with 
nitrogen can extend the range down to about 150 nm. 
 
The Visible radiation region extends from 400 nm to 800 nm. Common colors and their 
approximate wavelength ranges include  
Visible Spectrum Wavelengths: 
o Violet: 400 - 420 nm

UV/Visible Spectroscopy

UV/Visible (UV-Vis) Spectroscopy deals with the absorption of electromagnetic radiations in the
visible and UV region. It is also known as Electronic Spectroscopy because the absorption of UV/Visible
radiations primarily results in transitions among the electronic energy levels of the molecules.

I. Principle

When a beam of EMR passes through a compound, certain radiations are absorbed. The absorbed
energy causes electronic excitation, promoting electrons to higher quantum levels within the
molecule. A graph plotting the amount of radiation absorbed against the wavelength (the absorption
spectrum) consists of bands with peaks of maximum intensity, with the overall range typically from 180
nm to 850 nm.

The more loosely the electrons are held in the bonds of molecules, the longer the wavelength (lower
energy) is absorbed.

absorb in this region.

o Far UV Region: below 200 nm.

o °Vacuum UV region: 100 nm - 200 nm. This region cannot be studied by conventional UV
spectrometers because oxygen in the air absorbs strongly here. Expelling oxygen with
nitrogen can extend the range down to about 150 nm.

Electronic Transitions 
The absorption of UV-Visible radiations leads to the transfer of electrons from a lower energy state 
(ground state) to a higher energy state (excited state), which is called an "Electronic Transition".

Electronic Transitions

The absorption of UV-Visible radiations leads to the transfer of electrons from a lower energy state
(ground state) to a higher energy state (excited state), which is called an "Electronic Transition”.

In UV Spectroscopy, molecules undergo electronic transitions involving o (sigma), m (pi), and n (non-
bonding) electrons. The possible electronic transitions include:

1. o> 0* transition:

Involves the excitation of a sigma (o) electron from a bonding orbital to a corresponding anti-
bonding orbital (o*).

This transition requires a large amount of energy.

For example, Methane (CH4) undergoes this transition and shows an absorbance maximum
(Amax) at 125 nm.

2. n>o* transition:

Involves the transfer of electrons from a non-bonding (n) orbital of a heteroatom to an anti-
bonding sigma (o*) orbital.

Saturated compounds containing atoms with lone pair electrons (like O, N, S, and halogens)
are capable of this transition.

These transitions usually require less energy than o> o* transitions.
Peaks are typically observed in the UV region between 150-250 nm.

Examples include water (H20), methanol (CH30H), ammonia (NH3), and hydrogen disulfide
(H2S).

3. m>1* transition:

Involves the excitation of a pi (mt) electron from a bonding orbital to a corresponding anti-
bonding orbital (r*).

Compounds containing multiple bonds (like alkenes, alkynes, carbonyl, nitriles, and aromatic
compounds) undergo these transitions.

Alkenes generally absorb in the region of 170 to 205 nm, or more broadly, 170-250 nm.

4. n>n* transition:

An electron from a non-bonding (n) orbital is promoted to an anti-bonding pi (m*) orbital.

============================================================
PAGE 39
============================================================
Compounds containing a double bond involving heteroatoms (C=O, C≡N, N=O) undergo such 
transitions. 
 
These transitions require minimum energy and show absorption at longer wavelengths (λmax 
around 300 nm). 
The energy required for these transitions follows the order: σ σ

. 
5. σ 
σ* transition: 
These electronic transitions are forbidden transitions and are only theoretically possible. 
π* and π π* electronic transitions are the most commonly observed in the region 
above 200 nm, making them accessible to standard UV-Visible spectrophotometers, and are frequently 
involved in the analysis of organic molecules and drugs. 
A molecule possesses three types of energy. These include: 
 
Electronic Energy Levels: At room temperature, molecules are typically in their lowest electronic 
energy state (E0). When they absorb UV-visible light, an electron (from outermost bonding or lone 
pair) is promoted to a higher electronic energy state (excited states, E1, E2 ...En.). This is called an 
electronic transition, and the energy difference is given by ΔE = hν= En - E0. These transitions require 
the highest energy. 
 
Vibrational Energy Levels: These energy levels are lower than electronic energy levels, with 
smaller energy spacing (0.01 to 10 kcal/mole). When infrared (IR) radiation is absorbed, molecules 
are excited from one vibrational level to another, or they vibrate with higher amplitude. 
 
Rotational Energy Levels: These are quantized and discrete energy levels, with even smaller 
spacing than vibrational energy levels. They require the least amount of energy for transition. 
The hierarchy of energy required for these transitions is: ΔErotational < ΔEvibrational < ΔEelectronic. 
IV. 
Beer-Lambert Law 
The Beer-Lambert Law, also known as Beer-Lambert-Bouguer's Law, describes the relationship 
between the absorption of light and the properties of the material through which the light is traveling. 
 
History: Pierre Bouguer observed before 1729 that radiant power weakens as it passes through 
a solution. In 1760, Johann Heinrich Lambert stated that the loss of light intensity is directly 
proportional to the path length. In 1852, August Beer discovered that the loss of light intensity is 
directly proportional to the concentration. The modern Beer-Lambert law combines these two 
observations. 
 
Beer's Law: When a beam of monochromatic radiation is passed through an absorbing 
medium, the decrease in the intensity of the radiation is directly proportional to the 
concentration of the absorbing species in the solution. Mathematically, Absorbance (A) is 
proportional to Concentration (C). 
 
Lambert's Law: When a beam of monochromatic radiation is passed through an absorption 
medium, the decrease in the intensity of the radiation will be directly proportional to the 
thickness (path length) of the solution. Mathematically, Absorbance (A) is proportional to path 
length (l or b).

--- OCR TEXT ---
* Compounds containing a double bond involving heteroatoms (C=O, C=N, N=O) undergo such
transitions.

+ These transitions require minimum energy and show absorption at longer wavelengths (Amax
around 300 nm).

The energy required for these transitions follows the order:0>0*>n+o*>n>n*>n>n*.
5. on* transition and n+ o* transition:

These electronic transitions are forbidden transitions and are only theoretically possible.

Among these, n> 7m and 7r> 7 electronic transitions are the most commonly observed in the region

above 200 nm, making them accessible to standard UV-Visible spectrophotometers, and are frequently
involved in the analysis of organic molecules and drugs.

A molecule possesses three types of energy. These include:

e Electronic Energy Levels: At room temperature, molecules are typically in their lowest electronic
energy state (E0). When they absorb UV-visible light, an electron (from outermost bonding or lone
pair) is promoted to a higher electronic energy state (excited states, E1, E2 ...En.). This is called an
electronic transition, and the energy difference is given by AE = hv= En - E0. These transitions require
the highest energy.

e Vibrational Energy Levels: These energy levels are lower than electronic energy levels, with
smaller energy spacing (0.01 to 10 kcal/mole). When infrared (IR) radiation is absorbed, molecules
are excited from one vibrational level to another, or they vibrate with higher amplitude.

e Rotational Energy Levels: These are quantized and discrete energy levels, with even smaller
spacing than vibrational energy levels. They require the least amount of energy for transition.

The hierarchy of energy required for these transitions is: AErotational < AEvibrational < AEelectronic.

IV. Beer-Lambert Law

The Beer-Lambert Law, also known as Beer-Lambert-Bouguer's Law, describes the relationship
between the absorption of light and the properties of the material through which the light is traveling.

« History: Pierre Bouguer observed before 1729 that radiant power weakens as it passes through
a solution. In 1760, Johann Heinrich Lambert stated that the loss of light intensity is directly
proportional to the path length. In 1852, August Beer discovered that the loss of light intensity is
directly proportional to the concentration. The modern Beer-Lambert law combines these two
observations.

« Beer's Law: When a beam of monochromatic radiation is passed through an absorbing
medium, the decrease in the intensity of the radiation is directly proportional to the
concentration of the absorbing species in the solution. Mathematically, Absorbance (A) is
proportional to Concentration (C).

« Lambert's Law: When a beam of monochromatic radiation is passed through an absorption
medium, the decrease in the intensity of the radiation will be directly proportional to the
thickness (path length) of the solution. Mathematically, Absorbance (A) is proportional to path
length (lor b).

============================================================
PAGE 40
============================================================
Combined Beer-Lambert Law: States that when a beam of monochromatic radiation is passed 
through an absorbing medium, the decrease in the intensity of the radiation will be directly 
proportional to both the thickness (path length) and the concentration of the solution. 
The general equation is A = εbc. 
Where: 
 
A is Absorbance (dimensionless, defined as log10 (I0/I), where I0 is incident light intensity and I is 
transmitted light intensity). 
 
ε (epsilon) is the molar absorptivity (also called molar extinction coefficient), with units of L 
mol⁻¹ cm⁻¹. It's a proportionality constant that indicates how strongly a substance absorbs light 
at a particular wavelength. The larger the molar absorptivity, the more probable the electronic 
transition. 
 
b (or l) is the path length of the sample (typically in cm, often 1 cm). 
 
c is the concentration of the compound in solution (typically in mol L⁻¹ or mmol/L). 
Other Related Terms: 
 
Transmittance (T): The ratio of transmitted light (I) to incident light (I0) (T = I/I0). 
 
λ max (Absorbance Maximum): The wavelength at which a substance shows maximum 
absorbance. 
 
E1cm 1%: Absorbance of a 1% solution in a cell with 1 cm path length. This value is useful for 
determining drug concentrations when the molecular weight is unknown or a mixture is used. It 
can be related to molar absorptivity: ε = (E1cm 1% × Molecular weight) / 10. 
Limitations/Deviations of Beer-Lambert Law: The linearity of the Beer-Lambert law is limited by 
chemical and instrumental factors, leading to deviations (non-linear curves). 
 
Chemical Factors: 
o High Concentrations (>0.01M): At high concentrations, electrostatic interactions 
between molecules can alter their absorptivity coefficients. 
o Chemical Equilibria: Shifts in chemical equilibria (e.g., dissociation, association) as a 
function of concentration can affect the concentration of the absorbing species. 
o Fluorescence or Phosphorescence: If the sample fluoresces or phosphoresces, it re-
emits light, leading to an inaccurate absorbance reading. 
o Changes in Refractive Index: At high analyte concentrations, the refractive index of the 
solution can change, influencing light scattering. 
 
Instrumental Factors: 
o Non-monochromatic Radiation: The law strictly applies to monochromatic light. If the 
light beam is not perfectly monochromatic (i.e., it contains a range of wavelengths), 
deviations can occur as molar absorptivity varies with wavelength. 
o Stray Light: Unwanted light reaching the detector (stray light) can lead to inaccurate 
absorbance readings, especially at high absorbances.

--- OCR TEXT ---
* Combined Beer-Lambert Law: States that when a beam of monochromatic radiation is passed
through an absorbing medium, the decrease in the intensity of the radiation will be directly
proportional to both the thickness (path length) and the concentration of the solution.

The general equation is A = ebc.
Where:

+ Ais Absorbance (dimensionless, defined as log 10 (lo/I), where I is incident light intensity and | is

============================================================
o Instrumental Imperfections or Worn Components: Issues with the spectrophotometer 
itself, such as a dirty sample cell or an unstable light source, can also cause deviations. 
o Solubility Issues: If the solubility of the substance decreases at higher concentrations, 
light scattering can occur due to insoluble particulates. 
V. 
Key Concepts and Shifts in UV/Visible Spectroscopy 
 
Chromophore: A chromophore is a functional group within a molecule that is responsible for 
absorbing electromagnetic radiation, particularly in the UV and visible regions, and thus imparts 
color (if absorption is in the visible range). These groups typically contain multiple bonds capable of 
 
 
Examples: C=O (carbonyl), C=C (alkene), N=N (azo), NO2 (nitro), C≡N (nitrile). 
 
Effect of Conjugation: When double bonds or other unsaturated groups are conjugated 
(alternating single and double bonds), the λmax (wavelength of maximum absorption) shifts to 
longer wavelengths and often with increased intensity. For example, 1, 5-hexadiene (non-
conjugated) absorbs at 178 nm, while 2, 4-hexadiene (conjugated) absorbs at 227 nm. 
 
Auxochrome: An auxochrome is a functional group that does not absorb radiation in the near UV 
region on its own, but when attached to a chromophore, it modifies the chromophore's ability to 
absorb light. Auxochromes are typically groups with non-bonding electrons (lone pairs). They alter 
the wavelength (λmax) and/or intensity (ε) of absorption. 
 
Examples: -OH (hydroxyl), -NH2 (amino), -OCH3 (methoxy), -X (halogens). 
 
Effect: Auxochromes generally cause a bathochromic shift (red shift, shift to longer wavelength) 
and often a hyperchromic effect (increase in absorption intensity) by extending the conjugation or 
altering the electron distribution within the chromophore. For instance, benzene absorbs at 255 nm, 
while phenol (-OH attached) absorbs at 270 nm, and aniline (-NH2 attached) absorbs at 280 nm. 
VI. 
Absorption and Intensity Shifts  
They are categorized into four types, influenced by auxochromic substituents, solvent changes, or 
removal of conjugation: 
1. Bathochromic Shift (Red Shift): 
 
Occurs when the absorption maximum (λmax) of a compound shifts to a longer wavelength. 
 
This effect is typically due to the presence of an auxochrome or a change of solvent. 
 
Examples: An auxochrome group like -OH or -OCH3 causes absorption at longer wavelengths. 
Benzene (λmax = 255nm) to Phenol (λmax = 270nm) to Aniline (λmax = 280nm) is an example. 
2. Hypsochromic Shift (Blue Shift): 
 
Occurs when the absorption maximum (λmax) of a compound shifts to a shorter wavelength. 
 
This effect is due to the removal of conjugation or a change of solvent (towards higher 
polarity). 
 
Example: Aniline (λmax = 280 nm) shows a blue shift in acidic medium (λmax = 265 nm for 
anilinium ion) because it loses conjugation.

--- OCR TEXT ---
VI.

o Instrumental Imperfections or Worn Components: Issues with the spectrophotometer
itself, such as a dirty sample cell or an unstable light source, can also cause deviations.

o Solubility Issues: If the solubility of the substance decreases at higher concentrations,
light scattering can occur due to insoluble particulates.

V. Key Concepts and Shifts in UV/Visible Spectroscopy

Chromophore: A chromophore is a functional group within a molecule that is responsible for
absorbing electromagnetic radiation, particularly in the UV and visible regions, and thus imparts
color (if absorption is in the visible range). These groups typically contain multiple bonds capable of
undergoing n> n* and/or m > m* electronic transitions.

Examples: C=O (carbonyl), C=C (alkene), N=N (azo), NO2 (nitro), C=N (nitrile).

Effect of Conjugation: When double bonds or other unsaturated groups are conjugated
(alternating single and double bonds), the Amax (wavelength of maximum absorption) shifts to
longer wavelengths and often with increased intensity. For example, 1, 5-hexadiene (non-
conjugated) absorbs at 178 nm, while 2, 4-hexadiene (conjugated) absorbs at 227 nm.

Auxochrome: An auxochrome is a functional group that does not absorb radiation in the near UV
region on its own, but when attached to a chromophore, it modifies the chromophore's ability to
absorb light. Auxochromes are typically groups with non-bonding electrons (lone pairs). They alter
the wavelength (Amax) and/or intensity (€) of absorption.

Examples: -OH (hydroxyl), -NH2 (amino), -OCH3 (methoxy), -X (halogens).

Effect: Auxochromes generally cause a bathochromic shift (red shift, shift to longer wavelength)
and often a hyperchromic effect (increase in absorption intensity) by extending the conjugation or
altering the electron distribution within the chromophore. For instance, benzene absorbs at 255 nm,
while phenol (-OH attached) absorbs at 270 nm, and aniline (-NH2 attached) absorbs at 280 nm.

Absorption and Intensity Shifts

They are categorized into four types, influenced by auxochromic substituents, solvent changes, or
removal of conjugation:

1.

Bathochromic Shift (Red Shift):

* Occurs when the absorption maximum (Amax) of a compound shifts to a longer wavelength.

+ This effect is typically due to the presence of an auxochrome or a change of solvent.

+ Examples: An auxochrome group like -OH or -OCH3 causes absorption at longer wavelengths.
Benzene (Amax = 255nm) to Phenol (Amax = 270nm) to Aniline (Amax = 280nm) is an example.

2. Hypsochromic Shift (Blue Shift):

* Occurs when the absorption maximum (Amax) of a compound shifts to a shorter wavelength.

« This effect is due to the removal of conjugation or a change of solvent (towards higher
polarity).

« Example: Aniline (Amax = 280 nm) shows a blue shift in acidic medium (Amax = 265 nm for
anilinium ion) because it loses conjugation.

============================================================
PAGE 42
============================================================
3. Hyperchromic Shift: 
 
Occurs when the absorption intensity (ε) of a compound is increased. 
 
This can be due to the introduction of an auxochrome or alkyl substitution. 
 
Example: Pyridine (λmax = 257 nm, εmax = 2750) compared to 2-methyl pyridine (λmax = 260 nm, 
εmax = 3650). 
4. Hypochromic Shift: 
 
Occurs when the absorption intensity (ε) of a compound is decreased. 
 
This can happen when the introduction of a group alters the molecular pattern of the 
compound. 
 
Example: Naphthalene (ε = 19000) compared to 2-methyl naphthalene (ε = 10250). 
VII. 
Instrumentation of UV/Visible Spectrophotometer 
The main components of a UV-Vis spectrophotometer include a source of radiant energy, a collimating 
system, a monochromator system (or wavelength selector), a sample holder, a detector system, and a 
suitable amplifier or readout device. A general diagram shows light from a source passing through a 
collimating lens, a dispersing element, a sample container, then to a detector and finally a readout. 
There are generally two types of spectrophotometers: Single Beam Spectrophotometers and Double 
Beam Spectrophotometers. 
 
Single Beam Spectrophotometer: In a single beam spectrophotometer, light from the source 
passes through a lens or aperture and then through a suitable filter. The type of filter used is 
determined by the color of the solution. The sample solution to be analyzed is placed in cuvettes. 
o Uses only one beam of radiation that passes through a single cell. 
o The reference (blank) and sample cells must be slid into the radiation path in turn. The 
reference cell is used to set the absorbance scale to zero for the wavelength being 
studied, then replaced by the sample cell to measure absorbance. 
o Parts: Radiation energy source, Monochromator, Sample device, Detector, Data 
processing device. 
o How to use: Requires warming up, setting wavelength, selecting mode, placing blank for 
auto-zero, then placing sample and recording absorbance. 
o Example: Popular Bausch and Lomb Spectronic-20 Spectrophotometer. 
o Advantages: Inexpensive, wavelength can be easily selected, ease of operation due to 
simplicity. 
o Disadvantages: Any fluctuation in the intensity of radiation sources affects the 
absorbance. A continuous spectrum is not obtained. Requires manually switching 
reference and sample cuvettes for calibration 
 
Double Beam Spectrophotometer: A double beam spectrophotometer splits the light from the 
source into two paths: one for the sample and one for a reference or blank. This design uses

emax = 3650).

4. Hypochromic Shift:

« Occurs when the absorption intensity (<) of a compound is decreased.

« This can happen when the introduction of a group alters the molecular pattern of the
compound.

============================================================
PAGE 43
============================================================
mirrors to direct the light, and a chopper can alternate between the sample and blank beams 
before they reach the detector. 
o Divides the radiation into two beams of equal intensity, which are passed through two 
separate cells simultaneously. 
o One cell contains the sample solution, while the other (reference cell) contains the pure 
solvent or blank solution. 
o Example: Shimadzu spectrophotometer. 
o Advantages: More advantageous than single beam because the blank and sample can 
be used together, eliminating the need for continuous replacement. Little to no lamp 
warm-up time is required, improving throughput and conserving lamp life. Fluctuations 
due to the radiation source are minimized. It provides a ratio of intensities of sample and 
reference beams simultaneously. 
o Disadvantages: Construction is complicated and the instrument is expensive. 
The five basic optical instrument components are: 
1. Source 
2. Wavelength Selector 
3. Sample Container 
4. Detector/Photoelectric Transducer 
5. Signal Processor & Readout 
Let's discuss each component in detail: 
1. Source of Radiant Energy 
The source provides the radiant energy at the desired wavelength or range. 
 
Requirements of an Ideal Source: 
o It should be stable and not allow fluctuations. 
o It should emit light of a continuous spectrum of high and uniform intensity over the 
entire wavelength region in which it's used. 
o It should provide incident light of sufficient intensity for the transmitted energy to be 
detected at the end of the optic path. 
o It should not show fatigue on continued use. 
 
Sources for Visible Radiation: 
o Tungsten Halogen Lamp: Working range 350-2500 nm. This lamp is similar to a 
household lamp. The bulb contains a filament of Tungsten fixed in an evacuated 
condition and filled with an inert gas. The filament can be heated up to 3000 K, beyond 
which Tungsten starts sublimating. It is used when polychromatic light is required. To 
prevent sublimation, an inert gas, usually Iodine, is introduced, which reacts with 
sublimated tungsten to form a Tungsten-Iodine complex. This complex migrates back to 
the hot filament where it decomposes, and Tungsten is redeposited.

fe}

Tungsten Halogen Lamp: Working range 350-2500 nm. This lamp is similar to a
household lamp. The bulb contains a filament of Tungsten fixed in an evacuated
condition and filled with an inert gas. The filament can be heated up to 3000 K, beyond
which Tungsten starts sublimating. It is used when polychromatic light is required. To

============================================================
PAGE 44
============================================================
 
Demerit: It emits the major portion of its radiant energy in the near IR region of 
the spectrum. 
o For the visible region, a Tungsten lamp is generally used, and the sample holder is made 
of Glass/Quartz. 
 
Sources for UV Radiation: 
o Hydrogen Discharge Lamp: Working range 180-350 nm. In this lamp, a pair of electrodes 
is enclosed in a glass tube (typically with a silica or quartz window) for UV radiation to 
pass through, filled with hydrogen gas. When current passes through the electrodes at 
high voltage, discharge of electrons excites hydrogen molecules, causing emission of UV 
radiations in the near UV region. They are stable and robust. 
o Xenon Discharge Lamp: Working range 350-600 nm. This lamp uses two tungsten 
electrodes separated by some distance in a glass tube (for visible light) or quartz or fused 
silica (for UV light), filled with xenon gas under pressure. An intense arc forms between 
the electrodes by applying high voltage, providing a good source of continuous, 
additional intense radiation, with intensity higher than a hydrogen discharge lamp.  
 
Demerit: The lamp operates at high voltage and becomes very hot during 
operation, requiring thermal insulation. 
o Mercury Arc Lamp: In a mercury arc lamp, mercury vapor is stored under high pressure, 
and excitation of mercury atoms is done by electric discharge.  
 
Demerit: Not suitable for continuous spectral studies because it doesn't give 
continuous radiations. 
o For the ultraviolet region, a Deuterium lamp is generally used, and the sample holder is 
made of Quartz/Fused silica. Working range 180-380 nm. 
2. Collimating System 
The collimating system makes the radiation emitted by the source parallel using lenses, mirrors, and 
slits. 
 
Lenses: Materials used for lenses must be transparent to the radiation being used. Ordinary 
silicate glass transmits between 350 to 3000 nm and is suitable for visible and near IR regions. 
Quartz or fused silica is used for wavelengths below 300 nm. 
 
Mirrors: Mirrors are used to reflect, focus, or collimate light beams in the spectrophotometer. 
They are aluminized on their front surfaces to minimize light loss. 
 
Slits: Slits are important devices for resolving polychromatic radiation into monochromatic 
radiation. Both an entrance slit and an exit slit are used to achieve this. The width of the slit 
plays an important role in the resolution of polychromatic radiation. 
3. Monochromator System (Wavelength Selector) 
A wavelength selector isolates a restricted region of the electromagnetic (EM) spectrum for 
measurement. It provides a limited, narrow, continuous group of wavelengths called a band. 
Monochromators are devices used to isolate the desired wavelength from a continuous spectrum. There 
are two main types of wavelength selectors: Filters and Monochromators.
