plays an important role in the resolution of polychromatic radiation.

3. Monochromator System (Wavelength Selector)

A wavelength selector isolates a restricted region of the electromagnetic (EM) spectrum for
measurement. It provides a limited, narrow, continuous group of wavelengths called a band.
Monochromators are devices used to isolate the desired wavelength from a continuous spectrum. There
are two main types of wavelength selectors: Filters and Monochromators.

============================================================
PAGE 45
============================================================
 
Filters: 
o Selection of filters is a compromise between peak transmittance and band pass width; 
the former should be as high as possible and the latter as narrow as possible. 
o Absorption Filters: These work by selective absorption of unwanted radiation and 
transmitting the required radiation. Examples include Glass and Gelatin filters.  
 
Absorption glass filters are made of solid glass sheets colored by pigments 
dissolved or dispersed in the glass. The color in these filters is produced by 
incorporating metal oxides (e.g., V, Cr, Mn, Fe, Ni, Co, Cu). 
 
Gelatin filters are an example of absorption filters prepared by adding organic 
pigments to thin gelatin sheets. Gelatin filters are not commonly used today as 
they tend to deteriorate with time and are affected by heat and moisture, which 
can bleach the dye color. 
 
When analyzing a BLUE solution, a complementary color, ORANGE, is used as a 
filter. Similarly, the required filter in a colorimeter is selected based on the 
solution's color. 
 
Merits of Filters: Simple in construction, cheaper, and easy to select. 
 
Demerits of Filters: Less accurate. The band pass (bandwidth) is often wide (±20-
30nm), meaning if measuring at 400nm, radiation from 370-430nm might pass, 
leading to less accurate results. 
o Interference Filters: These work on the interference phenomenon, causing rejection of 
unwanted wavelengths by selective reflection. They are constructed using two parallel 
glass plates, which are silvered internally and separated by a thin film of dielectric 
material (e.g., CaF2, SiO, and MgF2) with a refractive index. These filters have a band 
pass of 10-15nm with peak transmittance of 40-60%.  
 
Merits: Provide greater transmittance and narrower band pass (10-15nm) 
compared to absorption filters. Inexpensive. Additional filters can be used to cut 
off undesired wavelengths. 
 
Monochromators: Wavelength selectors that can continuously scan a broad range of 
wavelengths. They are commonly used in most scanning spectrometers, including UV, visible, 
and IR instruments. 
o Prism: Made from glass, Quartz, or fused silica. Quartz or fused silica is the preferred 
material for the UV spectrum. When white light passes through a glass prism, dispersion 
of polychromatic light into a rainbow occurs. By rotating the prism, different 
wavelengths of the spectrum can be made to pass through an exit slit onto the sample. 
The effective wavelength depends on the dispersive power of the prism material and the 
optical angle of the prism.  
 
There are two types of mounting: 'Cornu type' (refractive), which has an optical 
angle of 60° and is adjusted so that the emerging light falls on the exit slit, and 
'Littrow type' (reflective), which has an optical angle of 30° and its surface is

fe}

Prism: Made from glass, Quartz, or fused silica. Quartz or fused silica is the preferred
material for the UV spectrum. When white light passes through a glass prism, dispersion
of polychromatic light into a rainbow occurs. By rotating the prism, different
wavelengths of the spectrum can be made to pass through an exit slit onto the sample.
The effective wavelength depends on the dispersive power of the prism material and the
optical angle of the prism.

« There are two types of mounting: 'Cornu type’ (refractive), which has an optical
angle of 60° and is adjusted so that the emerging light falls on the exit slit, and
‘Littrow type' (reflective), which has an optical angle of 30° and its surface is

============================================================
PAGE 46
============================================================
aluminized, reflecting light back through the prism to emerge from the same side 
of the light source. 
 
Comparison (Prism vs. Grating): Prisms give non-linear dispersion, hence no 
overlap of spectral order. They cannot be used over wide wavelength ranges and 
are not sturdy and long-lasting. 
o Gratings: More effective in converting polychromatic light to monochromatic light. A 
resolution of +/- 0.1nm can be achieved using gratings, making them commonly used in 
spectrophotometers. Gratings are of two types:  
 
Diffraction Grating: Provides more refined dispersion of light. These consist of a 
large number of parallel lines (grooves) (about 15000-30000 per inch) ruled on a 
highly polished aluminum surface. These gratings are often replicas made from a 
master grating by coating the original master grating with an epoxy resin, which 
is removed after setting. To make the surface reflective and minimize scattered 
radiation, a deposit of aluminum is made on the surface. Gratings are blazed to 
concentrate the radiation into a single order. 
 
Transmission Grating: Similar to a diffraction grating, but refraction takes place 
instead of reflection. Refraction produces reinforcement when radiation is 
transmitted through the grating and partially refracted radiation. 
 
Advantages of Gratings: Give higher and linear dispersions compared to prism 
monochromators. Can be used over wide wavelength ranges. Gratings can be 
constructed with materials like aluminum, which is resistant to atmospheric 
moisture. Provide light of narrow wavelength. No loss of energy due to 
absorption. They are sturdy and long-lasting. 
4. Sample Holders/Cuvettes 
The sample compartment holds the sample to be analyzed. Spectroscopy requires all materials in the 
beam path, including the analyte, to be transparent to the radiation as much as possible. 
 
Purpose: Cells or cuvettes are used for handling liquid samples. 
 
Shape: They can be rectangular or cylindrical. 
 
Materials:  
o For UV region studies, cells are prepared from quartz or fused silica. Quartz (Infrared) 
transmits 220-3800 nm, and Quartz (Far-UV) transmits 170-2700 nm. 
o For visible region studies, color-corrected fused glass is used. Optical Glass transmits 
335-2500 nm, and Special Optical Glass transmits 320-2500 nm. 
 
Maintenance: The surfaces of absorption cells must be kept scrupulously clean. No fingerprints 
or blotches should be present on cells. Cleaning is carried out by washing with distilled water or 
with dilute alcohol and acetone.

5. Detector System

============================================================
PAGE 47
============================================================
The detector system collects the transmitted radiation. A detector converts radiant energy into a 
useable signal, usually an electrical signal. The transmitted radiation falls on the detector, which 
determines the intensity of radiation absorbed by the sample. 
 
Requirements of an Ideal Detector:  
o It should give a quantitative response. 
o It should have high sensitivity and a low noise level. 
o It should have a short response time. 
o It should provide a signal or response quantitative to a wide spectrum of radiation 
received. 
 
Types of Detectors: Three common types are used:  
o Barrier Layer Cell/Photovoltaic Cell (Solar Cells): One of the simplest detectors, 
requiring no external power supply. It produces a current directly proportional to light 
intensity   
 
Construction: This detector has a thin film metallic layer coated with silver or 
gold acting as an electrode, and a metal base plate as another electrode. These 
two layers are separated by a semiconductor layer of selenium.  
 
Working: When light radiation falls on the selenium layer, electrons become 
mobile and are taken up by the transparent metal layer. This creates a potential 
difference between the two electrodes, causing a flow of current. When 
connected to a galvanometer, the flow of current observed is proportional to the 
intensity and wavelength of light falling on it. 
 
Sensitivity: Only moderate, typically used for instruments like photometers that 
allow a wide band of radiations to strike the detector. 
o Phototubes/Photoemissive Tubes: Consists of an evacuated glass tube with a 
photosensitive-cathode and a collector anode. The photocathode surface is coated with 
elements like cesium, silver oxide, or a mixture of them.  
 
Principle: When radiant energy falls on the photosensitive cathode, electrons are 
emitted, which are attracted to the anode, causing current to flow. Electrons flow 
to a positively charged collector (anode), and the current is proportional to 
radiation intensity.  
 
They are more sensitive compared to barrier layer cells and are widely used. 
o Photomultiplier Tube (PMT): Most commonly used detector in UV-Vis spectroscopy, very 
sensitive, especially for weak light intensities   
 
Principle: This detector works on the principle of multiplication of photoelectrons 
by secondary emission of electrons. It is a vacuum tube where a primary 
photocathode receives radiation from the sample. It contains about eight to ten 
dynodes, each fixed with an increasing potential of 75-100V higher than the 
preceding one. The last dynode is fixed with an anode or electron collector 
electrode.

--- OCR TEXT ---
The detector system collects the transmitted radiation. A detector converts radiant energy into a
useable signal, usually an electrical signal. The transmitted radiation falls on the detector, which
determines the intensity of radiation absorbed by the sample.

e Requirements of an Ideal Detector:
o  Itshould give a quantitative response.
o Itshould have high sensitivity and a low noise level.
o Itshould have a short response time.

o  Itshould provide a signal or response quantitative to a wide spectrum of radiation
received.

e Types of Detectors: Three common types are used:

o Barrier Layer Cell/Photovoltaic Cell (Solar Cells): One of the simplest detectors,

requiring no external power supply. It produces a current directly proportional to light
intensity

= Construction: This detector has a thin film metallic layer coated with silver or
gold acting as an electrode, and a metal base plate as another electrode. These
two layers are separated by a semiconductor layer of selenium.

* Working: When light radiation falls on the selenium layer, electrons become
mobile and are taken up by the transparent metal layer. This creates a potential
difference between the two electrodes, causing a flow of current. When
connected to a galvanometer, the flow of current observed is proportional to the
intensity and wavelength of light falling on it.

« Sensitivity: Only moderate, typically used for instruments like photometers that
allow a wide band of radiations to strike the detector.

o Phototubes/Photoemissive Tubes: Consists of an evacuated glass tube with a
photosensitive-cathode and a collector anode. The photocathode surface is coated with
elements like cesium, silver oxide, or a mixture of them.

» Principle: When radiant energy falls on the photosensitive cathode, electrons are

 
Characteristics: Very sensitive to UV and visible radiation, fast response times. 
However, intense light can damage them, so they are limited to measuring low 
power radiation. 
 
Sensitivity: Photomultipliers are extremely sensitive to light and are best suited 
where weaker or low radiation is received. 
For both Ultraviolet and Visible regions, Phototube, PM tube, and diode array detectors are used. 
o Photodiode Array (PDA): 
 
Also known as the "heart of spectroscopy". 
 
Principle: Based on the photoelectric effect (emission of electrons from a metal 
surface when exposed to light of suitable frequency). 
 
Construction/Working: Rows of p-type silicon on an n-type silicon substrate 
create pn-junction diodes. A reverse bias is applied, creating a depletion region 
that acts as a capacitor. When radiation strikes, free electrons and holes are 
created, partially discharging the capacitor. The amount of discharge is 
proportional to the radiation intensity. 
6. Amplifier: 
 
The current generated by detectors is often very low intensity. The main purpose of the 
amplifier is to amplify the signals many times to produce clear and recordable signals. 
7. Recording Devices: 
 
Signals from detectors are received by a recording system, often connected to a computer. The 
computer stores data and produces the spectrum of the compound. 
VIII. 
Applications of UV/Visible Spectroscopy 
UV/Visible spectroscopy has a wide range of applications, including: 
 
Detection of conjugation: Helps identify the presence of double and triple bonds (C=O, C=C, 
C≡C) and predict the location of substituents by observing λmax values. 
 
Detection of functional groups: Can detect specific functional groups, especially alpha, beta 
unsaturated carbonyl systems. The absence of absorption above 200 nm indicates the absence 
of conjugation. 
 
Detection of geometrical isomerism (Cis/Trans)
higher wavelengths with larger extinction coefficients compared to Cis isomers. 
 
Qualitative analysis (Identification of unknown compounds): Unknown compounds can be 
identified by comparing their spectrum with known spectra. Useful for identifying structures of 
vitamins and organic compounds. 
 
Quantitative analysis: 
o Determines the concentration of a known compound if it follows Beer-Lambert's law.

o Determines the concentration of a known compound if it follows Beer-Lambert's law.

suspension), Vitamin syrup, and Dexamethasone Injection. 
8. Gas Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (GC-MS) 
Gas Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (GC-MS) is an advanced analytical technique that combines 
the separation power of gas chromatography with the identification and quantification capabilities of 
mass spectrometry. This coupling was first reported in 1958 and became commercially available in 
1967, representing a significant development in the history of chromatography. 
I. 
Introduction to GC-MS 
The primary purpose of combining GC with MS is to provide confirmatory identification with minimal 
effort. Before mass spectrometers were commonly available, confirmatory analysis required 
significantly more effort, often necessitating analysis on two different GC columns. 
II. 
Principle of GC-MS 
The fundamental principle of GC-MS relies on the sequential operation of its two main components: 
 
Gas Chromatography (GC) separates the chemical mixture into pulses of pure chemicals. This 
separation is based on the polarity of the stationary phase and the mixture components. 
Compounds are separated according to their retention time. 
 
The Mass Spectrometer (MS) then identifies and quantifies these separated chemicals based on their 
structure. This identification uses the fragmentation pattern of the molecules, alongside retention 
time information from the GC, to confirm drug identity and enable quantitation. The elemental 
composition and structural elucidation of a sample are determined by MS. 
III. 
Instrumentation of GC-MS 
A GC-MS instrument is comprised of both GC and MS components, coupled together. 
1) Gas Chromatography (GC) Assembly 
The primary components of the GC system include:

--- OCR TEXT ---
o Involves measuring absorbance of a series of standard solutions, plotting a standard
curve, measuring the absorbance of the unknown sample, and then using the standard
curve to determine concentration.

o Candetermine quantitative analysis of drugs even at low concentrations, such as
paracetamol, vitamin B2 (riboflavin), vitamin C (ascorbic acid), and vitamin B12
(cyanocobalamin).

« Detection of impurities: One of the best methods for detecting impurities in organic
compounds, as impurities often produce intense additional peaks that can be compared with
standard raw materials.

Naphthalene Amax = 314nm, Anthracene Amax = 380nm).

« Pharmaceutical examples: Used for analysis of drugs like lbuprofen, Ponstan (tablet and
suspension), Vitamin syrup, and Dexamethasone Injection.

8. Gas Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (GC-MS)

Gas Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (GC-MS) is an advanced analytical technique that combines
the separation power of gas chromatography with the identification and quantification capabilities of
mass spectrometry. This coupling was first reported in 1958 and became commercially available in
1967, representing a significant development in the history of chromatography.

I. Introduction to GC-MS

The primary purpose of combining GC with MS is to provide confirmatory identification with minimal
effort. Before mass spectrometers were commonly available, confirmatory analysis required
significantly more effort, often necessitating analysis on two different GC columns.

Il. Principle of GC-MS

The fundamental principle of GC-MS relies on the sequential operation of its two main components:

e Gas Chromatography (GC) separates the chemical mixture into pulses of pure chemicals. This
separation is based on the polarity of the stationary phase and the mixture components.
Compounds are separated according to their retention time.

e The Mass Spectrometer (MS) then identifies and quantifies these separated chemicals based on their
structure. This identification uses the fragmentation pattern of the molecules, alongside retention
time information from the GC, to confirm drug identity and enable quantitation. The elemental
composition and structural elucidation of a sample are determined by MS.

Ill. Instrumentation of GC-MS
A GC-MS instrument is comprised of both GC and MS components, coupled together.
1) Gas Chromatography (GC) Assembly

The primary components of the GC system include:

============================================================
PAGE 50
============================================================
 
Carrier Gas System: The mobile phase in GC is a gas, with common choices being Hydrogen, 
Nitrogen, or Helium.  
o Hydrogen offers excellent results but poses a fire hazard and can react with unsaturated 
compounds. 
o Nitrogen is economical but provides less excellent results. 
o Helium is inert and provides excellent results, though it is expensive. 
o It is crucial to use carrier gas meeting high purity specifications (e.g., 99.9995% UHP grade) 
and employ O2 & moisture traps to prevent damage to the column, especially when heated 
above 70°C. 
 
Sample Introduction System: A known volume of liquid mixture (typically 0.5-2 µl) is injected into 
the head of the column. For capillary GC, it's recommended to fill the syringe with solvent before the 
sample. 
 
Column (Stationary Phase): This is the most critical component where separation occurs. The 
column is typically in a coil form, placed inside a specially controlled oven. The inner layer is usually 
silica, which is weakly polar.  
o Types of Columns: Metal or glass tubing (stainless steel) and glass columns are used. 
o Types of GC (based on stationary phase):  
 
Gas-Solid Chromatography (GSC): Mobile phase is gas, stationary phase is solid 
silica. 
 
Gas-Liquid Chromatography (GLC): Mobile phase is gas, and an additional liquid 
layer (e.g., Polyethylene glycol or Polysiloxene) coats the silica. 
o Temperature: GC operates at high temperatures, up to 300°C, meaning only thermo-stable 
or heat-stable compounds can be separated. An isothermal process maintains a constant 
temperature, while a temperature program involves controlled changes in temperature. 
 
Detection System/GC Detectors: While various detectors exist (e.g., FID, TCD), the Mass 
Spectrometer acts as the detector in a GC-MS system. 
2) Mass Spectrometry (MS) Assembly (as part of GC-MS) 
The basic parts of a mass spectrometer relevant to GC-MS are: 
 
Ion Source: Converts the separated analytes from the GC into gas-phase ions. For GC-MS, Electron 
Impact (EI) is a common method, where an electron beam bombards the volatilized sample to 
create radical cations. Chemical Ionization (CI) is another method, where a reagent gas is ionized 
first, then reacts with the sample molecules. 
 
Mass Analyzer: Separates these ions based on their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio.  
o Quadrupole Mass Analyzer: This is commonly used in benchtop GC/MS systems due to its 
relatively small size and low cost. It acts as a mass filter, allowing only selected m/z ratios to 
pass through.

--- OCR TEXT ---
e Carrier Gas System: The mobile phase in GC is a gas, with common choices being Hydrogen,
Nitrogen, or Helium.

o Hydrogen offers excellent results but poses a fire hazard and can react with unsaturated
compounds.

o Nitrogen is economical but provides less excellent results.
o Helium is inert and provides excellent results, though it is expensive.

2) Mass Spectrometry (MS) Assembly (as part of GC-MS)
The basic parts of a mass spectrometer relevant to GC-MS are:

e lon Source: Converts the separated analytes from the GC into gas-phase ions. For GC-MS, Electron
Impact (El) is a common method, where an electron beam bombards the volatilized sample to
create radical cations. Chemical lonization (Cl) is another method, where a reagent gas is ionized
first, then reacts with the sample molecules.

e Mass Analyzer: Separates these ions based on their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio.

o Quadrupole Mass Analyzer: This is commonly used in benchtop GC/MS systems due to its
relatively small size and low cost. It acts as a mass filter, allowing only selected m/z ratios to
pass through.

============================================================
PAGE 51
============================================================
o Time of Flight (TOF) Analyzer: This is well-suited for very fast GC/MS systems. It measures 
the time taken for ions to travel from the ion source to the detector, with lighter ions arriving 
faster. 
 
Detector: Measures the ions after separation, producing an electrical signal proportional to the 
light intensity. For MS, these include electron multipliers. The detector signals are directly 
proportional to the number of ions hitting the detector. 
 
Vacuum Pump: All mass spectrometers, including GC-MS, operate under high vacuum (10^-4  10^-8 
torr) to prevent ion collisions, which could lead to ionic reactions, neutralization, and scattering. 
This also helps attract ions towards the detector. 
 
Recording of Mass Spectrum: The electrical signals from the detector are digitalized and 
transferred to a data system, typically an online computer, for analysis and spectrum generation. A 
printer can provide hard copies of the mass spectrum. 
IV. 
Working of GC-MS 
The GC-MS process involves several sequential steps: 
1. Sample Injection: A known volume of liquid sample (which will be converted to vapor if liquid) is 
injected into the GC system. 
2. Chromatographic Separation: A carrier gas (mobile phase) carries the volatilized sample 
components through the heated column (stationary phase). Components separate based on their 
differential interaction (adsorption/desorption) with the stationary phase and their volatility, 
resulting in different retention times. 
3. Ionization: As separated components elute from the GC column, they enter the ion source of the MS. 
Here, they are converted into gas-phase ions. 
4. Mass Analysis: The ions are then introduced into the mass analyzer, which separates them 
according to their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio. 
5. Detection: Separated ions hit the detector, which measures their abundance and generates an 
electrical signal. 
6. Data Acquisition: The electrical signals are processed by a data system, which constructs a mass 
spectrum a plot of ion signal intensity versus m/z ratio. The highest peak is recorded as 100%, 
known as the base peak. The fragments are then used to elucidate the structure of the sample. 
5. Data Analysis 
GC-MS enables both qualitative and quantitative analysis. 
 
Qualitative Analysis: This identifies which components are present in the sample mixture. The 
number of peaks corresponds to the number of components. If the retention time of a mixture 
component is similar to a reference compound, it suggests a match. The unique fragmentation 
pattern generated by the MS is crucial for positive identification. 
 
Quantitative Analysis: This determines the amount or concentration of each component.  
o Peak height can indicate the quality of the drug.

--- OCR TEXT ---
o Time of Flight (TOF) Analyzer: This is well-suited for very fast GC/MS systems. It measures
the time taken for ions to travel from the ion source to the detector, with lighter ions arriving
faster.

Detector: Measures the ions after separation, producing an electrical signal proportional to the
light intensity. For MS, these include electron multipliers. The detector signals are directly
proportional to the number of ions hitting the detector.

Vacuum Pump: All mass spectrometers, including GC-MS, operate under high vacuum (10-4 - 10-8
torr) to prevent ion collisions, which could lead to ionic reactions, neutralization, and scattering.
This also helps attract ions towards the detector.

Recording of Mass Spectrum: The electrical signals from the detector are digitalized and
transferred to a data system, typically an online computer, for analysis and spectrum generation. A
printer can provide hard copies of the mass spectrum.

IV. Working of GC-MS

The GC-MS process involves several sequential steps:

1.

Sample Injection: A known volume of liquid sample (which will be converted to vapor if liquid) is
injected into the GC system.

Chromatographic Separation: A carrier gas (mobile phase) carries the volatilized sample
components through the heated column (stationary phase). Components separate based on their
differential interaction (adsorption/desorption) with the stationary phase and their volatility,
resulting in different retention times.

lonization: As separated components elute from the GC column, they enter the ion source of the MS.
Here, they are converted into gas-phase ions.

Mass Analysis: The ions are then introduced into the mass analyzer, which separates them
according to their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio.

Detection: Separated ions hit the detector, which measures their abundance and generates an
electrical signal.

Data Acquisition: The electrical signals are processed by a data system, which constructs a mass
spectrum—a plot of ion signal intensity versus m/z ratio. The highest peak is recorded as 100%,
known as the base peak. The fragments are then used to elucidate the structure of the sample.

5. Data Analysis

GC-MS enables both qualitative and quantitative analysis.

Qualitative Analysis: This identifies which components are present in the sample mixture. The
number of peaks corresponds to the number of components. If the retention time of a mixture
component is similar to a reference compound, it suggests a match. The unique fragmentation
pattern generated by the MS is crucial for positive identification.

Quantitative Analysis: This determines the amount or concentration of each component.

o Peak height can indicate the quality of the drug.

============================================================
PAGE 52
============================================================
o Peak area provides information about the quantity of the drug. Peak areas are generally 
preferred for quantitation as they are independent of broadening effects. 
o Percent composition can be calculated by comparing the area under each peak to the total 
area of all peaks. Modern instruments often include computers or digital integrators for 
precise estimation of peak areas. 
V. 
Strengths of GC-MS 
The combined GC-MS technique offers significant advantages: 
 
High Separating Power: It has much greater separating power than HPLC when capillary columns 
are used. 
 
Sensitive and Selective Identification: Provides rapid and confirmatory identification, especially 
for trace impurities. 
 
Quantitative Accuracy and Precision: Capable of high quantitative accuracy and precision, 
particularly with an internal standard. 
 
Automation: Readily automated, from sample introduction to separation. 
 
Versatility: Can determine compounds lacking chromophores. 
 
Cost-Effective Mobile Phase: The mobile phase (gas) does not vary and does not require disposal, 
making it cheaper than organic solvents used in HPLC. 
 
Structural Information: The MS component is crucial for elemental composition and structural 
elucidation. 
VI. 
Limitations of GC-MS 
Despite its strengths, GC-MS has certain limitations: 
 
Sample Volatility and Thermal Stability: Only thermally stable and volatile compounds can be 
analyzed. Non-volatile or thermally unstable compounds cannot pass the GC separation. 
 
Sample Preparation: Samples may require derivatization to convert them into a volatile form, 
which adds an extra step and potential interferents. Aqueous solutions and salts generally cannot 
be injected into the instrument. 
 
Instrument Maintenance: The MS component operates under high vacuum, requiring critical 
vacuum maintenance. 
 
Purity of GC Feed: If the GC portion does not completely separate compounds, the MS feed will be 
impure, leading to background "noise" in the mass spectrum. 
 
Interpretation Complexity: The MS portion suffers from the inexact practice of interpreting mass 
spectra, requiring the analyst to correlate computer calculations with system conditions. 
 
Cost: The instrumentation is expensive and requires support from highly trained personnel for 
operation and regular maintenance. 
VII. 
Applications of GC-MS 
GC-MS has diverse applications across various fields: 
 
Pharmaceutical Analysis:

preferred for quantitation as they are independent of broadening effects.

o Percent composition can be calculated by comparing the area under each peak to the total
area of all peaks. Modern instruments often include computers or digital integrators for
precise estimation of peak areas.

9. Liquid Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (LC-MS) 
Liquid Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (LC-MS), also known as High-Pressure Liquid 
Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (HPLC-MS), is a sophisticated analytical technique that combines 
the high-resolution separation capabilities of liquid chromatography with the sensitive and specific 
detection of mass spectrometry. This coupling represents a significant advancement in the history of 
chromatography, emerging in the 1980s. 
I. 
Introduction to LC-MS 
LC-MS is an analytical method designed to separate components of a sample and then identify and 
characterize them based on their mass-to-charge ratio. It can also be found in variations such as 
Capillary Electrophoresis (CE)-MS and Capillary Electrochromatography (CEC)-MS. The primary goal of 
LC-MS is to determine the elemental composition and structural elucidation of a sample. 
II. 
Principle of LC-MS 
A typical LC-MS system integrates High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) with a Mass 
Spectrometer via an interface, which acts as an ionization source. The process unfolds in these steps: 
 
First, the sample is separated by the liquid chromatography (LC) portion. 
 
The separated sample components are then sprayed into an atmospheric pressure ion source, 
where they are converted into ions in the gas phase.

--- OCR TEXT ---
o Analysis of drugs in biological fluids and tissues.
o Determination of nicotine levels.
o Separation, characterization, and quantification of benzoic and phenolic antioxidants.
o Quality control in pharmaceutical manufacturing.
e Forensic Sciences:
o Identification and quantification of substances in toxicology cases and crime scene evidence.
o Arson investigation and paint-chip analysis.
o Confirmation of drug abuse.
e Environmental Analysis:
o Identification of phenolic compounds.
o Analysis of metals (e.g., mercury, arsenic, zinc, cadmium, antimony) in water.
o Airsample quality control (Air QC).

e Food Industry: Trace elements and harmful metals in food and drinks can be tracked.
Determination of rancidity in dairy products.

e General Chemical Analysis:

Liquid Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (LC-MS)

Liquid Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (LC-MS), also known as High-Pressure Liquid
Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (HPLC-MS), is a sophisticated analytical technique that combines
the high-resolution separation capabilities of liquid chromatography with the sensitive and specific
detection of mass spectrometry. This coupling represents a significant advancement in the history of
chromatography, emerging in the 1980s.

I. Introduction to LC-MS

LC-MS is an analytical method designed to separate components of a sample and then identify and
characterize them based on their mass-to-charge ratio. It can also be found in variations such as
Capillary Electrophoresis (CE)-MS and Capillary Electrochromatography (CEC)-MS. The primary goal of
LC-MS is to determine the elemental composition and structural elucidation of a sample.

Il. Principle of LC-MS

Atypical LC-MS system integrates High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) with a Mass
Spectrometer via an interface, which acts as an ionization source. The process unfolds in these steps:

e First, the sample is separated by the liquid chromatography (LC) portion.

e Theseparated sample components are then sprayed into an atmospheric pressure ion source,
where they are converted into ions in the gas phase.

============================================================
PAGE 54
============================================================
 
Next, a mass analyzer sorts these ions based on their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio. 
 
Finally, a detector counts the ions as they emerge from the mass analyzer, often amplifying the 
signal. 
 
This process generates a mass spectrum, which is a plot of the ion signal against the mass-to-
charge ratio, providing information about the elemental or isotopic nature, masses of particles and 
molecules, and chemical structures of the analytes. 
III. 
Requirements of Liquid Chromatography in LC-MS 
For the liquid chromatography component, HPLC is typically the chosen method. HPLC separation is 
primarily based on adsorption principles, operating in either normal phase or reverse phase mode. 
 
Normal Phase HPLC (NP-HPLC): Utilizes a polar stationary phase (e.g., silica) and a non-polar 
mobile phase (e.g., hexane, methylene chloride, chloroform, diethyl ether, and mixtures). Polar 
samples are retained longer on the polar stationary phase. However, this mode is generally not 
widely used in biomedical research or pharmaceutical applications, as most drugs are polar and 
would experience prolonged elution times. 
 
Reversed Phase HPLC (RP-HPLC): Employs a nonpolar (hydrophobic) stationary phase (e.g., 
octadecylsilane (ODS)/C18, C8, C4) and a polar mobile phase (e.g., mixtures of water with methanol 
or acetonitrile). Separation occurs based on hydrophobic interactions, with more nonpolar 
materials being retained longer. RP-HPLC finds wide application in pharmaceutical analysis due 
to its suitability for polar drugs. 
IV. 
Instrumentation of LC-MS 
The LC-MS system requires several key components to function effectively: 
 
Liquid Chromatography Assembly: This includes the mobile phase reservoir, pump, gradient 
controller, sample injector, and analytical columns.  
o Mobile Phase Reservoir: Holds HPLC-grade solvents, which must be degassed and filtered 
(e.g., through a 2µm stainless steel filter) to prevent pump damage and detector 
interference. 
o HPLC Pump: Forces the mobile phase through the column at high pressure and a constant 
flow rate, typically using inert materials to prevent corrosion. Reciprocating piston pumps 
are commonly used. 
o Gradient Controller: Allows for dynamic changes in the mobile phase composition (e.g., 
linear, concave, convex, or stepped gradients) to optimize separation. 
o Sample Injector: Introduces the sample into the column, requiring a zero dead volume 
valve. Both manual and automated systems are available with variable loop sizes. 
o Analytical Columns: The "heart of the chromatography system" where separation occurs, 
usually made of stainless steel and packed with specific stationary phases (e.g., fully porous, 
superficially porous layer beads, or micro-particulate). A guard column is often placed 
before the analytical column to protect it from impurities and extend its lifespan. 
 
Ion Generation Unit / Ionization Source: Converts the separated analytes into gas-phase ions.

--- OCR TEXT ---
Next, a mass analyzer sorts these ions based on their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio.
Finally, a detector counts the ions as they emerge from the mass analyzer, often amplifying the
signal.

This process generates a mass spectrum, which is a plot of the ion signal against the mass-to-
charge ratio, providing information about the elemental or isotopic nature, masses of particles and
molecules, and chemical structures of the analytes.

III. Requirements of Liquid Chromatography in LC-MS

For the liquid chromatography component, HPLC is typically the chosen method. HPLC separation is
primarily based on adsorption principles, operating in either normal phase or reverse phase mode.

IV.

Normal Phase HPLC (NP-HPLC): Utilizes a polar stationary phase (e.g., silica) and a non-polar
mobile phase (e.g., hexane, methylene chloride, chloroform, diethyl ether, and mixtures). Polar
samples are retained longer on the polar stationary phase. However, this mode is generally not
widely used in biomedical research or pharmaceutical applications, as most drugs are polar and
would experience prolonged elution times.

Reversed Phase HPLC (RP-HPLC): Employs a nonpolar (hydrophobic) stationary phase (e.g.,
octadecylsilane (ODS)/C18, C8, C4) and a polar mobile phase (e.g., mixtures of water with methanol
or acetonitrile). Separation occurs based on hydrophobic interactions, with more nonpolar
materials being retained longer. RP-HPLC finds wide application in pharmaceutical analysis due
to its suitability for polar drugs.

Instrumentation of LC-MS

The LC-MS system requires several key components to function effectively:

Liquid Chromatography Assembly: This includes the mobile phase reservoir, pump, gradient
controller, sample injector, and analytical columns.

o Mobile Phase Reservoir: Holds HPLC-grade solvents, which must be degassed and filtered
(e.g., through a 2um stainless steel filter) to prevent pump damage and detector
interference.

o HPLC Pump: Forces the mobile phase through the column at high pressure and a constant
flow rate, typically using inert materials to prevent corrosion. Reciprocating piston pumps
are commonly used.

o Gradient Controller: Allows for dynamic changes in the mobile phase composition (e.g.,
linear, concave, convex, or stepped gradients) to optimize separation.

o Sample Injector: Introduces the sample into the column, requiring a zero dead volume
valve. Both manual and automated systems are available with variable loop sizes.

o Analytical Columns: The “heart of the chromatography system" where separation occurs,
usually made of stainless steel and packed with specific stationary phases (e.g., fully porous,
superficially porous layer beads, or micro-particulate). A guard column is often placed
before the analytical column to protect it from impurities and extend its lifespan.

lon Generation Unit / lonization Source: Converts the separated analytes into gas-phase ions.

============================================================
PAGE 55
============================================================
o Most Common Types: Electrospray Ionization (ESI), Atmospheric Pressure Chemical 
Ionization (APCI), and Matrix-Assisted Laser Desorption/Ionization (MALDI). 
o Other Types: Electron Impact (EI), Chemical Ionization (CI), and Negative Chemical 
Ionization. 
 
Mass Analyzer: Separates ions by their mass-to-charge (m/z) ratio. For LC-MS, common types 
mentioned are Magnetic Sector Mass Analyzers and Fourier Transform-Ion Cyclotron Resonance 
(FT-ICR).  
 
Detectors: Measures the ions, producing an electrical signal. Examples include Electron Multipliers, 
Dynolyte Photomultipliers, and Micro Channel Fourier Transform-Ion Cyclotron Resonance. 
 
Mass Spectrometric Data Acquisition: Digitalizes the electrical signals from the detector and 
transfers them to a data system in a compatible format for analysis and spectrum generation. 
V. 
Strengths and Limitations 
Strengths: 
 
High Resolution Separation: LC-MS offers "high resolution chromatographic separation". 
 
Sensitive and Specific Detection: It provides "sensitive and specific mass spectrum detection". 
 
Elemental Composition and Structural Elucidation: The mass spectrometry component is crucial 
for determining elemental composition and elucidating the structure of samples. 
 
Overcomes MS limitations: Mass spectrometry alone is not efficient for separation, making its 
combination with LC essential for analyzing complex mixtures. 
Limitations (Inferred from broader context within the sources): 
 
Complexity of Instrumentation: The instrumentation for both HPLC and MS can be complex, 
implying that the combined LC-MS system is also inherently complex. 
 
Cost and Maintenance: Mass spectrometry instruments are expensive and require highly trained 
personnel for support and regular maintenance. This extends to LC-MS systems as well. 
VI. 
Applications of LC-MS 
LC-MS is a versatile technique with widespread applications across various fields: 
 
Molecular Weight Determination: It is capable of determining the molecular weight of chemical 
and pharmaceutical substances, as well as proteins. 
 
Structural Determination / Elucidation: Tandem mass spectrometry (MS/MS or MS2), which 
involves coupling two mass analyzers, is particularly used for obtaining structural information 
through mass spectral fragmentations. 
 
Pharmaceutical Applications: Plays a crucial role in determining the pharmacokinetic profile of 
drugs, including drug metabolites, degradation products, impurities, and especially chiral 
impurities. The detection of chiral impurities is vital due to potential differences in 
pharmacological, metabolic, and toxicological activities between D and L isomers. 
 
Clinical and Biochemical Applications: MALDI-TOF MS, a specific LC-MS technique, is utilized in SNP 
genotyping, DNA quantification, gene expression analysis, and DNA/RNA sequencing.

Chromatography 
Chromatography is a fundamental analytical method used primarily for the separation of a sample 
mixture. The term itself originates from the Greek words 'Khromatos' meaning "Color" and 'Graphy' 
meaning "measurements or writing," as it was initially used by Russian botanist Mikhail Tswett to separate 
colors from vegetable juice. Chromatography is considered both a physical process, because no new 
compounds are formed, and a chemical process, because the composition changes before and after the 
analysis. 
At its core, chromatography facilitates the separation of substances based on their differential migration 
within a porous sorptive medium, driven by the flow of solvents. This technique allows for the 
determination of the number and quantity of components in a mixture. 
Fundamental Principles 
The separation in chromatography relies on the distribution of the sample mixture between two phases: 
 
Stationary Phase: This can be a solid or a liquid, typically supported as a thin film on the surface of 
an inert solid. It is the phase over which the mobile phase flows. 
 
Mobile Phase: This is the solvent, which can be a gas or a liquid, that flows over the stationary 
phase to separate the components of the mixture. 
The underlying basis of chromatography is polarity. Separation occurs because of the differential 
interaction (based on polarity) of the sample components with the stationary and mobile phases. 
Components that are less attracted to the solid stationary phase or more soluble in the liquid mobile phase 
will move faster. The order of polarity refers to the sequence of solvents with increasing or decreasing 
polarity, for example, n-Hexane < Petroleum ether < Chloroform < Ethyl acetate < Alcohol (Methanol > 
Ethanol > Propanol > Butanol), with water being the most polar solvent. 
I. 
Types of Separation (Development of Chromatogram) 
Three main types of chromatographic development: 
1. Frontal Analysis: In this method, the sample solution continuously passes through the sorbent. 
Strongly adsorbed components occupy active sites, while the least strongly adsorbed components 
accumulate in the traveling front. The solvent elutes first, followed by the least strongly adsorbed 
solutes. This method is primarily used for the removal of relatively small, undesirable components. 
2. Displacement Analysis: The mixture is first introduced to the sorbent, followed by a mobile phase 
containing a strongly adsorbed displacing agent. This agent forces all components out of their 
sorption sites, causing them to move ahead of the displacer. Components then displace each other, 
with the least adsorbed leaving the column first. This technique allows for heavy sample loading 
and is suitable for preparative work. 
3. Elution: In elution, a solute partitions between the two phases, and separation is based on relative 
retention. Elution can be:

--- OCR TEXT ---
e Food and Environmental Applications: Used for identifying toxins like aflatoxins in food and
determining compounds such as vitamin D3 in poultry feed supplements. It also aids in detecting
phenols in drinking water and estrogens in coastal waters.

¢ Capillary Electrophoresis / MS Applications: Specifically employed for the analysis of peptides.
Chromatography

Chromatography is a fundamental analytical method used primarily for the separation of a sample
mixture. The term itself originates from the Greek words 'Khromatos' meaning "Color" and 'Graphy'
meaning "measurements or writing,” as it was initially used by Russian botanist Mikhail Tswett to separate
colors from vegetable juice. Chromatography is considered both a physical process, because no new
compounds are formed, and a chemical process, because the composition changes before and after the
analysis.

At its core, chromatography facilitates the separation of substances based on their differential migration
within a porous sorptive medium, driven by the flow of solvents. This technique allows for the
determination of the number and quantity of components in a mixture.

Fundamental Principles
The separation in chromatography relies on the distribution of the sample mixture between two phases:

e Stationary Phase: This can be a solid or a liquid, typically supported as a thin film on the surface of
an inert solid. It is the phase over which the mobile phase flows.

e Mobile Phase: This is the solvent, which can be a gas or a liquid, that flows over the stationary
phase to separate the components of the mixture.

The underlying basis of chromatography is polarity. Separation occurs because of the differential
interaction (based on polarity) of the sample components with the stationary and mobile phases.
Components that are less attracted to the solid stationary phase or more soluble in the liquid mobile phase
will move faster. The order of polarity refers to the sequence of solvents with increasing or decreasing
polarity, for example, n-Hexane < Petroleum ether < Chloroform < Ethyl acetate < Alcohol (Methanol >
Ethanol > Propanol > Butanol), with water being the most polar solvent.

I. Types of Separation (Development of Chromatogram)
Three main types of chromatographic development:

1. Frontal Analysis: In this method, the sample solution continuously passes through the sorbent.
Strongly adsorbed components occupy active sites, while the least strongly adsorbed components
accumulate in the traveling front. The solvent elutes first, followed by the least strongly adsorbed
solutes. This method is primarily used for the removal of relatively small, undesirable components.

2. Displacement Analysis: The mixture is first introduced to the sorbent, followed by a mobile phase
containing a strongly adsorbed displacing agent. This agent forces all components out of their
sorption sites, causing them to move ahead of the displacer. Components then displace each other,
with the least adsorbed leaving the column first. This technique allows for heavy sample loading
and is suitable for preparative work.

3. Elution: In elution, a solute partitions between the two phases, and separation is based on relative
retention. Elution can be:

============================================================
PAGE 57
============================================================
o Isocratic: Elution conditions remain constant throughout the experiment. 
o Gradient: Intentional changes are made to elution conditions during development, which 
can involve altering the mobile phase composition, temperature, or pressure. 
II. 
Theoretical Considerations 
Several key parameters and theories are used to describe and quantify chromatographic separation: 
 
Partition Coefficient (K): Represents the equilibrium distribution of an analyte between the 
stationary (Cs) and mobile (Cm) phases, expressed as K = Cs/Cm. A higher K value means the solute 
will take a longer time to pass through the column. 
 
Retention Time (tR): The time required for a component to travel the length of the column (L). It 
can be expressed as tR = L/V, where V is the average linear rate of solute migration. 
 
Capacity Factor (k'): An important experimental parameter describing the migration rate of a 
solute. It is proportional to the partition coefficient and the ratio of the stationary phase volume (Vs) 
to the mobile phase volume (Vm). An ideal k' value is typically between 1 and 5. Values less than 1 
indicate elution is too rapid, while values greater than 20-30 suggest excessively long elution times. 
It can be calculated as k' = (tR - tM) / tM, where tM is the dead time. 
 
Retention Volume (VR): The volume of mobile phase needed to elute a sample component. It is 
calculated as VR = tR * F, where F is the flow rate of the mobile phase. The basic retention equation is 
VR = VM(1 + K'), where VM is the void volume. 
 
Selectivity Factor (α): Compares the retention of two species (A and B), indicating the column's 
ability to differentiate them. It is defined as α = KB/KA or α = k'B/k'A. 
 
Resolution (Rs): Measures the degree of separation between two peaks, aiming for narrow peaks 
and large spacing between them. It is given as Resolution = Peak Separation / Average Peak Width. 
Ideal and Non-Ideal Chromatography: In an ideal situation, all molecules would behave identically, and 
component zones (bands or peaks) would not spread, resulting in sharp, completely separated peaks. In 
non-ideal situations, random molecular behavior causes zones to become diffuse and broad as they pass 
through the column. 
III. 
Theories of Chromatography 
Two main theories explain chromatography: 
1. Plate Theory: Developed by Martin & Synge in 1941, this theory conceptualizes the column as being 
divided into a number (N) of adjacent imaginary segments called theoretical plates. Within each 
theoretical plate, analytes are assumed to completely equilibrate between the stationary and 
mobile phases. 
o Significance: Greater separation is achieved with a greater number of theoretical plates 
(N) and a smaller plate height (H or HETP). HETP is defined as L/N, where L is the column 
length. N can be estimated experimentally from a chromatogram using the formula N = 
16(tR/w)^2 or N = 5.55(tR/w1/2)^2. 
o Predictions: The theory predicts that band spreading (width of bands) increases as retention 
time or volume increases. It also predicts that a smaller HETP leads to a narrower eluted

--- OCR TEXT ---
0 Isocratic: Elution conditions remain constant throughout the experiment.

o Gradient: Intentional changes are made to elution conditions during development, which
can involve altering the mobile phase composition, temperature, or pressure.

I. Theoretical Considerations

Several key parameters and theories are used to describe and quantify chromatographic separation:

Partition Coefficient (K): Represents the equilibrium distribution of an analyte between the
stationary (Cs) and mobile (Cm) phases, expressed as K = Cs/Cm. A higher K value means the solute
will take a longer time to pass through the column.

Retention Time (tR): The time required for a component to travel the length of the column (L). It
can be expressed as tR = L/V, where V is the average linear rate of solute migration.

Capacity Factor (k’): An important experimental parameter describing the migration rate of a
solute. It is proportional to the partition coefficient and the ratio of the stationary phase volume (Vs)
to the mobile phase volume (Vm). An ideal k' value is typically between 1 and 5. Values less than 1
indicate elution is too rapid, while values greater than 20-30 suggest excessively long elution times.
It can be calculated as k' = (tR - tM) / tM, where tM is the dead time.

Retention Volume (VR): The volume of mobile phase needed to elute a sample component. It is
calculated as VR = tR * F, where F is the flow rate of the mobile phase. The basic retention equation is
VR = VM(1 +k’), where VM is the void volume.

Selectivity Factor (a): Compares the retention of two species (A and B), indicating the column's
ability to differentiate them. It is defined as a = KB/KA or a =k'B/k'A.

Resolution (Rs): Measures the degree of separation between two peaks, aiming for narrow peaks
and large spacing between them. It is given as Resolution = Peak Separation / Average Peak Width.

Ideal and Non-Ideal Chromatography: In an ideal situation, all molecules would behave identically, and
component zones (bands or peaks) would not spread, resulting in sharp, completely separated peaks. In
non-ideal situations, random molecular behavior causes zones to become diffuse and broad as they pass
through the column.

Ill. Theories of Chromatography

Two main theories explain chromatography:

1.

Plate Theory: Developed by Martin & Synge in 1941, this theory conceptualizes the column as being
divided into a number (N) of adjacent imaginary segments called theoretical plates. Within each
theoretical plate, analytes are assumed to completely equilibrate between the stationary and
mobile phases.

o Significance: Greater separation is achieved with a greater number of theoretical plates
(N) and a smaller plate height (H or HETP). HETP is defined as L/N, where L is the column
length. N can be estimated experimentally from a chromatogram using the formula N =
16(tR/w)*2 or N = 5.55(tR/w1/2)42.

o Predictions: The theory predicts that band spreading (width of bands) increases as retention
time or volume increases. It also predicts that a smaller HETP leads to a narrower eluted

============================================================
PAGE 58
============================================================
peak. A key factor for maximum column efficiency (N_Max) is a smaller particle size (dp), with 
N_Max = 0.4 * L/dp. 
2. Rate Theory: Proposed by van Deemter in 1956, this theory provides a more realistic description by 
accounting for the dynamics of separation and the time taken for the solute to equilibrate between 
phases. It considers various processes that contribute to band broadening: 
o Eddy Diffusion (Term A): Molecules take different paths through the packed stationary 
phase at random, causing band broadening due to varying path lengths. This term is 
independent of mobile phase velocity (u). 
o Longitudinal Diffusion (Term B): Analyte diffuses from the center of the band to the edges, 
broadening the band. This term dominates at low mobile phase velocities; higher velocities 
reduce its effect. 
o Resistance to Mass Transfer (Term C): Analyte requires time to equilibrate between 
stationary and mobile phases. At high mobile phase velocities, analyte in the mobile phase 
can move ahead of analyte in the stationary phase, causing broadening. 
o The Van Deemter equation (HETP = A + B/u + C*u) describes how these factors contribute to 
plate height, where 'u' is the average velocity of the mobile phase. A plot of plate height vs. 
average linear velocity of the mobile phase (Van Deemter plot) is useful for determining the 
optimum flow rate. 
IV. 
General Instrumentation 
A typical chromatographic system, particularly column chromatography, consists of several key 
components: 
 
A Stationary Phase: Chosen to be appropriate for the analytes being separated. 
 
A Column: Contains the stationary phase, with dimensions varying by technique (e.g., stainless steel 
or glass, 25-50 cm long with 4mm internal diameter for liquid chromatography). 
 
A Mobile Phase and Delivery System: The solvent and the mechanism (e.g., pumping system or gas 
pressure) to deliver it through the column at a constant rate. 
 
An Injector System: Delivers the test samples to the top of the column in a reproducible manner. 
 
A Detector and Chart Recorder: Continuously records the presence of analytes as they emerge 
from the column, often based on physical parameters like visible or ultraviolet absorption or 
fluorescence. Each separated analyte typically appears as a peak on the chart recorder. 
 
A Fraction Collector: Gathers separated analytes for further studies. 
The preparation of the column typically involves packing it with the stationary phase using either a dry 
packing or wet packing method. The sample is then introduced, usually dissolved in a minimum quantity of 
mobile phase, and adsorbed onto the top of the column. Elution then separates the components. Detection 
methods vary depending on whether the compounds are colored or require reagents or UV absorbance to 
be visualized.

peak. A key factor for maximum column efficiency (N_Max) is a smaller particle size (dp), with
N_Max = 0.4 * L/dp.

Column Chromatography 
Column chromatography is a separation technique where components of a mixture are separated using a 
glass column packed with a stationary phase, through which a liquid mobile phase flows continuously. It 
operates primarily on the principle of adsorption, where solutes in a mixture separate based on their 
affinity for the stationary and mobile phases. Molecules with higher affinity for the stationary phase elute 
later, while those with lower affinity elute earlier, a process driven by differential migration. 
I. 
Principles of Column Chromatography 
Column chromatography encompasses several separation mechanisms, each based on distinct 
interactions between the analyte, stationary phase, and mobile phase: 
1. Adsorption Chromatography: 
o Principle: Separation is based on the adsorption of solutes at the solid-liquid interface. The 
stationary phase is a solid adsorbent (e.g., silica gel), and the mobile phase is a liquid. 
Separation occurs due to differences in the affinity of components for the stationary phase, 
which is typically reversible. Components with lower affinity move faster through the 
column. 
o Mechanism: In normal-phase chromatography, adsorption occurs via hydrogen bonding 
between the solute and hydroxyl groups on the stationary phase (e.g., silica). In reverse-
phase chromatography, van der Waals interactions dominate. 
o Solvents: 
 
Normal-phase: Hexane, dichloromethane, isopropanol, methanol (increasing 
polarity). 
 
Reverse-phase: Water, methanol, acetonitrile, tetrahydrofuran (increasing strength). 
2. Partition Chromatography: 
o Principle: Separation is based on the distribution of solutes between two immiscible liquid 
phases (liquid stationary phase and liquid or gaseous mobile phase). The separation 
aw as the ratio of 
solute activities in the two phases. 
o Application: Used for separating polar and non-polar compounds. 
3. Ion-Exchange Chromatography: 
o Principle: Separation involves the reversible exchange of ions between the sample and an 
ion-exchange resin (stationary phase). The resin contains charged functional groups that 
interact with oppositely charged ions in the solution. 
o Example Reaction: (\text{Resin-H}^+ + \text{M}^+ \rightarrow \text{Resin-M}^+ + \text{H}^+). 
o Application: Separation of proteins, amino acids, and nucleotides. 
4. Size-Exclusion Chromatography (Gel Permeation/Filtration): 
o Principle: Separation is based on molecular size, with the stationary phase being a porous 
matrix (e.g., cross-linked polystyrene, dextran, polyacrylamide, or agarose gels). Larger

--- OCR TEXT ---
Column Chromatography

Column chromatography is a separation technique where components of a mixture are separated using a
glass column packed with a stationary phase, through which a liquid mobile phase flows continuously. It
operates primarily on the principle of adsorption, where solutes in a mixture separate based on their
affinity for the stationary and mobile phases. Molecules with higher affinity for the stationary phase elute
later, while those with lower affinity elute earlier, a process driven by differential migration.

I. Principles of Column Chromatography

Column chromatography encompasses several separation mechanisms, each based on distinct
interactions between the analyte, stationary phase, and mobile phase:

1. Adsorption Chromatography:

o Principle: Separation is based on the adsorption of solutes at the solid-liquid interface. The
stationary phase is a solid adsorbent (e.g., silica gel), and the mobile phase is a liquid.
Separation occurs due to differences in the affinity of components for the stationary phase,
which is typically reversible. Components with lower affinity move faster through the
column.

o Mechanism: In normal-phase chromatography, adsorption occurs via hydrogen bonding
between the solute and hydroxyl groups on the stationary phase (e.g., silica). In reverse-
phase chromatography, van der Waals interactions dominate.

o Solvents:

» Normal-phase: Hexane, dichloromethane, isopropanol, methanol (increasing
polarity).

= Reverse-phase: Water, methanol, acetonitrile, tetrahydrofuran (increasing strength).
2. Partition Chromatography:

o Principle: Separation is based on the distribution of solutes between two immiscible liquid
phases (liquid stationary phase and liquid or gaseous mobile phase). The separation
depends on the partition coefficient, defined by Nernst’s distribution law as the ratio of
solute activities in the two phases.

o Application: Used for separating polar and non-polar compounds.
3. lon-Exchange Chromatography:

o Principle: Separation involves the reversible exchange of ions between the sample and an
ion-exchange resin (stationary phase). The resin contains charged functional groups that
interact with oppositely charged ions in the solution.

o Example Reaction: (\text{Resin-H}*+ + \text{M}*+ \rightarrow \text{Resin-M}‘+ + \text{H}‘+).
o Application: Separation of proteins, amino acids, and nucleotides.
4. Size-Exclusion Chromatography (Gel Permeation/Filtration):

o Principle: Separation is based on molecular size, with the stationary phase being a porous
matrix (e.g., cross-linked polystyrene, dextran, polyacrylamide, or agarose gels). Larger

============================================================
PAGE 60
============================================================
molecules elute first as they cannot enter the pores, while smaller molecules are retained 
longer. 
o Application: Separation of proteins, polysaccharides, enzymes, and synthetic polymers. 
II. 
Theoretical Concepts 
The efficiency of column chromatography depends on several key parameters: 
 
Concentration and Adsorption: When a sample solution of concentration (C) is passed through an 
adsorbent column, (n) grams of the sample are adsorbed per gram of the adsorbent, described by 
the function (n = f(C)). 
 
Retention Volume: The volume of solvent required to elute (n) grams of the sample is known as the 
retention volume (R), expressed as (R = f(C)). 
 
Differential Migration: Different components of a mixture travel through the stationary phase at 
varying speeds, influenced by the mobile phase. This differential migration is the basis for 
separation. 
 
Capacity Factor
column to the void time (or dead time), which is the time taken by the mobile phase to pass through 
the column without interacting with the stationary phase. 
III. 
Requirements for Column Chromatography 
The successful execution of column chromatography requires careful consideration of several components 
and steps: 
 Column Characteristics and Selection: 
o Dimensions: The efficiency depends on the length-to-width ratio, typically 10:1 to 100:1 for 
optimal separation. The sample-to-column packing ratio should be between 1:20 and 1:100. 
o Material: Glass columns are common for gravity chromatography, while stainless steel is 
used for high-pressure columns. 
IV. 
Types of Columns 
Columns in chromatography are classified based on the mechanism of mobile phase movement. The main 
types include: 
 Gravity Columns: The mobile phase flows under gravity, suitable for simple separations. 
 Flash Columns: Use air or nitrogen pressure to accelerate the mobile phase, commonly used in 
flash chromatography. 
 Low & Medium Pressure Columns: Utilize pumps to drive the mobile phase, offering better 
control over flow rates. 
 Vacuum Columns: The mobile phase is moved by applying a vacuum, enabling faster elution. 
 High Pressure Columns: Made of stainless steel, these columns use high-pressure pumps to 
push the mobile phase, ideal for high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC).

longer.

o Application: Separation of proteins, polysaccharides, enzymes, and synthetic polymers.

Theoretical Concepts

The efficiency of column chromatography depends on several key parameters:

¢ Concentration and Adsorption: When a sample solution of concentration (C) is passed through an
adsorbent column, (n) grams of the sample are adsorbed per gram of the adsorbent, described by
the function (n =f(C)).

e Retention Volume: The volume of solvent required to elute (n) grams of the sample is known as the
retention volume (R), expressed as (R = f(C)).

e Differential Migration: Different components of a mixture travel through the stationary phase at
varying speeds, influenced by the mobile phase. This differential migration is the basis for
separation.

e Capacity Factor: The capacity factor is defined as the ratio of a compound’s retention time in the
column to the void time (or dead time), which is the time taken by the mobile phase to pass through
the column without interacting with the stationary phase.

used for high-pressure columns.

3. Column Packing: 
 Wet Packing 
Wet packing involves preparing the column with a slurry of adsorbent and solvent. The steps are: 
o Mix the adsorbent (e.g., silica gel, alumina, or magnesia) with an appropriate solvent to 
form a slurry. 
o Pour the slurry slowly into the column to ensure even packing. 
o Place a sintered-glass disc, glass-wool plug, or cotton plug at the bottom to prevent 
leakage.

3. Column Packing:
e Wet Packing
Wet packing involves preparing the column with a slurry of adsorbent and solvent. The steps are:

o Mix the adsorbent (e.g., silica gel, alumina, or magnesia) with an appropriate solvent to
form aslurry.

o Pourthe slurry slowly into the column to ensure even packing.

o Place asintered-glass disc, glass-wool plug, or cotton plug at the bottom to prevent
leakage.

============================================================
PAGE 62
============================================================
o Stir the slurry with a glass rod to eliminate trapped air bubbles, which can affect 
performance. 
o Once the adsorbent settles, place a Whatman filter paper disc on top. 
o Add a layer of washed sand (SiO2) to protect the adsorbent. 
o Allow the solvent to drain until it is approximately 1 cm above the packed adsorbent. 
 Dry Packing 
Dry packing involves filling the column with dry adsorbent without a solvent. This method is not 
suitable for gel chromatography. The steps are: 
o Pour dry adsorbent (e.g., silica gel or alumina) into the column. 
o Tap the column gently and regularly to ensure even packing and prevent gaps. 
o Fill the column to about three-quarters of its height with the adsorbent. 
o Leave headspace above the packed adsorbent. 
o Add the mobile phase to the headspace before starting the separation. 
 Precautions for Column Packing 
o Ensure uniform packing, neither too compact nor too loose, to prevent uneven 
separation or tailing. 
o Avoid air bubbles or cracks in the column, as these can disrupt the separation process. 
4. Sample Introduction: 
o Wet Application: Dissolve the sample in the initial mobile phase and apply using a pipette 
l, just above the adsorbent layer (covered by sand and filter 
paper). Ideal when the sample is soluble in the mobile phase. 
o Dry Loading: Dissolve the sample in a volatile solvent, adsorb onto a small amount of 
adsorbent, evaporate the solvent, and apply the dry adsorbent to the column. Used when 
the sample is insoluble in the mobile phase. 
5. Development Techniques: 
o Frontal Analysis: Continuously add the sample solution without a separate mobile phase. 
Components separate based on their adsorption strength (least adsorbed elute first). 
o Displacement Analysis: Add a small volume of the sample, followed by a mobile phase 
containing a displacer (a solute with strong adsorptivity). The displacer pushes out less 
adsorbed components. 
o Elution Analysis: Add a small sample volume and elute with a mobile phase. Components 
separate based on their differential adsorption and are collected as eluates. 
 
Isocratic Elution: Isocratic elution uses a single, pre-determined solvent mixture 
(e.g., chloroform or chloroform: methanol = 1:1)) throughout the separation process. 
It is suitable for less complex mixtures and can be visualized as colored bands in a 
transparent column (e.g., a nylon tube). The separated components can be collected 
by segmenting the column.

 
Gradient Elution: Uses solvents of increasing polarity to elute strongly adsorbed 
components, reducing tailing and producing concentrated bands. Modern systems 
use microprocessor-controlled gradient profiles. 
6. Monitoring and Detection: 
o Collect fractions via elution analysis. 
o Analyze fractions using TLC to identify components with similar Rf values, which are 
combined. 
o Evaporate the solvent, dry the material, and collect it in an Eppendorf container. 
o Use spectral analysis (NMR, MS, X-ray diffraction) for compound identification. 
7. Isolation of Separated Compounds: 
o Components are collected as separate fractions, dried, and analyzed to confirm purity and 
identity. 
Factors Affecting Column Efficiency 
Several factors influence the efficiency of column chromatography, as outlined in the documents: 
1. Column Dimensions: A length-to-width ratio of 10:1 to 100:1 and a sample-to-packing ratio of 1:20 
to 1:100 optimize separation. 
2. Pore Diameter: Pores <20 Å enhance polar adsorption, while 17 20 Å maintain efficiency without 
impairment. 
3. Particle Size: Uniform particles (60 200 µm) ensure consistent flow and separation. Smaller 
particles are used in flash chromatography, larger ones in gravity chromatography. 
4. Ambient Temperature: Affects solvent viscosity and adsorption kinetics, impacting flow rate and 
separation efficiency. 
5. Column Packing: Must be uniform to avoid air bubbles, cracks, or uneven packing, which cause 
ineffective separation or tailing effects. 
6. Nature of Solvents: Low-viscosity solvents improve flow rates and separation efficiency, as per 
 
Applications of Column Chromatography 
Column chromatography is widely used in the pharmaceutical industry and other fields due to its 
versatility: 
o Purification of Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients (APIs): 
o Separates APIs (e.g., vitamin B12, oxytetracycline, oleandomycin) from reaction 
mixtures. 
o Removes impurities to meet regulatory standards. 
o Separation of Natural Products: 
o Isolates bioactive compounds (e.g., alkaloids, glycosides, flavonoids) from plant, 
microbial, or marine sources.

Factors Affecting Column Efficiency
Several factors influence the efficiency of column chromatography, as outlined in the documents:

1. Column Dimensions: A length-to-width ratio of 10:1 to 100:1 and a sample-to-packing ratio of 1:20
to 1:100 optimize separation.

2. Pore Diameter: Pores <20 A enhance polar adsorption, while 17-20 Amaintain efficiency without

4. Ambient Temperature: Affects solvent viscosity and adsorption kinetics, impacting flow rate and
separation efficiency.

5. Column Packing: Must be uniform to avoid air bubbles, cracks, or uneven packing, which cause
ineffective separation or tailing effects.

6. Nature of Solvents: Low-viscosity solvents improve flow rates and separation efficiency, as per
Poiseuille’s Law.

Applications of Column Chromatography

Column chromatography is widely used in the pharmaceutical industry and other fields due to its
versatility:

o Purification of Active Pharmaceutical Ingredients (APIs):

o Separates APIs (e.g., vitamin B12, oxytetracycline, oleandomycin) from reaction
mixtures.

o Removes impurities to meet regulatory standards.
o Separation of Natural Products:

o Isolates bioactive compounds (e.g., alkaloids, glycosides, flavonoids) from plant,
microbial, or marine sources.

o Analyzes pharmaceutical composition for consistency.

o Produces high-purity compounds for analytical standards. 
5. Advantages of Column Chromatography 
Column chromatography offers several benefits: 
 
Ability to separate any type of mixture. 
 
Capable of separating any quantity of mixture. 
 
Wider choice of mobile phase. 
 
Separated samples can be reused in preparative applications. 
 
Automation is possible. 
6. Limitations of Column Chromatography 
Despite its advantages, column chromatography has limitations: 
 
It can be time-consuming. 
 
Requires larger amounts of solvents, which can be expensive. 
 
Automation can make the technique more complicated and costly.

1. Liquid-Solid Chromatography (LSC)

Liquid-Solid Chromatography (LSC) is a type of liquid chromatography where the stationary phase is a
solid and the mobile phase is a liquid.

e Principle and Mechanism:

============================================================
PAGE 65
============================================================
o The separation mechanism in LSC is based on the competition of the components of the 
mixture sample for the active sites on an adsorbent. 
o This is an adsorption chromatography process, involving distribution between two phases 
through adsorption and desorption. 
o Attractive forces such as ionic, dipole-dipole, and dipole-induced dipole interactions play a 
role. 
o Good adsorbents are characterized by a large surface area and more active sites. 
o Equilibration occurs at the surface-solute, surface-solvent, and solvent-solute interfaces. 
 
Adsorption Isotherms: An adsorption isotherm plots the concentration or amount of analyte on the 
adsorbent surface as a function of its concentration in the bulk phase (mobile phase). Types include: 
o Linear: where the partition coefficient (k = Cs / Cm) equals 1. 
 
Adsorbents: The adsorbing power of a material depends on its chemical nature, available surface 
area, and pretreatment. Commonly used adsorbents include: 
o Alumina (Al2O3): Can be acidic, basic, or neutral, and is available in various grades. 
o Silica gel (silicon dioxide): Acidic in nature and available in various grades; it has a high 
capacity and is useful for both adsorption and partition chromatography. 
o CaCO3. 
o Sucrose. 
o Starch. 
o Cellulose. Adsorbents should have uniform size and a large surface area. The weight of the 
adsorbent should typically be 20-50 times more than the sample. 
 
Solvents & Adsorbates: 
o Since adsorbents are generally polar, non-polar compounds elute first. 
o The typical elution order for adsorbates, from non-polar to polar, is: alkyl halides < saturated 
hydrocarbons < unsaturated hydrocarbons < ethers < esters < ketones < amines < alcohols < 
phenols < acids and bases. 
o Polymeric compounds and salts often do not elute. 
o Solvents are ordered by increasing polarity (elution strength): Hexane/Petroleum ether < 
CCl4 < toluene < dichloromethane < chloroform < diethyl ether < acetone < ethyl acetate < 
propanol < ethanol < methanol < acetic acid < water. 
 
Columns and Packing: Various sizes of columns are available, and packing can be done using 
either wet method or dry method. 
 
Example Application: LSC is used for separating water-soluble vitamins.

2. Liquid-Liquid Chromatography (LLC) 
Liquid-Liquid Chromatography (LLC), also known as partition chromatography, is a technique where the 
stationary phase is a second liquid that is coated onto an inert solid surface and is immiscible with the 
solvent (mobile) phase. 
 
Principle and Mechanism: 
o Separation is achieved by the partitioning of the sample between the two immiscible 
liquid phases, which causes some components to be retained longer than others. 
o This partitioning delays or retains certain components, leading to their separation. 
 
Advantages over Adsorption Chromatography: 
o LLC is generally more reproducible and predictable. 
o The distribution coefficient remains constant over a much greater range of 
concentrations, resulting in sharper and more symmetrical peaks. 
 
Types of LLC: 
o Normal Phase LLC: Uses a polar stationary phase and a non-polar mobile phase. 
o Reversed Phase LLC: Employs a nonpolar (hydrophobic) stationary phase and a polar 
liquid mobile phase (e.g., mixtures of water and methanol or acetonitrile). This mode works 
on the principle of hydrophobic interactions, meaning the more nonpolar the material, the 
longer it will be retained. Reversed phase mode has a wide range of pharmaceutical 
applications. 
 
Supports for Stationary Phase: Common materials used to support the stationary liquid phase 
include: 
o Silica gel. 
o Kieselguhr / Celite. 
o Cellulose. 
 
Steps in Liquid-Liquid Chromatography: The process generally involves: 
o Sample preparation. 
o Sample loading. 
o Elution: The mobile phase carries the separated components through the column. 
o Detection: Chemical methods and diverse types of detectors can be used to monitor the 
separated components. 
Both LSC and LLC fall under the broader category of liquid-column chromatography, which uses glass or 
plastic columns, may require manual solvent flow (gravity, suction), and involves manual collection and 
analysis of fractions. These classical techniques have limitations such as tedious column packing, low 
column efficiency, long analysis times, and user dependency. Modern advancements, like High-
Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC), address many of these issues, offering improved efficiency 
and automation.

--- OCR TEXT ---
