analysis of fractions. These classical techniques have limitations such as tedious column packing, low
column efficiency, long analysis times, and user dependency. Modern advancements, like High-
Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC), address many of these issues, offering improved efficiency
and automation.

============================================================
PAGE 67
============================================================
3. High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) 
High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) is a highly refined form of column liquid 
chromatography. Unlike traditional methods where solvent drips through a column by gravity, HPLC 
forces the solvent through under high pressures, sometimes up to 400 atmospheres, making the 
separation process much faster. 
High Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) is a widely used and powerful separation technique for 
analyzing various compounds. It has been utilized for approximately 35 years and is currently the largest 
separation technique available. 
I. 
Fundamentals and Principles of HPLC 
HPLC is an abbreviation for High Performance Liquid Chromatography, though it was also previously 
known as High Pressure Liquid Chromatography. At its core, HPLC is a separation technique that involves: 
 
The injection of a small volume of liquid sample into a tube known as the column. 
 
This column is packed with tiny particles (typically 3 to 5 micrometers (µm) in diameter) called the 
stationary phase. 
 
Individual components of the sample are then moved down the packed column by a liquid called 
the mobile phase, which is forced through the column by high pressure delivered by a pump. 
 
The components of the sample are separated from one another by the column packing through 
various chemical and/or physical interactions between their molecules and the packing particles. 
 
These separated components are detected at the exit of the column by a flow-through device 
called a detector, which measures their amount. The output from this detector is called a liquid 
chromatogram. 
II. 
History of HPLC 
The journey of HPLC began in the early 1960s as High Pressure Liquid Chromatography. By the end of the 
1970s, significant improvements in column materials and instrumentation led to its rebranding as High 
Performance Liquid Chromatography. The "boom" in HPLC started around the early 1980s. Since 2006, 
newer terms like UPLC, RRLC, UFLC, and RSLC have also emerged. 
Comparison with Liquid Chromatography (LC) 
Traditional Liquid Chromatography (LC), now often referred to as "flash chromatography," involves: 
 
Injecting a small liquid sample volume into a tube packed with porous particles (stationary phase). 
 
Components are transported along the column by a liquid moved by gravity. 
 
Separated components are collected at the column exit and identified by an external measurement 
technique, such as a spectrophotometer. 
In principle, LC and HPLC operate similarly, but HPLC offers vastly superior speed, efficiency, sensitivity, 
and ease of operation. HPLC uses tiny particles in its stationary phase and high-pressure pumps, 
distinguishing it from traditional LC. 
III. 
HPLC Instrument Configurations 
HPLC systems can be found in various configurations:

--- OCR TEXT ---

High Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) is a widely used and powerful separation technique for
analyzing various compounds. It has been utilized for approximately 35 years and is currently the largest
separation technique available.

I. Fundamentals and Principles of HPLC

HPLC is an abbreviation for High Performance Liquid Chromatography, though it was also previously

e Theinjection of a small volume of liquid sample into a tube known as the column.

e This column is packed with tiny particles (typically 3 to 5 micrometers (um) in diameter) called the
stationary phase.

e Individual components of the sample are then moved down the packed column by a liquid called
the mobile phase, which is forced through the column by high pressure delivered by a pump.

e The components of the sample are separated from one another by the column packing through
various chemical and/or physical interactions between their molecules and the packing particles.

e These separated components are detected at the exit of the column by a flow-through device
called a detector, which measures their amount. The output from this detector is called a liquid

Il. History of HPLC

The journey of HPLC began in the early 1960s as High Pressure Liquid Chromatography. By the end of the
1970s, significant improvements in column materials and instrumentation led to its rebranding as High
Performance Liquid Chromatography. The "boom" in HPLC started around the early 1980s. Since 2006,
newer terms like UPLC, RRLC, UFLC, and RSLC have also emerged.

Comparison with Liquid Chromatography (LC)

Traditional Liquid Chromatography (LC), now often referred to as "flash chromatography," involves:
e Injecting a small liquid sample volume into a tube packed with porous particles (stationary phase).
e Components are transported along the column by a liquid moved by gravity.

e Separated components are collected at the column exit and identified by an external measurement
technique, such as a spectrophotometer.

In principle, LC and HPLC operate similarly, but HPLC offers vastly superior speed, efficiency, sensitivity,
and ease of operation. HPLC uses tiny particles in its stationary phase and high-pressure pumps,
distinguishing it from traditional LC.

II. HPLC Instrument Configurations

HPLC systems can be found in various configurations:

============================================================
PAGE 68
============================================================
 
Modular Systems: These consist of separate, distinct components (e.g., isocratic pump, manual 
injector, variable wavelength detector, handheld controller). High-end modular systems might 
include a quaternary pump, autosampler, column thermostat, and diode array detector with 
computer control. 
 
Integrated Systems: These are "all parts in one box," offering different configurations with 
components like gradient pumps, autosamplers, column ovens, and detectors, all controlled by a 
computer. 
IV. 
Major Components of an HPLC System 
An HPLC system typically consists of five major components: 
1. Mobile Phase Reservoir and its Functions 
o Holds the mobile phase in glass bottles fitted with PTFE tubing to deliver it to the pump. 
o Importance: Liquid entering the pump should be free of air, dust, or particulate matter to 
prevent interference or damage to seals and valves. Solvents must be HPLC grade. 
o Degassing: Air bubbles can affect detector behavior, so the mobile phase needs to be 
degassed. Filtration (e.g., through a 2µm stainless steel filter) extends pump life and reduces 
column plugging. 
2. Pump: The pump's role is to force the mobile phase liquid through the liquid chromatograph at a 
specific flow rate, usually 1 to 2 mL/min. Pumps can achieve pressures in the range of 6000-9000 psi 
(400-600 bar). Pumps can deliver a constant mobile phase composition (isocratic) or an 
increasing mobile phase composition (gradient). 
Pump Module 
o Isocratic Pump: Delivers a constant mobile phase composition. The solvent must be pre-
mixed. These are typically the lowest cost pumps. They are best for simple separations and 
often used in quality control. 
o Gradient Pump: Delivers a variable mobile phase composition. These can be used for both 
isocratic and gradient mobile phases.  
o Binary gradient pumps deliver two solvents. 
o Quaternary gradient pumps can deliver up to four solvents. 
o Gradient elution, where the mobile phase solvent composition increases over time, is 
best for analyzing complex samples and is often used in method development for 
unknown mixtures. Linear gradients are most popular. Gradient elution can 
significantly reduce analysis time and improve peak sharpness compared to isocratic 
elution for complex mixtures. 
o Types:  
 Constant Pressure Pump: Flow rate determined by column resistance; not suitable 
for HPLC due to variable flow rate. 
 Constant Flow Pump: Generates a given liquid flow, with pressure depending on 
flow resistance. Compensation for flow resistance changes occurs by pressure

--- OCR TEXT ---
e Modular Systems: These consist of separate, distinct components (e.g., isocratic pump, manual
injector, variable wavelength detector, handheld controller). High-end modular systems might
include a quaternary pump, autosampler, column thermostat, and diode array detector with
computer control.

e Integrated Systems: These are "all parts in one box," offering different configurations with
components like gradient pumps, autosamplers, column ovens, and detectors, all controlled by a
computer.

Pump Module

mixed. These are typically the lowest cost pumps. They are best for simple separations and
often used in quality control.

o Gradient Pump: Delivers a variable mobile phase composition. These can be used for both

o Quaternary gradient pumps can deliver up to four solvents.

for HPLC due to variable flow rate.

PAGE 69
============================================================
adjustment. Two common types are Motor-driven syringe pump and Reciprocating 
piston pump (most used). 
3. Injector: This component introduces the liquid sample into the flow stream of the mobile phase. 
Typical sample volumes range from 5 to 20 microliters (µL). The injector must be capable of 
withstanding the high pressures of the system. An autosampler is an automated version used for 
analyzing many samples or when manual injection is impractical. 
o Manual Injector: The user manually loads the sample into the injector using a syringe and 
then turns a handle to inject it into the mobile phase. 
o Autosampler: The user loads sample vials into a tray, and the autosampler automatically 
measures the appropriate sample volume, injects it, and flushes the injector, allowing for 
unattended operation of many samples. 
4. Column: Considered the "heart of the chromatograph," the column's stationary phase separates 
the sample components using various physical and chemical parameters. The small particles within 
the column are responsible for the high backpressure observed at normal flow rates. The pump 
must exert significant force to push the mobile phase through the column, which creates this high 
pressure. 
The column is where the separation occurs, and its proper choice is critical for HPLC success. 
 Types of Columns:  
o Analytical: Internal diameters (i.d.) 1.0 - 4.6 mm; lengths 15  250 mm. 
o Preparative: i.d. > 4.6 mm; lengths 50  250 mm. 
o Capillary: i.d. 0.1 - 1.0 mm; various lengths. 
o Nano: i.d. < 0.1 mm, or < 100 µm. 
o Guard Column: A small column placed between the injector and the main analytical 
column. It extends the main column's life by preventing entry of impurities and acts 
as a saturator column to avoid dissolution of the stationary phase. It should have the 
same packing as the analytical column, typically with a ratio of 1:15 or 1:25. 
 Materials of Construction:  
o Stainless steel: Most popular, provides high pressure capabilities. 
o Glass: Primarily for biomolecules. 
o PEEK polymer: Biocompatible and chemically inert to most solvents. 
 Packing Materials: Columns are packed with small diameter porous particles, with popular 
sizes including 5-µm, 3.5-µm, and 1.8-µm. These particles usually have a chemically bonded 
phase on their surface (e.g., C18) that interacts with sample components to separate them. 
Users typically purchase pre-packed columns. 
 Mechanism of Separation: Sample components (analytes) are retained by the column packing 
material, and their retention is determined by the choice of column packing and the mobile 
phase used to push them through. Analytes are attracted to the chemically bonded phase on the 
packing material.

============================================================
PAGE 70
============================================================
5. Detector: The detector is responsible for seeing (detecting) individual molecules as they elute 
from the column. It measures the amount of these molecules, allowing for quantitative analysis of 
sample components. The detector provides an output to a recorder or computer, which generates 
the liquid chromatogram. 
o Required Characteristics of Detectors: 
 Adequate sensitivity. 
 Wide linear dynamic range (linearity). 
 Universal or selective response. 
 Predictable response, unaffected by changing conditions. 
 Short response time. 
 Low dead volume. 
 Non-destructive. 
 Cheap, reliable, and easy to use. 
Various detection principles are used to detect compounds eluting from an HPLC column: 
 
Spectroscopic Detection:  
o Ultraviolet (UV) Absorption: A UV light beam passes through a flow cell. When a 
compound absorbing this light elutes, it changes the light intensity falling on a sensor, 
generating an electrical signal. A UV spectrum can also be obtained to aid compound 
identification. Examples include Variable Wavelength Detectors and Diode Array 
Detectors. 
o Mass Spectroscopy (MS): An MS detector ionizes eluting compounds, then measures 
their mass and/or fragments them into unique pieces. The mass spectrum acts as a 
"fingerprint" and can directly identify the compound. 
 
Refractive Index (RI) Detection: This detector measures a molecule's ability to deflect light in a 
flowing mobile phase relative to a static reference mobile phase in a flow cell. The amount of 
deflection is proportional to concentration. RI detectors are considered universal detectors but 
are generally not very sensitive. 
 
Fluorescence Detection: These detectors offer higher sensitivity and selectivity compared to 
UV-Vis detectors, allowing for the quantification and identification of compounds and impurities 
at extremely low concentrations (trace levels). However, they only sense substances that 
fluoresce. 
6. Computer (Data System): This component controls all modules of the HPLC instrument. It 
processes the signal from the detector to determine the time of elution (retention time) for 
qualitative analysis and the amount of sample for quantitative analysis. 
Understanding a Liquid Chromatogram 
A liquid chromatogram is a graph showing the detector response over time after sample injection. It 
typically displays chromatographic peaks, where each peak represents a separated compound. Modern 
HPLC separations usually take 10 to 30 minutes.

Understanding a Liquid Chromatogram

A liquid chromatogram is a graph showing the detector response over time after sample injection. It
typically displays chromatographic peaks, where each peak represents a separated compound. Modern
HPLC separations usually take 10 to 30 minutes.

============================================================
PAGE 71
============================================================
V. 
Applications of HPLC 
HPLC is a versatile technique used for various purposes: 
 
Separation and Analysis of Non-volatile or Thermally-Unstable Compounds: HPLC is ideal for 
separating chemical and biological compounds that are non-volatile (e.g., pharmaceuticals like 
aspirin, ibuprofen; salts like sodium chloride; proteins like egg white; organic chemicals like 
polymers; heavy hydrocarbons like motor oil; many natural products) or thermally unstable (e.g., 
trinitrotoluene (TNT), enzymes). For volatile compounds (gases, fragrances), gas chromatography is 
generally a better technique. 
 
Qualitative Analysis (Identification): HPLC helps in the identification of individual compounds in 
a sample. The retention time (the time it takes for a compound to elute from the column) is the 
most common parameter for compound identification. Depending on the detector, identification 
can also be based on chemical structure, molecular weight, or other molecular parameters. 
 
Quantitative Analysis (Measurement of Amount): HPLC measures the amount (concentration) of 
a compound in a sample. This is done by interpreting a chromatogram, primarily by determining 
the peak height or peak area of a chromatographic peak. A linear relationship often exists between 
the height or area and the amount of sample, allowing for quantitative assessment when compared 
to samples with known concentrations. 
 
Preparation of Pure Compound(s) (Preparative Chromatography): By collecting the 
chromatographic peaks as they exit the detector and then concentrating the compound by 
removing/evaporating the solvent, pure substances can be prepared for later use in applications 
like organic synthesis, clinical studies, or toxicology studies. 
 
Trace Analysis: This involves detecting compounds of interest that are present at very low 
concentrations (usually less than 1% by weight, often parts per million (ppm) or lower). Trace 
analysis is crucial in pharmaceutical, biological, toxicology, and environmental studies because 
even minute amounts of certain substances can be harmful. High resolution separations and highly 
sensitive detectors are necessary for this application. 
VI. 
Separation Modes of HPLC 
The correct selection of the column packing and the mobile phase are the most important factors for 
successful HPLC. There are four major separation modes: 
1. Reversed-Phase Chromatography (RPC):  
o Column packing is non-polar (e.g., C18, C8, C3, and phenyl). 
o Mobile phase is water (buffer) + water-miscible organic solvent (e.g., methanol, 
acetonitrile). 
o Most popular mode, used by over 90% of chromatographers. 
o Versatile, applicable to non-polar, polar, ionizable, and ionic molecules. 
o For samples with a wide range of compounds, gradient elution is often used, starting with 
a predominantly water-based mobile phase and gradually adding organic solvent. The 
organic solvent increases solvent strength, eluting strongly retained compounds. 
2. Normal-Phase or Adsorption Chromatography:

V. Applications of HPLC

HPLC is a versatile technique used for various purposes:

VI.

Separation and Analysis of Non-volatile or Thermally-Unstable Compounds: HPLC is ideal for
separating chemical and biological compounds that are non-volatile (e.g., pharmaceuticals like
aspirin, ibuprofen; salts like sodium chloride; proteins like egg white; organic chemicals like
polymers; heavy hydrocarbons like motor oil; many natural products) or thermally unstable (e.g.,
trinitrotoluene (TNT), enzymes). For volatile compounds (gases, fragrances), gas chromatography is
generally a better technique.

Qualitative Analysis (Identification): HPLC helps in the identification of individual compounds in
asample. The retention time (the time it takes for a compound to elute from the column) is the
most common parameter for compound identification. Depending on the detector, identification
can also be based on chemical structure, molecular weight, or other molecular parameters.

Quantitative Analysis (Measurement of Amount): HPLC measures the amount (concentration) of
a compound in a sample. This is done by interpreting a chromatogram, primarily by determining
the peak height or peak area of a chromatographic peak. A linear relationship often exists between
the height or area and the amount of sample, allowing for quantitative assessment when compared
to samples with known concentrations.

Preparation of Pure Compound(s) (Preparative Chromatography): By collecting the

like organic synthesis, clinical studies, or toxicology studies.

Trace Analysis: This involves detecting compounds of interest that are present at very low
concentrations (usually less than 1% by weight, often parts per million (ppm) or lower). Trace
analysis is crucial in pharmaceutical, biological, toxicology, and environmental studies because
even minute amounts of certain substances can be harmful. High resolution separations and highly
sensitive detectors are necessary for this application.

Separation Modes of HPLC

The correct selection of the column packing and the mobile phase are the most important factors for
successful HPLC. There are four major separation modes:

1.

Reversed-Phase Chromatography (RPC):
o Column packing is non-polar (e.g., C18, C8, C3, and phenyl).

o Mobile phase is water (buffer) + water-miscible organic solvent (e.g., methanol,
acetonitrile).

o Most popular mode, used by over 90% of chromatographers.
o Versatile, applicable to non-polar, polar, ionizable, and ionic molecules.

o Forsamples with a wide range of compounds, gradient elution is often used, starting with
a predominantly water-based mobile phase and gradually adding organic solvent. The
organic solvent increases solvent strength, eluting strongly retained compounds.

2. Normal-Phase or Adsorption Chromatography:

============================================================
PAGE 72
============================================================
o Column packing is polar (e.g., silica gel, cyanopropyl-bonded, amino-bonded). 
o Mobile phase is non-polar (e.g., hexane, iso-octane, methylene chloride, ethyl acetate). 
o Used less than 10% of the time. 
o Useful for water-sensitive compounds, geometric isomers, cis-trans isomers, class 
separations, and chiral compounds. 
3. Ion Exchange Chromatography:  
o Column packing contains ionic groups (e.g., sulfonic, tetraalkylammonium). 
o Mobile phase is an aqueous buffer (e.g., phosphate, formate). 
o Used by about 20% of liquid chromatographers. 
o Well-suited for the separation of inorganic and organic anions and cations in aqueous 
solution, as well as ionic dyes, amino acids, and proteins. 
4. Size Exclusion Chromatography (SEC):  
o No interaction between sample compounds and column packing material. 
o Molecules separate based on their size relative to the pores of a porous medium. 
o Large molecules elute first because they cannot enter the pores, while smaller molecules 
enter the pores and elute later. 
o Used by 10-15% of chromatographers, mainly for polymer characterization and proteins. 
o Has two modes: non-aqueous SEC (Gel Permeation Chromatography - GPC) and aqueous 
SEC (Gel Filtration Chromatography - GFC). 
VII. 
Temperature Control in HPLC 
Temperature control is essential in HPLC for several reasons: 
 
Reproducibility: Retention times are temperature-dependent, so consistent temperature ensures 
that peaks correspond to specific compounds and that peak areas/heights are reproducible. 
 
Solubility: Some compounds may have low solubility in the mobile phase, and temperature control 
can prevent precipitation. 
 
Stability: Certain compounds, especially biomolecules like enzymes or proteins, may not be stable 
at room temperature or higher, requiring lower temperatures (e.g., down to 4°C). 
Temperature control can be achieved using an oven, a heater block, or a water bath.

Gas Chromatography (GC) 
Gas Chromatography (GC) is an analytical technique used primarily for the separation of chemical 
mixtures. It is a highly improved form of column liquid chromatography and involves the distribution of the 
sample mixture between two phases: a stationary phase and a mobile phase. The technique was initially 
conceived by Russian botanist Mikhail Tswett for separating compounds. 
I. 
Principle of Gas Chromatography (GC) 
The fundamental principle of GC is polarity. The separation of compounds occurs as they are carried by a 
mobile phase (carrier gas) through a column (stationary phase). Compounds separate based on their 
differential interaction (adsorption/desorption) with the stationary phase and their volatility. Substances 
less attracted to the solid stationary phase or more soluble in the liquid stationary phase move faster. This 
results in each component having a unique retention time  the time between sample injection and its 
detection. Only thermo-stable or heat-stable compounds can be separated using GC, as it operates at 
high temperatures, up to 300°C. 
II. 
Instrumentation of GC 
A typical GC system comprises several primary components: 
 
Carrier Gas System: 
o The mobile phase in GC is a gas, with common choices being Hydrogen, Nitrogen, or 
Helium.  
 
Hydrogen provides excellent results but poses a fire hazard and can react with 
unsaturated compounds (reduction/hydrogenation). 
 
Nitrogen is economical but provides less excellent results and is not considered a 
very good carrier gas. 
 
Helium is inert and yields excellent results, though it is expensive.

e The mobile phase does not vary and does not require disposal (unlike some GC setups) and is
cheaper compared to organic solvents used in HPLC. (Note: This statement appears under GC
strengths but again, compares the mobile phase cost to HPLC, implying an advantage of GC, but it
directly speaks about the nature of the mobile phase in both, which is relevant to HPLC's operation).

techniques like GC and CE, highlighting relative disadvantages. For instance, the mobile phase cost
is mentioned in comparison to GC.

Gas Chromatography (GC)

Gas Chromatography (GC) is an analytical technique used primarily for the separation of chemical
mixtures. It is a highly improved form of column liquid chromatography and involves the distribution of the
sample mixture between two phases: a stationary phase and a mobile phase. The technique was initially
conceived by Russian botanist Mikhail Tswett for separating compounds.

I. Principle of Gas Chromatography (GC)

The fundamental principle of GC is polarity. The separation of compounds occurs as they are carried by a
mobile phase (carrier gas) through a column (stationary phase). Compounds separate based on their
differential interaction (adsorption/desorption) with the stationary phase and their volatility. Substances
less attracted to the solid stationary phase or more soluble in the liquid stationary phase move faster. This
results in each component having a unique retention time — the time between sample injection and its
detection. Only thermo-stable or heat-stable compounds can be separated using GC, as it operates at
high temperatures, up to 300°C.

If. Instrumentation of GC
Atypical GC system comprises several primary components:
e Carrier Gas System:

o The mobile phase in GC is a gas, with common choices being Hydrogen, Nitrogen, or
Helium.

= Hydrogen provides excellent results but poses a fire hazard and can react with
unsaturated compounds (reduction/hydrogenation).

» Nitrogen is economical but provides less excellent results and is not considered a
very good carrier gas.

= Helium is inert and yields excellent results, though it is expensive.

============================================================
PAGE 74
============================================================
o High purity carrier gas (e.g., 99.9995% UHP grade) is crucial, and O2 & moisture traps are 
used to prevent column damage, especially when heated above 70°C. The selection of carrier 
gas is based on its purity. 
 
Sample Introduction System: 
o A known volume of liquid mixture (typically 0.5-2 µl) is injected into the head of the column. 
o For capillary GC, it is recommended to fill the syringe with solvent before the sample. Sample 
preparation is crucial, and samples should ideally be dissolved in the same solvent or 
mixture as the mobile phase. Aqueous solutions and salts generally cannot be injected into 
the instrument. 
 
Column (Stationary Phase): 
o This is the most critical component where separation occurs. The column is typically in a 
coil form, placed inside a specially controlled oven. 
o The inner layer is usually silica, which is a weakly polar compound. 
o Types of Columns: Metal or glass tubing (stainless steel) and glass columns are used. 
o Types of GC (based on stationary phase):  
 
Gas-Solid Chromatography (GSC): The mobile phase is gas, and the stationary 
phase is solid silica. 
 
Gas-Liquid Chromatography (GLC): The mobile phase is gas, and an additional 
liquid layer (e.g., Polyethylene glycol - polar, or Polysiloxene - non-polar) coats the 
silica. 
o Temperature: The GC column is placed in an oven with a specially controlled temperature.  
 
An isothermal process maintains a constant temperature throughout the process. 
 
A temperature program involves controlled changes in initial, rate of increase, and 
final temperatures. 
 
Detection System/GC Detectors: 
o Detectors identify components qualitatively and quantitatively by calculating their retention 
time. 
o An ideal GC detector should possess adequate sensitivity, good stability and 
reproducibility, a linear response over several orders of magnitude, a wide temperature 
range, a short response time independent of flow rate, high reliability, ease of use, and 
ideally be non-destructive. 
o Most commonly used detectors include:  
 
Flame Ionization Detector (FID): Primarily used for organic/hydrocarbon 
compounds. It relies on the pyrolysis of carbon-containing compounds in a 
hydrogen/air flame, generating a current. FID is a universal detector for organics and 
does not respond to common inorganic compounds or mobile phase impurities. It 
offers low detection limits (picograms per second) but is a destructive detector.

Detectors identify components qualitatively and quantitatively by calculating their retention

An ideal GC detector should possess adequate sensitivity, good stability and
reproducibility, a linear response over several orders of magnitude, a wide temperature
range, a short response time independent of flow rate, high reliability, ease of use, and
ideally be non-destructive.

Most commonly used detectors include:

= Flame lonization Detector (FID): Primarily used for organic/hydrocarbon
compounds. It relies on the pyrolysis of carbon-containing compounds ina
hydrogen/air flame, generating a current. FID is a universal detector for organics and
does not respond to common inorganic compounds or mobile phase impurities. It
offers low detection limits (picograms per second) but is a destructive detector.

============================================================
PAGE 75
============================================================
 
Thermal Conductivity Detector (TCD): Relies on the thermal conductivity of matter 
passing around a tungsten-rhenium filament. When analyte molecules elute, mixed 
with carrier gas, the thermal conductivity decreases, causing a detector response. It 
is universal and nondestructive but has low sensitivity. 
 
Electron Capture Detector (ECD): Used for molecules containing electronegative 
elements (e.g., halogens, carbonyl, nitriles, nitro groups). 
 
Atomic Emission Detector (AED): An element-selective detector where microwaves 
induce plasma to decompose analytes, generating atomic emission spectra. 
 
Mass Spectrometer (MS): Often coupled with GC, forming GC-MS. It acts as the 
detector, identifying and quantifying chemicals based on their structure and 
fragmentation pattern. 
 
Computer ChemStation/Integrator: Modern chromatographic instruments are often equipped 
with computers or digital electronic integrators for precise estimation of peak areas and data 
acquisition. 
III. 
Working of GC 
The working process of GC involves the following steps: 
1. Sample Injection: A known volume of liquid sample is injected into the GC system, where it is 
converted into vapor if it's a liquid. 
2. Chromatographic Separation: A carrier gas (mobile phase) carries the volatilized sample 
components through the heated column (stationary phase). Components separate based on their 
differential interaction with the stationary phase and their volatility, leading to different retention 
times. 
3. Detection: As separated components elute from the column, the detector measures them, 
producing signals in the form of peaks. 
4. Data Acquisition: The results are processed and displayed, often by a computer. 
IV. 
Data Analysis 
GC enables both qualitative and quantitative analysis. 
 
Qualitative Analysis: The number of peaks corresponds to the number of components in the 
mixture. If the retention time of a mixture component is similar to a reference compound, it 
suggests a match. 
 
Quantitative Analysis:  
o Peak height can indicate the quality of the drug. 
o Peak area provides information about the quantity of the drug. Peak areas are generally 
preferred for quantitation as they are independent of broadening effects. Percent 
composition can be calculated by comparing the area under each peak to the total area of 
all peaks.

II,

passing around a tungsten-rhenium filament. When analyte molecules elute, mixed
with carrier gas, the thermal conductivity decreases, causing a detector response. It
is universal and nondestructive but has low sensitivity.

V. 
Comparison of GC with Other Techniques 
 
With Fractional Distillation (FD): Both GC and FD work based on boiling point, but FD is used on a 
large (industrial) scale, while GC is for small (lab) scale. 
 
With TLC and HPLC:  
o GC uses high temperatures (up to 300°C), making it suitable only for thermo-stable 
compounds. It is expensive but provides very precise and accurate results. 
o Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC) operates at room temperature, is very economical, but 
its results are not as accurate or precise. 
o High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) is also expensive and uses low 
temperatures (25 30°C), yielding very accurate and precise results. 
VI. 
Strengths of GC 
GC offers several significant advantages: 
 
High Separating Power: When used with capillary columns, it has much greater separating power 
than HPLC. 
 
Quantitative Accuracy and Precision: Capable of high quantitative accuracy and precision, 
especially with an internal standard. 
 
Automation: Readily automated, from sample introduction to separation. 
 
Versatility: Can determine compounds lacking chromophores. 
 
Cost-Effective Mobile Phase: The mobile phase (gas) does not vary, does not require disposal, and 
is generally cheaper than organic solvents used in HPLC. 
VII. 
Limitations of GC 
Despite its advantages, GC has certain limitations: 
 
Sample Volatility and Thermal Stability: Only thermally stable and volatile compounds can be 
analyzed. Non-volatile or thermally unstable compounds cannot pass the GC separation. 
 
Sample Preparation: Samples may require derivatization to convert them into a volatile form, 
adding an extra step and potential interferents. Aqueous solutions and salts generally cannot be 
injected into the instrument. 
 
Quantitative Sample Introduction: More difficult due to the small volumes of sample injected. 
 
Chemical Range Optimization: The instrument needs to be optimized for the specific types of 
chemicals of concern. An instrument configured for volatile chemicals may miss very high or very 
low molecular weight chemicals. 
VIII. 
Applications of GC 
GC has wide-ranging applications: 
 
Pharmaceutical Analysis: Analysis of drugs in biological fluids and tissues, determination of 
nicotine levels, separation, characterization, and quantification of benzoic and phenolic 
antioxidants. It is also used for quality control.

 
Forensic Sciences: Identification and quantification in toxicology cases, crime scene evidence,

 
Environmental Analysis: Air sample quality control (Air QC). 
 
Food Industry: Determination of rancidity in dairy products. 


Houttuynia cordata injection. 
IX. 
Gas Chromatography-Mass Spectrometry (GC-MS) 
GC-MS is a technique where Mass Spectrometry is coupled with GC. The combination was first reported in 
1958 and became commercially available in 1967. 
 
Purpose of Coupling: The primary purpose is to provide confirmatory identification with minimal 
effort. While GC alone can provide confirmatory analysis, it often requires analysis on two different 
columns; coupling with MS significantly enhances specificity and identification. 
 
Working: The GC portion separates the chemical mixture into pulses of pure chemicals, and the MS 
identifies and quantifies these chemicals based on their structure. 
 
Limitations of GC-MS: These include critical instrument maintenance due to the high vacuum 
required by the MS component, the need for optimization for specific chemical ranges (e.g., 
volatile vs. high molecular weight chemicals), and potential impure MS feed from incomplete GC 
separation, leading to background "noise". Interpretation of mass spectra can also be complex, 
requiring correlation of computer calculations with system conditions. GC-MS may not be sufficient 
for comprehensive analysis of all plant metabolites, as it's limited to volatile compounds. 
Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC) 
Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC) is a widely used analytical method for separating chemical mixtures. It is 
also known as open column chromatography, drop chromatography, strip chromatography, spread layer 
chromatography, and surface chromatography. 
I. 
Introduction to Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC) 
TLC is a method of analysis where the stationary phase, which is a finely divided solid, is spread as a thin 
layer on a rigid supporting plate. The mobile phase, a liquid, then migrates across the surface of this plate. 
The mobile phase is drawn by capillary action. TLC is a very popular method and is often used similarly to 
paper chromatography. It can be used for qualitative, quantitative, and preparative purposes. 
II. 
Characteristics 
 
The mobile phase is a liquid, which flows past a thin layer of powder on a solid support. 
 
Substances that are less attracted to the solid stationary phase or more soluble in the liquid 
mobile phase move faster. 
 
This differential movement causes substances to move further up the plate by the time the process 
is stopped, resulting in a larger Retention Factor (Rf) value.

--- OCR TEXT ---

arson investigation, and paint-chip analysis.

e Environmental Analysis: Air sample quality control (Air QC).
¢ Food Industry: Determination of rancidity in dairy products.

e General Chemical Analysis: Separation of geometrical isomers, and it is easy to automate.
Metabolite profiling for plant functional genomics, and identification and quality assessment of
Houttuynia cordata injection.

for comprehensive analysis of all plant metabolites, as it's limited to volatile compounds.

Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC)

Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC) is a widely used analytical method for separating chemical mixtures. It is
also known as open column chromatography, drop chromatography, strip chromatography, spread layer
chromatography, and surface chromatography.

I. Introduction to Thin Layer Chromatography (TLC)

TLC is a method of analysis where the stationary phase, which is a finely divided solid, is spread as a thin
layer on arigid supporting plate. The mobile phase, a liquid, then migrates across the surface of this plate.
The mobile phase is drawn by capillary action. TLC is a very popular method and is often used similarly to
paper chromatography. It can be used for qualitative, quantitative, and preparative purposes.

If. Characteristics
e The mobile phase is a liquid, which flows past a thin layer of powder on a solid support.

e Substances that are less attracted to the solid stationary phase or more soluble in the liquid
mobile phase move faster.

e This differential movement causes substances to move further up the plate by the time the process
is stopped, resulting in a larger Retention Factor (Rf) value.

============================================================
PAGE 78
============================================================
III. 
Principle of TLC 
The primary principle involved in TLC is adsorption. 
1. One or more compounds are spotted onto a thin layer of adsorbent coated on a chromatographic 
plate. 
2. The mobile phase solvent flows through the layer due to capillary action. 
3. Compounds with a greater affinity for the stationary phase move at a slower rate, while 
compounds with lesser affinity move faster. 
4. The identification of compounds is achieved by calculating the Rf value for each compound.  
o Rf (Retention Factor) is defined as the ratio of the distance travelled by the components 
to the distance travelled by the solvent. 
o Its value ranges from 0 to 1, and it has no units. 
IV. 
Requirements for TLC Stationary Phase (Instrumentation) 
The requirements for a TLC setup include: 
 
TLC Plates: These are the rigid supports on which the stationary phase is coated. 
 
Preparation & Activation of TLC Plates: Plates need to be prepared (if not commercially 
purchased) and often activated (e.g., by heating to remove moisture). 
 
Application of Sample: A small amount of the sample mixture is applied as a spot onto the plate. 
 
Development Tank: A chamber (e.g., a glass tank) where the chromatographic development takes 
place. 
 
Mobile Phase: The solvent system that moves through the stationary phase. 
 
Development Techniques: The specific method used to run the chromatogram (e.g., ascending, 
descending). 
 
Detecting & Visualizing Agents: Chemicals or UV light used to make the separated compounds 
visible. 
V. 
Nature of the Layer (Stationary Phase) 
 
Commonly used adsorbents include silica gel, alumina, diatomaceous earth, and powdered 
cellulose. 
 
Silica gel is acidic and has a high capacity, making it useful for both adsorption and partition 
chromatography. 
 
Alumina is basic and primarily used for adsorption chromatography. 
 
Diatomaceous earth is nearly neutral and used for partition chromatography. 
 
The thickness of the layer typically varies from 0.15-2.00 mm. 
 
TLC plates can be prepared in the lab or, more conveniently and reproducibly, purchased 
commercially.

--- OCR TEXT ---
Ill. Principle of TLC
The primary principle involved in TLC is adsorption.

1. One or more compounds are spotted onto a thin layer of adsorbent coated on a chromatographic
plate.

2. The mobile phase solvent flows through the layer due to capillary action.

3. Compounds with a greater affinity for the stationary phase move at a slower rate, while
compounds with lesser affinity move faster.

4. The identification of compounds is achieved by calculating the Rf value for each compound.

o Rf (Retention Factor) is defined as the ratio of the distance travelled by the components
to the distance travelled by the solvent.

o Its value ranges from 0 to 1, and it has no units.
IV. Requirements for TLC Stationary Phase (Instrumentation)
The requirements for a TLC setup include:
e TLC Plates: These are the rigid supports on which the stationary phase is coated.

e Preparation & Activation of TLC Plates: Plates need to be prepared (if not commercially
purchased) and often activated (e.g., by heating to remove moisture).

e Application of Sample: A small amount of the sample mixture is applied as a spot onto the plate.

e¢ Development Tank: A chamber (e.g., a glass tank) where the chromatographic development takes
place.

e Mobile Phase: The solvent system that moves through the stationary phase.

e¢ Development Techniques: The specific method used to run the chromatogram (e.g., ascending,
descending).

¢ Detecting & Visualizing Agents: Chemicals or UV light used to make the separated compounds
visible.

V. Nature of the Layer (Stationary Phase)

e¢ Commonly used adsorbents include silica gel, alumina, diatomaceous earth, and powdered
cellulose.

e Silica gel is acidic and has a high capacity, making it useful for both adsorption and partition
chromatography.

e Alumina is basic and primarily used for adsorption chromatography.
e Diatomaceous earth is nearly neutral and used for partition chromatography.
e The thickness of the layer typically varies from 0.15-2.00 mm.

e TLCplates can be prepared in the lab or, more conveniently and reproducibly, purchased
commercially.

============================================================
PAGE 79
============================================================
VI. 
Procedure of Development 
The development procedure in TLC is similar to paper chromatography. 
 
Mostly, it's done by the ascending method or horizontal method. 
 
Isocratic development means constant elution conditions throughout the experiment. 
 
Gradient development involves intentional changes in elution conditions during the process, which 
can be achieved by changing mobile phase composition, temperature, or pressure. 
VII. 
Detection 
If the separated spots are not colored and cannot be seen by the naked eye, specific methods are used: 
 
UV lamp: For compounds that are UV-active (most aromatics fall into this category). 
 
Chemical derivatization: If compounds are not UV-active, they can be made visible by spraying 
with a chemical reagent that reacts with the compounds to produce a colored product. Once spots 
are visualized, they are often encircled with a pencil. 
 
Rf values are then calculated for each spot to help identify unknown compounds, ensuring the 
same mobile phase is used as Rf values vary with different mobile phases. 
VIII. 
Other Special Techniques 
 
Preparative TLC:  
o The sample mixture is applied as a thin, even layer horizontally, rather than as dots. 
o Upon development, compounds separate into horizontal bands. 
o Each band is scraped off, extracted with a suitable solvent, and filtered to isolate the 
material. 
o It is more time and cost-efficient than column chromatography for small-scale separations. 
o Best used for colored compounds or those visible under UV light. 
 
2-D TLC:  
o The plate is developed in one direction. 
o Then, the plate is rotated by 90 degrees. 
o And developed again using a different mobile phase. 
 
Reversed Phase TLC:  
o Plates are coated with a non-polar adsorbent, and the mobile phase used is polar. 
 
Modern TLC:  
o Features automation in sample application, development, detection, and quantification. 
IX. 
Advantages of TLC 
TLC offers several benefits: 
 
It is cheap and easy to perform. 
 
It is more versatile than paper chromatography.

--- OCR TEXT ---

 
With High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC): HPLC is expensive and uses low 
temperatures (25 30°C), yielding very accurate and precise results. TLC is economical and 
performed at room temperature with less accurate results. TLC also has a flat flow, compared to the 
pumped parabolic flow of HPLC. TLC has a greater peak capacity when compared to HPLC, using 
millions of theoretical plates. However, HPLC is more thoroughly developed with a wider variety of 
mobile and stationary phases, and more complex instrumentation. 
Capillary Electrophoresis (CE) 
Capillary Electrophoresis (CE) is an analytical technique used for separating ions based on their 
electrophoretic mobility with the application of an electric voltage. 
I. 
Introduction  
Capillary Electrophoresis separates ions by leveraging their electrophoretic mobility, which is influenced 
by the molecule's charge, the solution's viscosity, and the atom's radius. Only ions are affected by the 
electric field, while neutral species remain unaffected. If two ions are of the same size, the one with a 
greater charge will migrate faster. For ions with the same charge, smaller particles experience less friction 
and thus have a faster overall migration rate. CE is predominantly used due to its speed and high-
resolution separation capabilities, and it offers a wide range of detection methods. 
II. 
Instrumental Setup 
A typical Capillary Electrophoresis system comprises several key components: 
 
High-voltage power supply 
 
Sample introduction system 
 
Capillary tube 
 
Detector 
 
Output device 
Some instruments also incorporate a temperature control device to ensure reproducible results. This is 
important because the separation of the sample depends on electrophoretic mobility, and the viscosity of 
the solutions decreases as the column temperature rises. 
In the setup: 
 
Each side of the high-voltage power supply connects to an electrode.

--- OCR TEXT ---
e  Itis faster and more reproducible.

e Often used for complex sample separation and visualization.

e Modern TLC is complementary to HPTLC (High-Performance Thin Layer Chromatography).
X. Comparison with other Techniques

e With Gas Chromatography (GC): TLC is carried out at room temperature, is very economical, but
its results are not as accurate or precise as GC. GC, in contrast, uses high temperatures (up to
300°C), making it suitable only for thermo-stable compounds, is expensive, but provides very precise
and accurate results.

e With High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC): HPLC is expensive and uses low
temperatures (25-30°C), yielding very accurate and precise results. TLC is economical and
performed at room temperature with less accurate results. TLC also has a flat flow, compared to the
pumped parabolic flow of HPLC. TLC has a greater peak capacity when compared to HPLC, using
millions of theoretical plates. However, HPLC is more thoroughly developed with a wider variety of
mobile and stationary phases, and more complex instrumentation.

Capillary Electrophoresis (CE)

Capillary Electrophoresis (CE) is an analytical technique used for separating ions based on their
electrophoretic mobility with the application of an electric voltage.

I. Introduction

Capillary Electrophoresis separates ions by leveraging their electrophoretic mobility, which is influenced
by the molecule's charge, the solution's viscosity, and the atom's radius. Only ions are affected by the
electric field, while neutral species remain unaffected. If two ions are of the same size, the one witha
greater charge will migrate faster. For ions with the same charge, smaller particles experience less friction
and thus have a faster overall migration rate. CE is predominantly used due to its speed and high-
resolution separation capabilities, and it offers a wide range of detection methods.

If. Instrumental Setup
Atypical Capillary Electrophoresis system comprises several key components:
e High-voltage power supply
e Sample introduction system

III. 
Capillary Electroseparation Methods 
There are six main types of capillary electroseparation methods: 
1. Capillary Zone Electrophoresis (CZE): 
o Also known as free solution capillary electrophoresis, CZE is the most commonly used 
technique. 
o It quickly and easily separates mixtures into individual components based on differences in 
electrophoretic mobility. 
o The mobility is directly proportional to the molecule's charge and inversely proportional to 
the solvent's viscosity and the atom's radius. 
o The velocity of ion movement is directly proportional to electrophoretic mobility and the 
electric field magnitude. 
o Fused silica capillaries have silanol groups that ionize in buffer, attracting cations to form a 
stationary and a diffuse cation layer. An applied electric field causes the diffuse layer to 
migrate towards the negatively charged cathode, creating an electroosmotic flow (EOF) 
that carries bulk solvent. 
o Cations with the largest charge-to-mass ratios separate first, followed by cations with 
reduced ratios, neutral species, anions with smaller ratios, and finally anions with greater 
ratios. 
o EOF velocity can be adjusted by changing pH, solvent viscosity, ionic strength, voltage, and 
the buffer's dielectric constant. 
2. Capillary Gel Electrophoresis (CGE): 
o CGE separates based on differences in solute size as particles migrate through a gel. 
o Gels are beneficial because they minimize solute diffusion (which causes zone broadening), 
prevent capillary walls from absorbing solutes, and limit heat transfer by slowing down 
molecules. 
o A common gel apparatus for protein separation is capillary SDS-PAGE, which is highly 
sensitive and requires only small sample amounts. 
3. Capillary Electrochromatography (CEC): 
o This technique employs a packed column similar to traditional chromatography.

--- OCR TEXT ---
e The capillary tube is dipped into vials containing an electrode and an electrolytic solution (or
aqueous buffer) on each side.

e Before sample introduction, the capillary must be flushed with the chosen buffer solution.

e Asmall window near the cathodic end of the capillary typically allows UV-VIS light to pass through
the analyte to measure absorbance.

e¢ Aphotomultiplier tube is also connected at the cathodic end, enabling the construction of a mass
spectrum which provides information about the mass-to-charge ratio of the ionic species.

III. Capillary Electroseparation Methods

There are six main types of capillary electroseparation methods:

1. Capillary Zone Electrophoresis (CZE):

fe}

Also known as free solution capillary electrophoresis, CZE is the most commonly used
technique.

It quickly and easily separates mixtures into individual components based on differences in
electrophoretic mobility.

The mobility is directly proportional to the molecule's charge and inversely proportional to
the solvent's viscosity and the atom's radius.

The velocity of ion movement is directly proportional to electrophoretic mobility and the
electric field magnitude.

Fused silica capillaries have silanol groups that ionize in buffer, attracting cations to forma
stationary and a diffuse cation layer. An applied electric field causes the diffuse layer to
migrate towards the negatively charged cathode, creating an electroosmotic flow (EOF)
that carries bulk solvent.

Cations with the largest charge-to-mass ratios separate first, followed by cations with
reduced ratios, neutral species, anions with smaller ratios, and finally anions with greater
ratios.

EOF velocity can be adjusted by changing pH, solvent viscosity, ionic strength, voltage, and
the buffer's dielectric constant.

2. Capillary Gel Electrophoresis (CGE):

fe}

fe}

CGE separates based on differences in solute size as particles migrate through a gel.

Gels are beneficial because they minimize solute diffusion (which causes zone broadening),
prevent capillary walls from absorbing solutes, and limit heat transfer by slowing down
molecules.

Acommon gel apparatus for protein separation is capillary SDS-PAGE, which is highly
sensitive and requires only small sample amounts.

3. Capillary Electrochromatography (CEC):

fe}

This technique employs a packed column similar to traditional chromatography.

============================================================
PAGE 82
============================================================
o The mobile liquid flows over the silica wall and particles, and an electroosmosis flow occurs 
due to charges on the stationary surface. 
o Like CZE, CEC exhibits a plug-type flow (as opposed to pumped parabolic flow), which helps 
reduce band broadening. 
4. Micellar Electrokinetic Capillary Chromatography (MEKC): 
o MEKC is a separation technique based on solute partitioning between micelles and the 
solvent. 
o Micelles are aggregates of surfactant molecules that form above a critical micelle 
concentration, having polar, negatively charged surfaces that are attracted to the anode. 
o Due to electroosmotic flow towards the cathode, micelles are pulled to the cathode but at a 
slower rate. 
o Hydrophobic molecules spend more time in the micelle, while hydrophilic molecules migrate 
faster through the solvent. 
o The presence of micelles allows the separation of neutral molecules, which would otherwise 
migrate with the electroosmotic flow without separation. 
o Factors affecting EOF in MEKC include pH, surfactant concentration, additives, and capillary 
wall polymer coatings. 
5. Capillary Isoelectric Focusing (CIEF): 
o CIEF is commonly used to separate peptides and proteins, which are zwitterionic 
compounds (containing both positive and negative charges). 
o The charge of these molecules depends on their functional groups and the surrounding pH. 
Each molecule has a specific isoelectric point (pI) where it carries no net charge. 
o During CIEF, the capillary is filled with the sample, and typically no EOF is used (often 
removed by coated capillaries). When voltage is applied, ions migrate to a region where their 
charge becomes neutral (pH=pI). 
o The anodic end is in acidic solution (low pH), and the cathodic end in basic solution (high 
pH). Compounds with equal pIs are "focused" into sharp segments, allowing for distinct 
detection. 
6. Capillary Isotachophoresis (CITP): 
o CITP is unique as it is the only method used in a discontinuous system. 
o The analyte migrates in consecutive zones, and each zone's length can be measured to 
determine the sample's quantity. 
IV. 
Capillary Electrophoresis versus HPLC 
CE and High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) are both analytical separation techniques, but 
they have distinct differences: 
Advantages of CE over HPLC:

IV. Capillary Electrophoresis versus HPLC

CE and High-Performance Liquid Chromatography (HPLC) are both analytical separation techniques, but
they have distinct differences:

Advantages of CE over HPLC:

============================================================
PAGE 83
============================================================
 
CE has a flat flow profile, compared to HPLC's pumped parabolic flow, which results in narrower 
peaks and better resolution. 
 
CE offers a greater peak capacity and utilizes millions of theoretical plates. 
 
CE instruments are generally simpler for the operator compared to HPLC's more complex 
instrumentation. 
 
CE is limited to thin capillaries, while HPLC has a wide variety of column lengths and packing 
materials. 
Similarities: 
 
Both techniques use similar modes of detection. 
 
They can be used complementarily to one another. 
Advantages of HPLC over CE: 
 
HPLC is more thoroughly developed, with a wider range of mobile and stationary phases that can be 
implemented. 
V. 
Applications of Capillary Electrophoresis 
CE has largely replaced gel separation techniques in genetic analysis applications due to significant 
improvements in workflow speed, throughput, and ease of use. 
Specific advantages in genetic analysis include: 
 
Ease of use (no gels to pour). 
 
Reusability of the polymer matrix (product-dependent). 
 
Fast separation times. 
 
Better resolution (single base pair). 
 
Automated reads (optical sequence reading). 
 
Higher throughput (multiple capillaries can be used simultaneously). 
 
Complete automation available on automatic genetic analysis systems. 
Polarography 
Polarography, also referred to as polarographic analysis or voltammetry, is an electrochemical method of 
analyzing solutions of reducible or oxidizable substances. 
I. 
Introduction and Principle 
Polarography is an analytical technique based on the measurement of current flow resulting from the 
electrolysis of a solution at a polarizable microelectrode as a function of applied voltage. The electric 
potential (voltage) is varied in a regular manner between two sets of electrodes (indicator and reference), 
while the current is monitored. The shape of the resulting curve, called a polarogram, depends on the 
analytical method chosen, the type of indicator electrode, and the applied potential ramp. 
The fundamental process involves placing the solution to be analyzed in a glass cell with two electrodes. As 
the voltage applied to the cell increases, a very small residual current flows until the substance under assay 
undergoes reduction or oxidation. At this point, the current increases, initially gradually and then almost

Polarography

Polarography, also referred to as polarographic analysis or voltammetry, is an electrochemical method of
analyzing solutions of reducible or oxidizable substances.

I. Introduction and Principle

Polarography is an analytical technique based on the measurement of current flow resulting from the
electrolysis of a solution at a polarizable microelectrode as a function of applied voltage. The electric
potential (voltage) is varied in a regular manner between two sets of electrodes (indicator and reference),
while the current is monitored. The shape of the resulting curve, called a polarogram, depends on the
analytical method chosen, the type of indicator electrode, and the applied potential ramp.

The fundamental process involves placing the solution to be analyzed in a glass cell with two electrodes. As
the voltage applied to the cell increases, a very small residual current flows until the substance under assay
undergoes reduction or oxidation. At this point, the current increases, initially gradually and then almost

============================================================
PAGE 84
============================================================
linearly with voltage, forming the characteristic polarograms. Polarography allows for measurements of 
concentrations at submilimolar levels, typically ranging from 10⁻² molar to 10⁻  molar. 
II. 
Electrodes Used 
In polarography, two electrodes are typically used in the solution: 
 
One electrode has a fixed potential (voltage) and acts as a reference microelectrode; the 
Saturated Calomel Electrode (SCE) is commonly used for this purpose. 
 
The other is an indicator electrode, which is primarily the Dropping Mercury Electrode (DME) for 
reduction reactions. In some cases, a platinum electrode (stationary or rotatory) may be used for 
oxidation reactions, producing cathodic and anodic waves respectively. 
 
Dropping Mercury Electrode (DME) 
o The Dropping Mercury Electrode (DME) is a central component in polarography and is often 
referred to as its "hero". 
Construction of DME 
o The DME consists of a flow of mercury passing through an insulating capillary, which 
produces a droplet that grows and then detaches. The capillary tube is typically 10-15 cm 
long with an internal diameter of about 0.05 mm. Each droplet grows to approximately 0.5 
mm in diameter before releasing, and the drop time is usually maintained between 1 to 5 
seconds. After release, the mercury collects at the bottom of the cell. 
Advantages of DME 
o Mercury is advantageous for several reasons: 
o Its surface is reproducible, smooth, and continuously renewed. 
o Mercury forms soluble amalgams with many metals, which lowers their reduction 
potentials. 
o The high overpotential of hydrogen on mercury allows for the deposition of ions that are 
difficult to reduce in aqueous solutions, such as alkali metal ions. 
o The surface area can be calculated from the weight of the drops. 
o The diffusion current reaches a steady value. 
Limitations of DME 
o Despite its advantages, the DME has limitations: 
o Mercury is costly and poisonous. 
o The current passing through the cell increases as the drop grows and decreases as it 
detaches, which can add error. 
o The DME generates some current, such as residual current, migration current, and kinetic 
current, which can introduce errors in current measurement. 
o Mercury is oxidized, restricting the electrode's use as an anode.

--- OCR TEXT ---
linearly with voltage, forming the characteristic polarograms. Polarography allows for measurements of
concentrations at submilimolar levels, typically ranging from 10 @ molar to 10 ~molar.

If. Electrodes Used
In polarography, two electrodes are typically used in the solution:

e Oneelectrode has a fixed potential (voltage) and acts as a reference microelectrode; the
Saturated Calomel Electrode (SCE) is commonly used for this purpose.

e The other is an indicator electrode, which is primarily the Dropping Mercury Electrode (DME) for

e Dropping Mercury Electrode (DME)

o The Dropping Mercury Electrode (DME) is a central component in polarography and is often
referred to asits "hero".

Construction of DME

o The DME consists of a flow of mercury passing through an insulating capillary, which
produces a droplet that grows and then detaches. The capillary tube is typically 10-15 cm
long with an internal diameter of about 0.05 mm. Each droplet grows to approximately 0.5
mm in diameter before releasing, and the drop time is usually maintained between 1 to 5

Advantages of DME
o Mercury is advantageous for several reasons:
o Its surface is reproducible, smooth, and continuously renewed.

o Mercury forms soluble amalgams with many metals, which lowers their reduction
potentials.

o The high overpotential of hydrogen on mercury allows for the deposition of ions that are
difficult to reduce in aqueous solutions, such as alkali metal ions.

o The surface area can be calculated from the weight of the drops.
o The diffusion current reaches a steady value.

Limitations of DME

o Despite its advantages, the DME has limitations:

o Mercury is costly and poisonous.

o Thecurrent passing through the cell increases as the drop grows and decreases as it
detaches, which can add error.

o The DME generates some current, such as residual current, migration current, and kinetic
current, which can introduce errors in current measurement.

o Mercury is oxidized, restricting the electrode's use as an anode.

============================================================
PAGE 85
============================================================
III. 
Characteristic Features of Polarography 
Key features that define a polarographic analysis include: 
 
Applied Voltage: This typically varies from 0 to 2.5 volts. 
 
Applied Current: The current value usually ranges from 0.12 to 100 microampere. 
 
Half-wave Potential: This is the oxidation or reduction potential at the current midpoint of a 
polarographic curve. It is a characteristic property of an electrolyzable species, independent of 
its concentration, and related to the standard electrode potential (E°) value. The half-wave 
potential can be shifted by varying the pH of the solution or by adding a complexing agent, which 
can help resolve compounds with overlapping half-wave potentials. For instance, Tin (Sn) and 
Antimony (Sb) have different half-wave potentials in acid versus alkaline solutions, allowing for their 
determination in mixtures under specific conditions. 
IV. 
Techniques or Types of Polarography 
Several types of polarography exist, including: 
 
Direct current polarography 
 
Sample DC polarography 
 
Single sweep cathode ray polarography 
 
Pulse polarography 
 
Differential pulse polarography 
V. 
Applications of Polarography 
Polarography has a wide range of applications, including: 
 
Heavy Metal Determination: It can determine trace amounts of most heavy metal ions, as well as 
some non-metals like oxygen, chlorine, and ozone. 
 
Qualitative and Quantitative Analysis: The characteristic S-shaped current-voltage curve can be 
used for both qualitative identification and quantitative analysis of electro-reducible and electro-
oxidizable substances or ions. The critical voltage identifies the substance, while the magnitude of 
the limiting current measures its concentration. 
 
Biological Applications: Oxygen can be determined in biological fluids both in vivo and in vitro. 
 
Environmental Applications: Metals such as lead (Pb) and cadmium (Cd) in chemical or biological 
samples can be readily determined. 
 
Organic Compound Analysis: Various organic functional groups like azo, diazo, nitroso, and dioxy 
groups, as well as simple functional groups, can be determined. 
 
Inorganic Applications: It is widely applicable for analyzing inorganic species, including ions of 
alkali and alkaline earth metals. 
 
Biochemical Analysis: The technique is useful for biochemical analysis.

Potentiometry 
Potentiometry is an electro-analytical chemistry technique used to measure the electrochemical 
potential of charged particles. It is one of the volumetric techniques. 
I. 
Principle 
Potentiometry is based on the measurement of electrochemical potential. An electrode system connected 
to a potentiometer is used to measure this potential. The potential measured is dependent on the activity 
or concentration of ions, which allows for quantitative analysis using the Nernst equation, under 
conditions where no or minimum current passes through the potentiometer circuit. 
The Nernst equation, discovered by Walther H. Nernst (who was awarded the Nobel Prize in 1920), enables 
the determination of the electromotive force (EMF) of many processes, the cell potential of a galvanic cell, 
and the energy of a chemical reaction. This equation relates the electrode potential (E) to the standard 
electrode potential (E°), the gas constant (R), temperature (T), the number of moles of electrons (n), 
Faraday's Constant (F), and the concentrations or activities of reactants and products. 
The general form of the Nernst equation is: $E = E^o - \frac{RT}{nF} \ln Q$ Where $Q$ represents the reaction 
quotient (ratio of product activities to reactant activities). 
II. 
Methods Involved 
Potentiometric methods primarily involve two types of electrochemical analysis: 
 
Determination of pH: This is based on the principle that the potential difference between two 
suitable electrodes 
⁺ or H⁺) depends on their 
concentration or activity. The development of potential is not exclusive to H O⁺; a solution of any ion 
will develop a potential directly proportional to its concentration if a suitable pair of electrodes is 
present. 
 
Potentiometric Titrations: This involves measuring changes in the electromotive force (EMF) of a 
cell as a titrant is added. The monitoring of the potential serves to locate the equivalence point for 
a titration. 
III. 
Electrodes Used 
Typically, electrochemical measurements in potentiometry utilize two main types of electrodes: 
 
Reference Electrodes 
 
Indicator Electrodes 
3.1. Reference Electrodes

--- OCR TEXT ---
VI. Precautions (Oxygen Waves)

Dissolved oxygen in the solution to be analyzed polarographically often interferes with the accurate
determination of other species. Therefore, removal of oxygen before analysis is necessary, typically by
using a vacuum or purging the solution with nitrogen (N,) for several minutes. This interference occurs
because oxygen produces two equal waves: the first from its reduction to hydrogen peroxide (H,0,) and the
second from the further reduction of hydrogen peroxide to water.

The general form of the Nernst equation is: $E = E*o - \frac{RT}{nF} \In Q$ Where $Q$ represents the reaction

e Determination of pH: This is based on the principle that the potential difference between two

3.1. Reference Electrodes

============================================================
PAGE 87
============================================================
A reference electrode is a half-cell with an accurately known electrode potential (Eref) that is 
independent of the concentration of the analyte or other ions in the solution. It is always considered the left-
hand electrode. 
Properties of an ideal Reference electrode: 
 
Accurately known and constant potential. 
 
Completely insensitive to the composition of the analyte solution. 
 
Rugged and easy to assemble. 
 
Maintains a constant potential even when a small amount of current passes through it. 
Commonly used reference electrodes include: 
 
Saturated Calomel Electrode (SCE)  
o Construction: Consists of mercury (Hg) and mercury(I) chloride ($Hg_2Cl_2$) paste, 
immersed in a saturated KCl solution. The tube is typically 5-15 cm long with a diameter of 
0.5-1 cm. A porous salt bridge connects it to the analyte solution. 
o Electrode Reaction: $Hg_2Cl_2 (s) + 2e^- \rightarrow 2Hg (l) + 2Cl^- (aq)$. 
o Potential: Its potential depends on the KCl concentration. For a saturated KCl solution, the 
potential is +0.244 V at 25°C (vs. SHE). 
o Applications: Used for pH measurement, cyclic voltammetry, and general aqueous 
electrochemistry. 
o Limitation: Contains mercury, which is a health hazard. 
 
Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE)  
o The universal reference for reporting relative half-cell potentials. 
o It is a theoretical electrode with a defined half-cell potential of 0.00 volts. 
o It cannot be manufactured practically because it requires a hydrogen ion activity of 1.00 M, 
which is not achievable. 
o A platinum-black electrode is needed to provide a surface for the contact between hydrogen 
gas and hydrogen ions. 
 
Silver-Silver Chloride Electrode (Ag/AgCl)  
o Construction: A silver wire or platinum wire coated with a thin layer of silver chloride, 
immersed in a potassium chloride solution of known concentration. It functions as a redox 
electrode. 
o Electrode Reaction: $AgCl(s) + e^- \rightarrow Ag(s) + Cl^-$. 
o Potential: For a saturated KCl solution, the potential is +0.199 V at 25°C (vs. SHE). 
o Advantages: Simple construction, inexpensive, stable potential, and non-toxic components. 
o Applications: Widely used in biological electrode systems (e.g., ECG, EEG). 
 
Mercury(I) Sulfate Electrode

--- OCR TEXT ---
Areference electrode is a half-cell with an accurately known electrode potential (Eref) that is
independent of the concentration of the analyte or other ions in the solution. It is always considered the left-
hand electrode.

Properties of an ideal Reference electrode:

e Accurately known and constant potential.

e Completely insensitive to the composition of the analyte solution.

e Rugged and easy to assemble.

e Maintains a constant potential even when a small amount of current passes through it.
Commonly used reference electrodes include:

e Saturated Calomel Electrode (SCE)

o Construction: Consists of mercury (Hg) and mercury(I) chloride ($Hg_2Cl_2$) paste,
immersed in a saturated KCI solution. The tube is typically 5-15 cm long with a diameter of
0.5-1 cm. A porous salt bridge connects it to the analyte solution.

o Electrode Reaction: $Hg_2Cl_2 (s) + 2e*- \rightarrow 2Hg (I) + 2CI*- (aq)$.

o Potential: Its potential depends on the KCl concentration. For a saturated KCI solution, the
potential is +0.244 V at 25°C (vs. SHE).

o Applications: Used for pH measurement, cyclic voltammetry, and general aqueous
electrochemistry.

o Limitation: Contains mercury, which is a health hazard.
e Standard Hydrogen Electrode (SHE)
o Theuniversal reference for reporting relative half-cell potentials.
o Itisatheoretical electrode with a defined half-cell potential of 0.00 volts.

o  Itcannot be manufactured practically because it requires a hydrogen ion activity of 1.00 M,
which is not achievable.

o Aplatinum-black electrode is needed to provide a surface for the contact between hydrogen
gas and hydrogen ions.

e Silver-Silver Chloride Electrode (Ag/AgCl)

o Construction: A silver wire or platinum wire coated with a thin layer of silver chloride,
immersed in a potassium chloride solution of known concentration. It functions as a redox
electrode.

o Electrode Reaction: $AgCl(s) + e*- \rightarrow Ag(s) + Cl*-$.
o Potential: For a saturated KCI solution, the potential is +0.199 V at 25°C (vs. SHE).
o Advantages: Simple construction, inexpensive, stable potential, and non-toxic components.
o Applications: Widely used in biological electrode systems (e.g., ECG, EEG).
e Mercury(l) Sulfate Electrode

============================================================
PAGE 88
============================================================
o Construction: Similar to the saturated calomel electrode, but uses 0.05 M H2SO4 saturated 
with Mercury (I) sulfate. 
o Application: Used in solutions containing lead (Pb) or silver (Ag) ions, with a potential of 683 
mV. 
3.2. Indicator Electrodes 
An indicator electrode is an electrode system whose potential varies with changes in the concentration 
(activity) of the analyte. It is selective in its response. 
Characteristics of an ideal Indicator electrode: 
 
Responds rapidly and reproducibly to changes in analyte concentration. 
 
No indicator electrode is absolutely specific, but some are remarkably selective. 
Types of indicator electrodes: 
1. Glass Electrode  
o Introduction: A type of ion-selective electrode made of a doped glass membrane sensitive 
to specific ions, most commonly H⁺. 
o Principle/Response: Its pH sensing ability comes from the ion exchange property of its 
glass membrane. When hydrated, H⁺ ions from the analyte solution diffuse into the 
membrane, replacing Na⁺ ions, establishing a boundary potential. 
o Construction: A thin-walled glass bulb (approx. 0.1 mm thick) sealed to a thicker tube, filled 
with an internal reference electrode (e.g., Ag/AgCl) and a constant HCl solution. 
o Applications: Primarily used for pH measurements. Also used for determining 
concentrations of lithium, sodium, and ammonium ions, and in various applications like soil 
acidity analysis and biological cell membrane potential measurements. 
o Interfering Ions: Other ions of similar size, especially alkali metals, can interact with the ion-
exchange centers, affecting the response. 
o Storage & Precautions: Must be kept hydrated (e.g., in 0.1 M HCl or H2SO4 or distilled water) 
to function properly and prevent an increased response time. 
o Advantages: Wide pH range, unaffected by oxidizing/reducing agents, fast response, 
functions well in physiological systems. 
o Disadvantages: Extremely fragile, high resistance requiring specialized potentiometers. 
2. Ion-Selective Electrodes (ISE)  
o Introduction: Sensor electrodes that convert the activity of a specific ion in a solution into 
an electrical potential, measurable by a pH meter or voltmeter. They respond selectively to 
ions in the presence of others. 
o Principle: Based on the galvanic cell principle, measuring the electric potential generated 
across a specific membrane by "selected" ions, compared to a reference electrode. The net 
charge is proportional to the concentration of the selected ion.

--- OCR TEXT ---
Construction: Similar to the saturated calomel electrode, but uses 0.05 M H2S04 saturated
with Mercury (I) sulfate.

Application: Used in solutions containing lead (Pb) or silver (Ag) ions, with a potential of 683
mV.

3.2. Indicator Electrodes

An indicator electrode is an electrode system whose potential varies with changes in the concentration
(activity) of the analyte. It is selective in its response.

Characteristics of an ideal Indicator electrode:

e Responds rapidly and reproducibly to changes in analyte concentration.

e Noindicator electrode is absolutely specific, but some are remarkably selective.

Types of indicator electrodes:
