2. lon-Selective Electrodes (ISE)

fe}

Introduction: Sensor electrodes that convert the activity of a specific ion in a solution into
an electrical potential, measurable by a pH meter or voltmeter. They respond selectively to
ions in the presence of others.

Principle: Based on the galvanic cell principle, measuring the electric potential generated
across a specific membrane by "selected" ions, compared to a reference electrode. The net
charge is proportional to the concentration of the selected ion.

============================================================
PAGE 89
============================================================
o Properties: Available for a limited number of common ionic species (cations like $NH_4^+$, 
$Ba^{++}$, $Ca^{++}$, $K^+$, $Na^+$; anions like $Br^-$, $Cl^-$, $F^-$, $NO_3^-$). 
o Measurement Considerations: Require agitation, pH adjustment, longer stabilization time, 
a calibration curve (using three standard solutions), and proper rinsing (with deionized or 
distilled water). Electrodes must remain moist. 
o Advantages: Wide concentration range (0.1 10,000 ppm), shock-proof and chemically 
resistant bodies, fast sample analysis (1 2 minutes). 
o Types (based on membrane):  
 
Glass membrane: Selective for single-charged ions ($H^+$, $Na^+$, $Ag^+$). 
 
Crystalline membrane: Selective for both cations and anions.  
 
Solid State Electrode: Example is the fluoride electrode, using a single 
crystal of Lanthanum Fluoride ($LaF_3$) doped with $Eu^{II}$. Highly 
selective for fluoride over other halide and common ions. 
 
Ion exchange resin membrane: Selectivity changes with resin composition.  
 
Coated Wire Electrode: Prepared by coating a metal wire (Pt, Ag, Cu) with a 
PVC membrane. Convenient, easily made, and show near-Nernstian response. 
Example: Nitrate-coated ISE. 
 
Liquid-Liquid Electrode: Potential-determining membrane is a layer of 
water-immiscible liquid ion exchanger held by an inert/porous membrane. 
Example: $Ca^{2+}$ selective electrodes using $Ca^{2+}$ organo-
phosphorous compounds. 
 
Plastic Membrane Ionophore Electrodes: Uses a neutral, lipophilic 
ionophore dissolved in a soft plastic membrane (e.g., PVC) that selectively 
complexes with the ion of interest. Example: $K^+$ ion selective electrode 
with valinomycin. 
 
Potentiometric Enzyme-Based Electrodes: Prepared by immobilizing an 
enzyme (e.g., urease) in a gel coated on a cation-sensitive glass electrode. 
Example: Urea electrode, which hydrolyzes urea to ammonium ions, sensed 
by the electrode. 
IV. 
Salt Bridge 
A salt bridge is typically a U-shaped glass tube containing a neutral inert salt (e.g., KCl) in an agar 
medium. 
Purpose: 
 
To obtain electrical neutrality between the two half-cells of a voltaic or electrochemical cell. 
 
To prevent the components of the analyte solution from mixing with those of the reference 
electrode. 
 
To maintain the passage of electricity through the external circuit.

--- OCR TEXT ---
o Properties: Available for a limited number of common ionic species (cations like $NH_4*+$,
$Ba{++}$, $Car{++}$, $K%+$, $Na*+$; anions like $Br4-$, $Cl4-$, $F*-$, $NO_3”-$).

o Measurement Considerations: Require agitation, pH adjustment, longer stabilization time,
a calibration curve (using three standard solutions), and proper rinsing (with deionized or
distilled water). Electrodes must remain moist.

o Advantages: Wide concentration range (0.1-10,000 ppm), shock-proof and chemically
resistant bodies, fast sample analysis (1-2 minutes).

o Types (based on membrane):

= Glass membrane: Selective for single-charged ions ($H*+$, $Na*+$, $Ag’+$).

« Crystalline membrane: Selective for both cations and anions.

= lon exchange resin membrane: Selectivity changes with resin composition.

A salt bridge is typically a U-shaped glass tube containing a neutral inert salt (e.g., KCl) in an agar
medium.

Purpose:

To obtain electrical neutrality between the two half-cells of a voltaic or electrochemical cell.

To prevent the components of the analyte solution from mixing with those of the reference

electrode.

To maintain the passage of electricity through the external circuit.

============================================================
PAGE 90
============================================================
Working: Positive ions accumulate in the anode compartment and negative ions in the cathode 
compartment over time, which would stop current flow. Salt bridges release negative ions into the anode 
compartment and positive ions into the cathode compartment, neutralizing the charge imbalance and 
maintaining the electrical circuit. 
Characteristics of a Good Salt Bridge: 
 
Should not provide a pathway for the diffusion of electrolytes from one half-cell to another. 
 
The salt used should be chemically inert and should not form any precipitate with the electrolytes. 
 
Potassium chloride is ideal due to the nearly equal mobilities of K+ and Cl- ions. 
V. 
Electrode Potential (E) and Standard Cell Potential (E°) 
 
Electrode Potential (E): The difference in potential between an electrode and the solution in 
which it is immersed. It depends on the nature of the electrodes, the concentration of the solution, 
and temperature. 
 
Standard Cell Potential (E°): The potential when the temperature is 25°C (298 K), and the 
concentration of all species is 1 M (or 1 atmospheric partial pressure for gases).  
o E° Cell = E° Cathode - E° Anode. 
 
Limitations of Electrode Potential: While they predict whether a chemical reaction will occur, they 
do not indicate the rate of reaction or assure its success. They are useful for predicting if a reaction 
will not occur if potential differences are insufficient. 
 
Applications: Used for determining thermodynamic cell potential (e.g., EMF) and calculating 
equilibrium constants for redox reactions. 
VI. 
Measurement of pH 
pH is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen (H+) ion concentration (or activity, for 
thermodynamic accuracy). It is a critical factor influencing reaction rates and the nature of species present. 
 
$pH = -\log[H^+]$. 
 
For dilute solutions, activity ($a_{H+}$) is approximately equal to concentration ($[H^+]$). 
Role of Buffers: Buffer solutions are used to control pH, maintaining a definite pH that is not significantly 
affected by the addition of small amounts of acids or bases, or by dilution. Weak acids/bases and their salts 
typically act as buffers. 
pH Meter: A device used for determining the pH of a solution. 
 
Principle: Measures the potential difference between an indicator electrode (e.g., glass electrode) 
and a reference electrode (e.g., calomel electrode) immersed in the solution. 
VII. 
Potentiometric Titrations 
Potentiometric titrations involve measuring and recording the cell potential (in mV or pH) after each 
addition of titrant. 
 
Process: Titrant is added in increments, with smaller volumes near the endpoint. Sufficient time is 
allowed for equilibrium after each addition. 
 
Advantages:

--- OCR TEXT ---
Working: Positive ions accumulate in the anode compartment and negative ions in the cathode
compartment over time, which would stop current flow. Salt bridges release negative ions into the anode
compartment and positive ions into the cathode compartment, neutralizing the charge imbalance and
maintaining the electrical circuit.

Characteristics of a Good Salt Bridge:
e Should not provide a pathway for the diffusion of electrolytes from one half-cell to another.
e The salt used should be chemically inert and should not form any precipitate with the electrolytes.
e Potassium chloride is ideal due to the nearly equal mobilities of K+ and Cl- ions.

VII. Potentiometric Titrations

Potentiometric titrations involve measuring and recording the cell potential (in mV or pH) after each
addition of titrant.

e Process: Titrant is added in increments, with smaller volumes near the endpoint. Sufficient time is
allowed for equilibrium after each addition.

e Advantages:

============================================================
PAGE 91
============================================================
o Provides more reliable data than titrations using chemical indicators. 
o Particularly useful for colored and turbid solutions. 
o Useful for detecting unsuspected species. 
o Readily automated. 
 
Disadvantage: More time-consuming than titrations using indicators. 
 
End Point Determination:  
o Direct Plot: Plotting potential vs. reagent volume. The endpoint is visually estimated as the 
midpoint of the steep rising portion of the curve. 
o Derivative Plots: Plotting the first or second derivative of the titration curve, where the slope 
of the curve reaches its maximum value at the inflection point. 
 
Types of Titrations: Various titrations can be performed, including acid-base, redox, 
precipitation, and complex formation titrations. 
 
Applications: Assay of various pharmaceutical compounds like Caffeine, Phenobarbitone, 
Thonzylamine hydrochloride, and Tetrahydrozoline hydrochloride. 
VIII. 
Common Features of Electroanalytical Techniques 
All electrochemical techniques, including potentiometry, share several common features: 
 
The electrode's potential determines the analyte's form (or reaction position) at the electrode's 
surface. 
 
The concentration of the analyte at the electrode's surface may differ from its concentration in 
the bulk solution. 
 
The analyte may participate in other reactions (e.g., adsorption, complex formation). 
 
Current is a measure of the rate of the analyte's oxidation or reduction. 
 
Current and potential cannot be controlled simultaneously. 
Introduction to Official Inorganic Compounds 
Inorganic chemistry is a branch of chemistry that focuses on the study of all elements and their 
compounds, except for carbon and its compounds (which are primarily studied in organic chemistry). It 
describes the characteristics of nonliving matter and minerals found in the Earth. While there is overlap 
with organic chemistry, especially in organometallic chemistry, inorganic chemistry generally deals with 
compounds lacking hydrocarbon radicals. Historically, inorganic compounds were viewed as inanimate, 
with the first significant synthetic inorganic compound being ammonium nitrate for soil fertilization. Many 
naturally occurring inorganic compounds are found as minerals. 
Inorganic pharmaceuticals can be useful in several ways: 
 
Medicinal/Therapeutic Purpose: For example, as astringents and antimicrobials. 
 
Pharmaceutical Aids: Such as Bentonite and talc. 
 
Modifying Body Fluid Reactions: To acidify or alkalize, like antacids or mineral acids.

--- OCR TEXT ---

o Particularly useful for colored and turbid solutions.
o Useful for detecting unsuspected species.
o Readily automated.
Disadvantage: More time-consuming than titrations using indicators.

o Direct Plot: Plotting potential vs. reagent volume. The endpoint is visually estimated as the
midpoint of the steep rising portion of the curve.

o Derivative Plots: Plotting the first or second derivative of the titration curve, where the slope

Inorganic chemistry is a branch of chemistry that focuses on the study of all elements and their

describes the characteristics of nonliving matter and minerals found in the Earth. While there is overlap
with organic chemistry, especially in organometallic chemistry, inorganic chemistry generally deals with
compounds lacking hydrocarbon radicals. Historically, inorganic compounds were viewed as inanimate,
with the first significant synthetic inorganic compound being ammonium nitrate for soil fertilization. Many
naturally occurring inorganic compounds are found as minerals.

Inorganic pharmaceuticals can be useful in several ways:

Medicinal/Therapeutic Purpose: For example, as astringents and antimicrobials.
Pharmaceutical Aids: Such as Bentonite and talc.

Modifying Body Fluid Reactions: To acidify or alkalize, like antacids or mineral acids.

 
Replenishing Normal Body Fluid Content: Replacing essential ions like sodium, potassium, 
calcium, chloride, and phosphate. 
 
Reagents: Acting as catalysts (e.g., platinum, nickel) or oxidizing/reducing agents (e.g., lithium 
aluminum hydride). 
 
Pharmaceutical Analysis: As titrants, for example, potassium permanganate. 
Specific applications of inorganic pharmaceuticals include: 
 
Abrasives: For teeth cleaning (e.g., Dibasic calcium phosphate). 
 
Absorbents: To absorb toxins and bacteria in the GIT (e.g., Calcium carbonate). 
 
Acidifiers: To temporarily enhance GIT acidity (e.g., Dilute hydrochloric acid). 
 
Adsorbents: For treating mild dysentery, diarrhea, or other GIT disturbances by adsorbing gases, 
toxins, and bacteria (e.g., Bismuth subcarbonate, Bismuth subnitrate). 
 
Alkalizers: To induce alkaline conditions or treat acidic conditions (e.g., Sodium citrate). 
 
Anesthetics: To produce reversible loss of sensation (e.g., Nitrous oxide). 
 
Analgesics: To relieve pain (e.g., Nitrous oxide). 
 
Antacids: Alkaline substances neutralizing excess stomach acid (e.g., Aluminium hydroxide gel, 
Calcium carbonate, Magnesium carbonate). 
 
Antibacterial: For bacterial infections (e.g., Yellow mercuric oxide). 
 
Antidepressants: For depression treatment (e.g., Lithium carbonate). 
 
Antidotes: For poison treatment (e.g., Sodium nitrite, Sodium thiosulphate). 
 
Anti-irritant agents: To prevent irritation or allergic reactions (e.g., Aluminium metal powder). 
 
Antiseptics: To inhibit microbial growth without killing (e.g., Strong iodine solution). 
 
Antioxidants: Substances preventing or delaying oxidation (e.g., Sodium bisulphate, sodium 
metabisulphite, sodium sulphite). 
 
Astringents: Substances causing protein precipitation (e.g., Calamine, Aluminium citrate).

o Dissolve 15mg of the substance in 2ml of water, add 0.5m of dilute HCI and 0.5ml of
thioacetamide (no precipitate forms). Upon dropwise addition of dilute NaOH, a gelatinous
white precipitate forms, which then dissolves with further addition of dilute NaOH,
confirming Al(OH).

o Alternatively, dissolve 2.5g of Al(OH); in 15ml of HCl, heat on a water bath, dilute to 100ml
with distilled water. Take 2ml of this solution, add 0.5ml of thioacetamide reagent (no
precipitate forms), then add dilute NaOH dropwise; white gelatinous precipitate forms,
confirming Al(OH).

e = Assay:
o Principle: Complexometric titration of Aluminium.

o Procedure: Dissolve 0.8g in 10ml of HCI, heat, cool, and dilute to 50ml with water. Take 10ml
of this solution, add dilute ammonia until precipitation begins, then add a small amount of
dilute HCl to dissolve the precipitate and dilute to 20ml with water. Introduce 20ml of this
solution into a conical flask. Add 25ml of 0.1M sodium edetate and 10ml of a mixture of equal
volumes of a 155g/L ammonium acetate solution and acetic acid. Boil for 2 minutes, cool,
and add 50ml of ethanol with 3ml of 0.25g/L dithizone solution.

o Indicator: Dithizone.

o Standard Solution: 0.1M zinc sulfate.

o End Point: Color changes from greenish blue to reddish violet.

o Calculation: 1m of 0.1M sodium edetate is equivalent to 5.098mg of Al,0O3.

e Chemical Reaction: Thermal decomposition of aluminium hydroxide produces aluminium oxide
(Al,0;) and water, occurring above 575°C: 2Al(OH); > Al,O3 + 3H, 0.

e Uses:
o Used as an antacid (neutralizes HCI in gastric secretions).
o Used as an adjuvant in some vaccines.
o Relieves symptoms of dyspepsia, heartburn, and ulcers.
o Used in hyperphosphatemia.
e Preparations: Al(OH); tablets, Al(OH); oral syrup.
e Brands: Algicon (liquid), Xenon (manufacturer).

e Dosage: 500-600 mg.

Preparation:
o Solvay Process (or ammonia-soda process): The major industrial process.
= Chemistry: Starts with salt brine (NaCl) and limestone (CaCO3).

. Carbon dioxide (CO,) is passed through a concentrated aqueous solution of NaCl and ammonia
(NH), precipitating sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3): NaCl + CO, + NH; + H,O > NaHCO; + NH,Cl.
Ammonia buffers the solution to a basic pH, preventing the byproduct HCI from making it acidic and
stopping precipitation.

. CO, is produced by heating (calcination) of limestone: CaCO; > CO, + CaO.

The NaHCO, precipitate is filtered from the hot NH,Cl solution, which then reacts with quicklime
(CaO) from step (II): 2 NH,Cl + CaO > 2 NH; + CaCl, + H,O. The NH; is recycled back to step (I).

The NaHCO; precipitate is converted to sodium carbonate (Na,CO;) by calcination (160-230 °C),
producing water and CO, as byproducts: 2 NaHCO; > Na,CO; + H,0 + CO).

Properties:
o Appearance: Pure form is a white, odorless powder that is hygroscopic.
o Taste: Strong alkaline taste.

o Solubility: Water soluble sodium salt of carbonic acid, forming moderately basic solutions in
water.

Identification: The basic test for carbonate salts is a reaction with a diluted acid solution that
leads to release of bubbles of carbon dioxide gas.

Assay:
o Principle: Acid-base titration.

o Procedure: Accurately weigh about 2g of dried sample. Dissolve carefully in 50ml of 1N
sulfuric acid. Add methyl orange and titrate the excess acid with 1N sodium hydroxide.

o Calculation: Each ml of 1N sulfuric acid is equivalent to 53.0 mg of sodium carbonate.

o Manufacture of glass, paper, soap, and detergents.
o Water softener (precipitates calcium and magnesium ions in hard water).
o Used in toothpastes as a foaming agent and abrasive to increase mouth pH.
o Usedasaprimary base for acid-base titrations as it is solid and air stable.

Solvay Process: 
The Solvay process, also known as the ammonia-soda process, is the major industrial process for the 
production of sodium carbonate (soda ash). It was developed into its modern form by Ernest Solvay 
during the 1860s. 
The key ingredients for the Solvay process are readily available and inexpensive: 
 
Salt brine (sodium chloride, NaCl) 
 
Limestone (calcium carbonate, CaCO3) 
 
Ammonia (NH3) 
The chemistry of the Solvay process involves a series of steps: 
1. Carbon dioxide (CO2) is passed through a concentrated aqueous solution of sodium chloride 
(NaCl) and ammonia (NH3). 
o This reaction is typically carried out by bubbling ammonia up through brine, where it is 
absorbed. 
o Then, carbon dioxide is bubbled through the ammoniated brine. 
o Sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) precipitates out of the solution. 
o The ammonia buffers the solution at a basic (high) pH, which is crucial because without it, 
the hydrochloric acid byproduct would make the solution acidic and stop the precipitation. 
o The

limestone (CaCO3) at 950 1100 °C. 
o This process converts calcium carbonate into quicklime (calcium oxide, CaO) and carbon 
dioxide
 
3. The sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) precipitate from the first step is filtered out from the hot 
ammonium chloride (NH4Cl) solution. 
o The remaining ammonium chloride solution then reacts with the quicklime (CaO) from the 
limestone calcination. 
o Ammonia (NH3) is regenerated in this step and recycled back to the initial brine solution 
for reuse in the process.

carbonate (Na2CO3), through calcination (heating at 160 230 °C). 
o This step produces water and carbon dioxide as byproducts

Solvay Process:

The Solvay process, also known as the ammonia-soda process, is the major industrial process for the
production of sodium carbonate (soda ash). It was developed into its modern form by Ernest Solvay
during the 1860s.

The key ingredients for the Solvay process are readily available and inexpensive:
e Salt brine (sodium chloride, NaCl)
e Limestone (calcium carbonate, CaCO3)
e Ammonia (NH3)

The chemistry of the Solvay process involves a series of steps:

1. Carbon dioxide (CO2) is passed through a concentrated aqueous solution of sodium chloride
(NaCl) and ammonia (NH3).

o This reaction is typically carried out by bubbling ammonia up through brine, where it is
absorbed.

o Then, carbon dioxide is bubbled through the ammoniated brine.
o Sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) precipitates out of the solution.

o The ammonia buffers the solution at a basic (high) pH, which is crucial because without it,
the hydrochloric acid byproduct would make the solution acidic and stop the precipitation.

o Thereaction is: NaCl + CO2 + NH3 + H20 > NaHCO3 + NH4CI.

2. The carbon dioxide needed for the first reaction is produced by heating ("calcination")
limestone (CaCO3) at 950-1100 °C.

o This process converts calcium carbonate into quicklime (calcium oxide, CaO) and carbon
dioxide: CaCO03 > CO2 + CaO.

3. The sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) precipitate from the first step is filtered out from the hot
ammonium chloride (NH4Cl) solution.

o Theremaining ammonium chloride solution then reacts with the quicklime (CaO) from the
limestone calcination.

o Ammonia (NHS3) is regenerated in this step and recycled back to the initial brine solution
for reuse in the process.

o The reaction is: 2 NH4CI + CaO > 2 NH3 + CaCl2 + H20. CaO creates a strong basic solution.

4. Finally, the sodium bicarbonate (NaHCO3) precipitate is converted to the final product, sodium
carbonate (Na2CO3), through calcination (heating at 160-230 °C).

o This step produces water and carbon dioxide as byproducts: 2 NaHCO3 > Na2CO3 + H20 +
C02.

Insummary, the overall simplified reaction of the Solvay process is: 2 NaCl + CaCO3 > Na2CO3 + CaCcl2.

Magnesium Carbonate -MgCoO;

Introduction: An inorganic salt that is a white solid. Several hydrated and basic forms exist as
minerals.

Other Names: Magnesite, Carbonic acid, Magnesium salt.

Physical Properties:
o Appearance: White, yellowish, grayish-white or brown crystalline solid or crystalline powder.
o Odor: Odorless.
o Density: 3-3.1 g cm 3.

o Solubility: Practically insoluble in water or ethanol but dissolves, with effervescence, in
dilute acids.

Preparation:
o Ordinarily obtained by mining the mineral magnesite.

o Canbe prepared in the laboratory by the reaction between any soluble magnesium salt and
sodium bicarbonate: MgCl, (aq) + 2NaHCO; (aq) > MgCO; (s) + 2NaCl (aq) + H2O (I) + CO, (g).

o Ifmagnesium chloride (or sulfate) is treated with aqueous sodium carbonate, a precipitate
of basic magnesium carbonate (a hydrated complex of magnesium carbonate and
magnesium hydroxide) is formed: 5MgCl, (aq) + 5Na,CO; (aq) + 5H,0 (I) >
Mg(OH),.3MgCO3.3H,0 (s) + Mg(HCO;)> (aq) + 10NaCl (aq).

Chemical Reaction:

o With Acids: Like many metal carbonates, reacts with aqueous acids to release carbon
dioxide and water: MgCO; + 2 HCl > MgCl, + CO, + H,0.

o Decomposition: At high temperatures, MgCO; decomposes to magnesium oxide and
carbon dioxide. This process, called calcining, is important in MgO production: MgCO; >
MgO + CO, (AH = +118 kJ/mol). Decomposition temperature is about 350 °C, but calcination is
generally considered complete above 900 °C due to CO, re-adsorption.

Identification: When treated with 3 N hydrochloric acid, it dissolves with effervescence, and the
resulting solution responds to the tests for Magnesium.

Assay:
o Principle: Acid-base titration.

o Procedure: Dissolve 1g of magnesium carbonate in 30ml of IN H,SO,, add methyl orange
and titrate the excess acid with IN NaOH. Deduct the volume of 1N H,SO, corresponding to
calcium content. The difference in volume is equivalent to MgO present.

o Calculation: Each ml of 1N sulphuric acid is equivalent to 20.15 mg of MgO and 20.04 mg of
Ca.

Uses:

============================================================
PAGE 99
============================================================
o Production of magnesium oxide by calcining. 
o 
antacid. 
o Additive in table salt to keep it free flowing (does not dissolve in water). 
o Used in taxidermy for whitening skulls (mixed with hydrogen peroxide). 
o Used in the manufacture of materials capable of withstanding very high temperatures and 
to produce carbon dioxide.

Sodium Nitrite – NaNO₂

o Non-combustible but accelerates burning of combustible material. Large quantities or

o Principle: Acid-base titration.

o Procedure: Dissolve about 1g of accurately weighed lithium carbonate in 50ml of 1N

o Calculation: Each ml of 1N sulphuric acid is equivalent to 36.95mg of Li,CO3.
Reactions:

o Reacts with dilute acids, liberating CO : Li,CO3 + HCl > LiCl + CO, + H,0.

o Thermal decomposition yields lithium oxide and CO): Li,CO3 > Li,0 + CO.

o Reaction with lime produces lithium hydroxide: Li,CO; + Ca(OH) > 2LiIOH + CaCO3.

o Combines with CO, in aqueous slurry to form soluble bicarbonate, which decomposes to
carbonate upon heating: Li,CO; + CO, + H,O > 2LiHCO;. Bicarbonate exists only in solution
when carbonate dissolves in water saturated with CO, under pressure.

Uses: Used systemically in the treatment of Bipolar Disorder, Borderline Personality Disorder,
Cluster Headaches, Depression, Intermittent Explosive Disorder, Mania, and Schizoaffective
Disorder.

Preparations: Capsules, Tablet, Injection.
Brands: Lithium carbonate ER, Litho bid, ESKalith, ESKalith CR.

Therapeutic Monitoring: Considered a narrow therapeutic index (NTI) drug, meaning small
dose/blood concentration differences can lead to serious therapeutic failures or adverse reactions.
Generic substitution should be done cautiously, and additional/frequent monitoring is advised.
Safety and efficacy not established for patients younger than 12.

Dose: Acute Control: 1800 mg/day; Long-term Control: 900 to 1200 mg/day.
Adverse Effect: Not explicitly detailed, but implied by NTI classification.

Sodium Nitrite - NaNO,

Introduction: An inorganic compound, a useful precursor to pharmaceuticals, dyes, and pesticides.
Best known as a food additive in processed meats and fish products.

Other Names: Nitrous acid, sodium salt; sodiumnitrite; Erinitrit.
Physical Description:

o Appearance: Yellowish-white crystalline solid.

o Solubility: Soluble in water and hygroscopic.

o Non-combustible but accelerates burning of combustible material. Large quantities or
finely divided combustible material can lead to explosion.

o Contamination by ammonium compounds can cause spontaneous decomposition, igniting
surrounding material.

o Prolonged heat exposure may result in an explosion.

Introduction: A mineral supplement and medication. Supplementation is needed when dietary

Other Names: Calcium D-gluconate, Calciofon, Calglucon. 


 
Properties:  
o Solubility: Soluble slowly in water, insoluble in organic solvents and alcohol. 
o Nature: Inorganic and mildly acidic. 
o Appearance: Powdered and granules. 
o Odor: Odorless. 
 
Identification:  
o Dissolve 20mg of substance in minimum dilute HCl, neutralize with dilute NaOH, or mix 5ml 
of 10% w/v solution in water, then add 5ml ammonium carbonate solution, boil and cool. 
o Results in white amorphous precipitate (confirming calcium) and imparts yellow color 
when treated with ferric chloride solution. 
 
Assay:  
o Procedure: Accurately weigh 0.4g of previously dried calcium gluconate. Dissolve in 100ml of 
water, add 2ml of 8 mol/L KOH TS and 0.1g of NN indicator. Titrate immediately with 0.05 
mol/L disodium dihydrogen ethylenediamine tetraacetate VS until color changes from red-
purple to blue. 
o Calculation: Each ml of 0.05 mol/L disodium dihydrogen ethylenediamine tetraacetate 
contains 22.420 mg of Calcium gluconate. 
 
Chemical Reaction: Decomposes and produces carbonaceous foam; combustion also occurs 
 
 
Uses:  
o Hypocalcemic tetany. 
o Hypocalcemia related to hypoparathyroidism, rapid growth, or pregnancy. 
 
Preparations: Sterile Injections. 
 
Brand Name: Calglucon (Novartis). 
 
Dosage: 1300 mg/day depending on body weight.

VIII. 
Antimony Gluconate – C₁₈H₃₃O₂₁Sb 
 
Introduction: Sodium stibogluconate is a medicine used to treat leishmaniasis, available only by 
injection. It belongs to the pentavalent antimonials class. Widespread resistance limits its utility, 
with amphotericin or miltefosine often used instead. Also investigated as an anti-tumor agent. It 
has been in medical use since the 1940s and is on the WHO's List of Essential Medicines. 
 
Properties:  
o Molecular weight: 100-4000Da. 
o Low osmolarity of 789 miliosmol for a 100mg antimonyl/ml solution. 
o Main route of excretion is via kidneys. 
o Soluble in water, soluble in alcohol or ether. 
 
Preparation:  
o Trivalent stibogluconate: Prepared by treating an aqueous solution of calcium gluconate 
with sodium gluconate. The resulting sodium gluconate is reacted with antimony trichloride 
and NaOH to yield sodium stibogluconate. 
o Alternatively, calcium gluconate is treated with oxalic acid to get free gluconic acid, to which 
antimony trichloride and NaOH are added. 
o Pentavalent stibogluconate: Prepared by treating an aqueous solution of gluconic acid 
gluconate, with antimony content ranging from 28-29.5%. 
 
Uses:  
o Treatment of various types of protozoal infection called leishmaniasis. 
o Investigated for use/treatment in cancer/tumors (unspecified) and solid tumors. 
 
Preparations: Injection. 
 
Brands: Pentostam, Stibogluconate sodium. 
 
Dosage:  
o Adults: 20 mg Sb/kg/day (maximum 850 mg) IV/IM for 20-28 days. 
o Pediatric: 20 mg Sb/kg/day (maximum 850 mg) IV/IM for 20-28 days. 
o Available in the US only from CDC. 
 
Storage Conditions: Store in a well-closed container and away from moisture.

silver is formed. 
o Reacting silver (e.g., bullion or foil) with nitric acid, resulting in silver nitrate, water, and 
oxides of nitrogen. Reaction byproducts depend on nitric acid concentration.

Silver Nitrate - AgNO;

e Introduction: An inorganic compound, a versatile precursor to many other silver compounds
(e.g., in photography). Less sensitive to light than halides. Historically called lunar caustic.

e Preparation:
o Made in large quantities by dissolving silver in nitric acid.

o When heated to about 320°C (608°F), it loses oxygen and forms silver nitrite. At red heat,

o Reacting silver (e.g., bullion or foil) with nitric acid, resulting in silver nitrate, water, and

= 3Ag+4 HNO; (cold and diluted) > 3 AgNO; + 2 H,O +NO.
= Ag+2HNO; (hot and concentrated) > AgNO; + H,O + NO.
o Performed under a fume hood due to toxic nitrogen oxide evolution.

o Decomposes when heate
because silver oxide decomposes at a lower temperature than silver nitrate. 
 
Uses:  
o Antiseptic properties. 
o Used in newborn babies' eyes to prevent gonorrhea-induced blindness (Credé's method, 
1881). 
o Cauterizing agent (traditionally "lunar caustic"), e.g., to remove granulation tissue around 
a stoma. 
o Used by podiatrists to kill cells in the nail bed. 
o Used to cauterize superficial blood vessels in the nose to prevent nose bleeds.

Titrimetric analysis

Titrimetric analysis, often referred to as titrimetry or volumetric analysis, is used to determine the
unknown concentration of an analyte by reacting it stoichiometrically with a reagent of known
concentration. The process involves the gradual addition of the known reagent, called the titrant, toa

============================================================
PAGE 108
============================================================
sample containing the analyte until the reaction is complete. The completion of the reaction is detected at 
an endpoint, which ideally coincides with the equivalence point. 
Important Terms & Definitions 
 
Titrant: This is the solution of known concentration that is gradually added from a calibrated 
burette to the unknown solution. It is typically a strong acid or a strong base. 
 
Titrate (Titrand/Titer/Analyte): This refers to the substance whose concentration is to be 
determined. It can be a strong or weak acid or base. 
 
Standard Solution: A solution whose concentration is accurately known. 
 
Equivalence Point: This is the theoretical completion of the reaction, where stoichiometrically 
equivalent amounts of the analyte and titrant have reacted. At this point, the moles of titrant added 
are equal to the moles of analyte. 
 
End Point: This is the practically observed point in a titration where the reaction completion is 
indicated, and the addition of titrant is stopped. It is often indicated by a change in the color of an 
indicator substance. 
 
Titration Error: This is a systematic error due to the difference between the observed end point 
and the theoretical equivalence point. Selecting an appropriate endpoint that closely coincides with 
the equivalence point is crucial for accurate results. 
 
Primary Standard: A reagent that is highly pure and stable, can be weighed easily, and whose 
weight accurately represents the number of moles of substance.  
o Characteristics of a Primary Standard: High purity, stability to air (low reactivity), low 
hygroscopicity (minimal weight changes due to humidity), ready availability at modest cost, 
reasonable solubility in the titration medium, reasonably large molar mass (to minimize 
weighing errors), and non-toxicity. 
o Examples: Sodium carbonate (for standardizing aqueous acids like HCl, H2SO4, HNO3) and 
sodium chloride (for standardizing silver nitrate solutions). 
 
Secondary Standard: A reagent whose purity is established relative to a primary standard, 
meaning it is usually standardized against a primary standard. 
 
Displacement Titration: A titration where the analyte displaces a species, typically from a complex, 
and the amount of the displaced species is then determined by a titration. 
Titration Procedure 
A typical titration involves: 
1. Placing a precise volume of the analyte and a small amount of indicator (e.g., phenolphthalein) in 
a beaker or Erlenmeyer flask. 
2. Positioning the flask underneath a calibrated burette containing the titrant. 
3. Gradually adding small volumes (drops) of the titrant from the burette to the analyte solution. 
4. Stopping when the indicator changes color permanently, signaling the endpoint.

an endpoint, which ideally coincides with the equivalence point.

equivalent amounts of the analyte and titrant have reacted. At this point, the moles of titrant added

indicated, and the addition of titrant is stopped. It is often indicated by a change in the color of an
indicator substance.

the equivalence point is crucial for accurate results.

1.

a beaker or Erlenmeyer flask.

5. Measuring the volume of titrant consumed and using it to calculate the analyte's concentration. 
The concentration can be calculated using the formula: Ca = C1V1 = C2V2. 
Volumetric Apparatus / Glassware 
Volumetric apparatus consists of laboratory glassware calibrated to contain or deliver precise volumes. Key 
types include: 
 
Burettes: Graduated glass tubes of uniform bore used for the accurate delivery and measurement 
of variable liquid volumes. Common sizes are 50 ml, graduated in milliliters and tenths of milliliters. 
They feature a stopcock (Teflon or glass) to control liquid outflow. A small internal diameter 
provides a better-defined meniscus for precise reading. 
 
Pipettes: Used to deliver a definite volume of liquid. 
 
Volumetric Flasks: Used to contain a definite volume of liquid. 
 
Measuring Cylinders: Also used to contain a definite volume of liquid. 
Methods to Determine the End Point in Titrations 
Various methods can be employed to detect the endpoint: 
 
Indicator: A substance that undergoes a visible change, typically a color change, in response to a 
chemical change, such as pH or redox state. 
 
Potentiometer: Measures the electrode potential of the solution; a sudden change indicates the 
endpoint. 
 
pH Meter: A potentiometer with an electrode sensitive to H+ ion concentration, providing a more 
accurate pH measurement throughout the titration; a sudden pH change marks the endpoint. 
 
Conductivity: Measures the change in ion concentration in the solution, which affects conductivity. 
 
Color Change (Self-Indication): In some reactions, the solution itself changes color without an 
added indicator, often seen in redox titrations where reactants and products have different colors. 
 
Precipitation: When a solid forms during the titration (e.g., AgCl in argentometric titrations). Cloudy 
precipitates can make endpoint determination difficult, sometimes requiring back titrations. 
 
Isothermal Titration Calorimeter: Measures the heat produced or consumed by the reaction to 
determine the endpoint, used in biochemical titrations. 
 
Spectroscopy: Measures light absorption by the solution, relying on Beer's Law if the spectrum of 
reactant, titrant, or product is known. 
 
Amperometry: Measures the current produced by the oxidation or reduction of the analyte, with the 
endpoint detected as a change in current. 
Titration Curve 
A titration curve is a graph that plots the progress of a titration as a function of the volume of titrant 
added. Typically, the volume of titrant is on the x-axis, and the pH or concentration of the solution is on the 
y-axis. 
 
The curve visually represents how a property (like pH) changes with titrant addition.

The curve visually represents how a property (like pH) changes with titrant addition.

 
The inflection point (the steepest part of the curve where pH rises fastest) is visually located as the 
end point. More accurate methods involve plotting the first or second derivative of the curve to 
pinpoint this point. 
Necessary Conditions for Titrations 
For any chemical reaction to be suitable for titrimetric analysis, three conditions must be met: 
1. Known Stoichiometry: All reactions involving the titrant and analyte must have a known and 
quantitative stoichiometry. 
2. Rapid Reaction Rate: The titration reaction must occur rapidly to prevent significant deviation 
between the end point and equivalence point. 
3. Suitable Endpoint Detection: A reliable method must be available to determine the end point with 
an acceptable level of accuracy. Reactions should also ideally proceed at room temperature and 
without collateral reactions. 
Selecting and Standardizing a Titrant 
Most common acid-base titrants are not available as primary standards and must be standardized.

standardized against primary weak acid standards such as oxalic acid dihydrate, benzoic acid, or 
. 
Types of Titrimetric Determinations 
Titrimetric determinations can be classified in various ways: 
A. Based on Method of Titration 
1. Direct Titration: This is the most basic form where a known volume of titrant is directly added to the 
analyte until consumption is complete, indicated by an indicator. The unknown concentration is 
then calculated using the N1V1 = N2V2 or C1V1 = C2V2 formula. 
2. Indirect Titration: Used for substances that do not react readily or precisely by direct titration. The 
substance is chemically altered to a more reactive form (acidic or basic) before being analyzed with 
a titrant. 
3. Back Titration (Residual Titration): In this method, a known excess of a reagent is added to the 
analyte. The excess, unreacted reagent is then determined by a subsequent titration with another 
standard solution. This method is suitable when the direct titration reaction is too slow, a suitable 
indicator is unavailable, or the standard solution lacks stability. An example is the assay of ZnO, 
where excess sulfuric acid is added, and the remaining acid is then titrated with a standardized 
NaOH solution. 
B. Based on Quantity of Analyte to be measured 
 
Volumetric Titrimetry: Determines analyte quantity using measured volumes of reagents of known 
concentrations.

end point. More accurate methods involve plotting the first or second derivative of the curve to
pinpoint this point.

Potassium hydrogen phthalate (KHC,H,0,).

C. Based on Goals and Procedures (Common Types) 
1. Acid-base titration 
Acid-base titration is a fundamental quantitative chemical analysis method used to determine the 
unknown concentration of an acid or a base by neutralizing it with a solution of known concentration. This 
technique falls under the broader category of titrimetry or volumetric analysis, where the determination 
of an analyte is based on its stoichiometric reaction with a reagent of established concentration. 
I. 
Principle 
The core principle of acid-base titration is the neutralization reaction between an acid and a base. The 
reaction aims for the equivalence point, which is the theoretical point where stoichiometrically equivalent 
amounts of analyte and titrant have reacted. In the context of acid-base titrations, this means equal molar 
⁺ and OH⁻ ions are present in the titration flask, leading to the formation of water. 
However, in practice, the titration is stopped at the end point, which is the point where the completion of 
the reaction is physically observed, typically by a change in color of an indicator. The difference between 
the equivalence point and the end point is known as titration error, which is a determinate error that can 
be minimized by selecting an appropriate end point. 
II. 
Types of Acid-Base Titrations 
Acid-base titrations are categorized based on the strength of the acid and base involved in the reaction: 
 
Strong Acid vs. Strong Base 
o Reaction: A strong acid reacts with a strong alkali (base) to form salt and water. This 
reaction is swift, complete, and occurs in stoichiometric means, meaning each acid molecule 
reacts with a corresponding base molecule, leading to precise and sharp equivalence and 
end points. 
o Example: Hydrochloric acid (HCl) vs. Sodium hydroxide (NaOH). 
o Equation: HCl + 
 
o End Point pH: The end point is at an exact pH of 7, as the salt formed (NaCl) produces a 
neutral solution upon ionization. 
o Suitable Indicators: Indicators like methyl red (pH range 4.2 to 6.3), methyl orange (pH 
range 3.1 to 4.4), and phenolphthalein (pH range 8.3  10) are suitable because the 
equivalence point lies in the broad pH range of 4-10. 
 
Strong Acid vs. Weak Base 
o Reaction: A strong acid reacts with a weak base. Due to the strong acid, the pH at the end 
point will be acidic (below 7). 
o Example

electrical current, relating the extent of reaction to the number of moles of electrons.

concentration.

III. 
Finding the End Point by Monitoring pH 
An alternative to visual indicators is using a sensor (like a pH electrode) to monitor pH changes during 
titration. Plotting pH versus titrant volume gives a titration curve, where the equivalence point is the 
steepest part (inflection point). Derivatives of the titration curve can be plotted for more precise endpoint 
determination. 
IV. 
Alkalimetry and Acidimetry 
These are specialized terms within volumetric analysis focusing on acid-base reactions: 
 
Alkalimetry: The analytical use of acid-base titration to determine the concentration of a basic 
substance.

Acid-base titrations are widely used in pharmaceutical analysis.

 
Analysis of Weak Acids/Bases: Many pharmaceutical compounds are weak acids or bases, such as





 
Kjeldahl Analysis: This is an acid-base titrimetric method specifically for determining the amount of

⁺, then determined by titration. 
2. Oxidation-Reduction (Redox) Titration 
Oxidation-reduction (redox) titration is a type of titrimetric analysis where the reaction between the analyte 
and titrant involves a transfer of electrons. 
I. 
Principle 
Redox titrations involve an oxidant (oxidizing agent) being estimated by adding a reductant (reducing 
agent) or vice-versa. The fundamental principle is the transfer of electrons. In every such reaction, the 
oxidation and reduction processes essentially take place concurrently within the reaction, with one entity 
being reduced while oxidizing the second. 
Terms Defined by Electron Transfer: 
 
Oxidation: Loss of electrons. 
 
Reduction: Gain of electrons. 
 
Oxidizing Agents: Substances that remove (accept) electrons from another substance, causing it 
to oxidize. They add oxygen or remove hydrogen from another substance. 
 
Reducing Agents: Substances that transfer (give) electrons to another substance, causing it to 
reduce. They remove oxygen or add hydrogen to another substance. 
II. 
Endpoint Determination 
The equivalence point in a redox titration occurs when stoichiometrically equivalent amounts of analyte 
and titrant react. Any discrepancy between this theoretical point and the end point (where the titration is 
stopped) introduces a determinate error. 
 
Symmetrical Equivalence Point: This occurs when the stoichiometry of the redox titration is 
symmetrical (e.g., one mole of analyte reacts per mole of titrant). The inflection point (steepest part 
of the titration curve) is in the middle of the sharp rise. 
 
Asymmetrical Equivalence Point: If the stoichiometry is not symmetrical, the equivalence point will 
not be at the midpoint of the titration curve's sharp rise; instead, it will be closer to either the top or

--- OCR TEXT ---
Analysis of Weak Acids/Bases: Many pharmaceutical compounds are weak acids or bases, such as
salicylic acid, phenobarbital, caffeine, and sulfanilamide, which can be analyzed using aqueous or
nonaqueous acid-base titrations.

Functional Group Analysis: Carboxylic (—COOH), sulfonic (—SO;H), and phenolic (—C;HsOH) groups
(weak acids) can be titrated in aqueous or nonaqueous solvents, often using NaOH as the titrant in
aqueous solutions. Aliphatic and aromatic amines (weak bases) can be titrated using HCl in
aqueous solution or HCIO, in glacial acetic acid.

Amino Acid and Protein Analysis: Amino acids and proteins can be analyzed in glacial acetic acid
using HCIO, as the titrant. For instance, the amount of nutritionally available protein can be
determined by titrating lysine residues.

Kjeldahl Analysis: This is an acid-base titrimetric method specifically for determining the amount of
nitrogen in organic compounds. It is used in the analysis of caffeine and saccharin in
pharmaceutical products, as well as proteins and fertilizers. Nitrogen in the -3 oxidation state is
oxidized to NH, *, then determined by titration.

2. Oxidation-Reduction (Redox) Titration

Oxidation-reduction (redox) titration is a type of titrimetric analysis where the reaction between the analyte
and titrant involves a transfer of electrons.

Principle

Redox titrations involve an oxidant (oxidizing agent) being estimated by adding a reductant (reducing
agent) or vice-versa. The fundamental principle is the transfer of electrons. In every such reaction, the
oxidation and reduction processes essentially take place concurrently within the reaction, with one entity
being reduced while oxidizing the second.

Terms Defined by Electron Transfer:

Oxidation: Loss of electrons.
Reduction: Gain of electrons.

Oxidizing Agents: Substances that remove (accept) electrons from another substance, causing it
to oxidize. They add oxygen or remove hydrogen from another substance.

Reducing Agents: Substances that transfer (give) electrons to another substance, causing it to
reduce. They remove oxygen or add hydrogen to another substance.

Il. Endpoint Determination

The equivalence point in a redox titration occurs when stoichiometrically equivalent amounts of analyte
and titrant react. Any discrepancy between this theoretical point and the end point (where the titration is
stopped) introduces a determinate error.

Symmetrical Equivalence Point: This occurs when the stoichiometry of the redox titration is
symmetrical (e.g., one mole of analyte reacts per mole of titrant). The inflection point (steepest part
of the titration curve) is in the middle of the sharp rise.

Asymmetrical Equivalence Point: If the stoichiometry is not symmetrical, the equivalence point will
not be at the midpoint of the titration curve's sharp rise; instead, it will be closer to either the top or

============================================================
PAGE 114
============================================================
bottom. For instance, in the titration of Fe²⁺ 
⁻, the steepest portion is near the top due to 
proton consumption, making the curve asymmetrical. 
III. 
Redox Titration Curve 
A redox titration curve is a plot of the potential (E, in volts) versus the volume (mL) of titrant. It visually 
depicts how the electrochemical potential changes as titrant is added. The Nernst equation is used to 
relate the electrochemical potential to the concentrations of reactants and products. 
 
Before the equivalence point, the solution contains significant amounts of both oxidized and 
reduced forms of the analyte but very little unreacted titrant. The potential is best calculated using 
the Nernst equation for the analyte's half-reaction. 
IV. 
Visual Detection of the End Point 
While potentiometric methods can determine the end point, visual indicators are often more convenient. 
Three main visual indication methods are used: 
 
Self-Indication: This occurs when the titrant itself is highly colored, and its oxidized and reduced 
forms have significantly different colors. For example, 
⁻) solutions 
are intensely purple, while its reduced product, Mn²⁺, is nearly colorless.  
 
Starch Indicator: Starch is commonly used for redox titrations where 
⁻) is present. 
Starch forms a dark blue-black complex with triiodide. In titrations involving reducing agents and 
iodine, the solution remains colorless until the equivalence point, after which a slight excess of 
titrant turns the solution a definite blue.

3. Argentometric titration 
Argentometric titration is a process for determining the content of a substance based on a precipitation 
reaction from a component of the test substance, using the formation of silver (Ag+) precipitates. 
I. 
Principle 
It involves the use of silver nitrate (AgNO3) as the standard solution or titrant, which reacts with halide ions 
(like chloride, bromide, and iodide) or other substances to form an insoluble silver salt precipitate. The 
endpoint of the reaction is detected visually, often by using a specific indicator. 
II. 
Influencing Factors 
Several factors can influence the results of an Argentometric titration: 
 
Temperature 
 
Solvent Characteristics 
 
Ion Effect of a Kind 
 
Effect of Other Ions 
 
Effect of pH 
 
Complex Influence 
 
Hydrolysis Influence 
III. 
Types of Argentometric Methods 
There are three classical methods based on color indicators for endpoint detection in argentometric 
titrations: Mohr, Volhard, and Fajans methods. 
1. Mohr Method 
 
Use: This method is used to determine the content of chloride and bromide ions. It is specifically 
limited to solutions with a pH value between 6 and 10. 
 
Principle: It utilizes a chromate indicator (K2CrO4). The standard solution is silver nitrate (AgNO3).  
o Initially, silver ions react with chloride (or bromide) ions to form a white precipitate of silver 
 (precipitate) + NaNO3 
o Once all the chloride ions have precipitated, the excess silver ions react with the chromate 
indicator to form a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4), indicating the 
 + 2KNO3 
 
Conditions: The titration must be done in a neutral or slightly alkaline atmosphere (pH 6-10).  
o In acidic conditions, silver chromate (Ag2CrO4) would dissolve because silver dichromate 
(Ag2Cr2O7) would form. 
o In a basic atmosphere, silver nitrate (AgNO3) would react with hydroxide ions to form silver 
hydroxide (AgOH) precipitate, which interferes. 
 
Disturbances/Limitations:  
o Ions that precipitate sooner than AgCl (e.g., F-, CNS-, Br-) can interfere. 
o Ions that form complexes with Ag+ (e.g., CN-, NH3, above pH 7) can interfere.

Several factors can influence the results of an Argentometric titration:

e Temperature e Effect of pH

Ill. Types of Argentometric Methods

There are three classical methods based on color indicators for endpoint detection in argentometric
titrations: Mohr, Volhard, and Fajans methods.

1. Mohr Method

e Use: This method is used to determine the content of chloride and bromide ions. It is specifically
limited to solutions with a pH value between 6 and 10.

e Principle: It utilizes a chromate indicator (K2Cr04). The standard solution is silver nitrate (AgNO3).

o Initially, silver ions react with chloride (or bromide) ions to form a white precipitate of silver
chloride (AgCl): NaCl + AgNO3 > AgCl (precipitate) + NaNO3

o Onceall the chloride ions have precipitated, the excess silver ions react with the chromate
indicator to form a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2Cr04), indicating the
endpoint: 2AgNO3 + K2Cr04 > Ag2Cr04 (precipitate) + 2KNO3

¢ Conditions: The titration must be done in a neutral or slightly alkaline atmosphere (pH 6-10).

o Inacidic conditions, silver chromate (Ag2CrO4) would dissolve because silver dichromate
(Ag2Cr207) would form.

o Inabasic atmosphere, silver nitrate (AgNO3) would react with hydroxide ions to form silver
hydroxide (AgOH) precipitate, which interferes.

e Disturbances/Limitations:
o lons that precipitate sooner than AgCl (e.g., F-, CNS-, Br-) can interfere.

o lons that form complexes with Ag+ (e.g., CN-, NH3, above pH 7) can interfere.

============================================================
PAGE 117
============================================================
o Ions that form complexes with Cl- (e.g., Hg2+) can interfere. 
o Cations that precipitate chromate (e.g., Ba2+) can interfere. 
o The method is sensitive to light (photochemistry reduction), so it should be avoided from 
direct sunlight or neon light. 
2. Volhard Method 
 
Use: This method is primarily used for determining the amount of chloride, bromide, and iodide or 
other halogen ions in acidic conditions (pH under 3). 
 
Principle: It is an indirect titration. A known excess of silver ion is added to precipitate the halide 
ion. The excess silver is then back-titrated with a standard thiocyanate solution (KSCN or NH4SCN).  
o Indicator: Iron (III) (Fe3+), specifically ferric ammonium nitrate, is used as the indicator. This 
forms a red-colored complex with thiocyanate (SCN-) once all the excess silver ions have 
reacted. 
 
Conditions: The titration is usually performed in acidic pH medium (under 3) to prevent the 
precipitation of iron hydroxides (Fe (OH) 3), which would otherwise interfere with the indicator. 
 
Materials Used: Standard silver nitrate solution, halide ions (analyte), standard thiocyanate 
solution (titrant), and iron (III) indicator. 
 
Limitations: While considered the most stable method, it involves the use of toxic hydrocyanic acid 
(HCN). 
3. Fajans Method 
 
Indicator: This method uses adsorption indicators, which are organic compounds that get 
adsorbed onto the surface of the solid precipitate. A common example is Fluorescein. 
 
Principle: The indicator changes color when it is adsorbed onto the precipitate surface at the 
equivalence point. Adsorption indicators work best when they do not precipitate silver ions at low 
concentrations and only bind to the precipitate when excess Ag+ is present to produce color. 
 
Conditions: It has to be performed at near-neutral pH values. 
 
Limitations:  
o Requires near-neutral pH values. 
o The endpoint determination is operator-dependent. 
o It cannot be easily automated. 
o The presence of the precipitate can be influenced by sunlight acting as a catalyst.

V. 
General Procedure for Argentometric Titration (Mohr method example) 
1. Equipment and Reagents: 
o Solid reagents: NaCl, CaCO3, NaHCO3, K2CrO4, AgNO3. 
o Equipment: Burette, transfer pipette (25 mL) and pipette pump, Erlenmeyer flasks (250 mL), 
desiccator, volumetric flask (500 mL), amber bottle, graduated cylinder (100 mL), wash 
bottle. 
o Preparation of 5% K2CrO4 indicator: Dissolve 1.0 g of K2CrO4 in 20 mL of distilled water. 
2. Titration Process (Example with diluted seawater for chloride determination): 
o Dilute a seawater sample by pipetting 20 mL into a 100 mL volumetric flask and diluting to 
the mark with distilled water. 
o Pipette a 10 mL aliquot of the diluted seawater into a conical flask. 
o Add about 50 mL of distilled water and 1 mL of Chromate indicator. 
o Titrate the sample with 0.1 mole L-1 silver nitrate solution from a burette. 
o Observe the color: The silver chloride that forms is a white precipitate, and the chromate 
indicator initially gives the cloudy solution a faint lemon-yellow color. 
o Endpoint: The endpoint is identified as the first appearance of a red-brown color of silver 
chromate. 
o Repeat the titration with further aliquots of diluted seawater until concordant results (titers 
agreeing within 0.1 mL) are obtained. 
4. Non-Aqueous Titration 
I. 
Introduction  
Non-aqueous titration refers to a titration method where the analyte substance is dissolved in a solvent 
that does not contain water. This procedure is very important for pharmacopoeial assays. 
II. 
Theory 
The need for non-aqueous titration arises because water can behave as both a weak acid and a weak base. 
This dual nature allows water to compete in proton acceptance or donation with other weak acids and 
bases dissolved in it. For example, in an aqueous solvent, the reaction of a weak base (R-NH2) with a proton 
(H+) to form R-NH3+ is competed by water reacting with H+ to form H3O+. This competition makes it difficult 
to detect the end point precisely for very weak acids or bases in aqueous solutions. 
Non-aqueous titration provides a solution by using a solvent that doesn't interfere with the proton transfer, 
and it also offers the ability to dissolve organic compounds that are insoluble in water. Many reactions in 
non-aqueous titrations can be explained by the Bronsted-Lowry Theory, which defines acids as proton 
donors and bases as proton acceptors. An acidic substance behaves as an acid only in the presence of a 
base that can accept a proton, and similarly, a basic substance acts as a base only when an acid from 
which it can accept a proton is present.

--- OCR TEXT ---
V. General Procedure for Argentometric Titration (Mohr method example)
1. Equipment and Reagents:
o Solid reagents: NaCl, CaCO3, NaHCO3, K2Cr04, AgNO3.

o Equipment: Burette, transfer pipette (25 mL) and pipette pump, Erlenmeyer flasks (250 mL),
desiccator, volumetric flask (500 mL), amber bottle, graduated cylinder (100 mL), wash
bottle.

o Preparation of 5% K2Cr04 indicator: Dissolve 1.0 g of K2Cr04 in 20 mL of distilled water.

2. Titration Process (Example with diluted seawater for chloride determination):

o Dilute aseawater sample by pipetting 20 mL into a 100 mL volumetric flask and diluting to
the mark with distilled water.

o Pipette a 10 mL aliquot of the diluted seawater into a conical flask.
o Add about 50 mL of distilled water and 1 mL of Chromate indicator.
o Titrate the sample with 0.1 mole L-1 silver nitrate solution from a burette.

o Observe the color: The silver chloride that forms is a white precipitate, and the chromate
indicator initially gives the cloudy solution a faint lemon-yellow color.

o Endpoint: The endpoint is identified as the first appearance of a red-brown color of silver
chromate.

o Repeat the titration with further aliquots of diluted seawater until concordant results (titers
agreeing within 0.1 mL) are obtained.

4. Non-Aqueous Titration
I. Introduction

Non-aqueous titration refers to a titration method where the analyte substance is dissolved in a solvent
that does not contain water. This procedure is very important for pharmacopoeial assays.

Il. Theory

The need for non-aqueous titration arises because water can behave as both a weak acid and a weak base.
This dual nature allows water to compete in proton acceptance or donation with other weak acids and
bases dissolved in it. For example, in an aqueous solvent, the reaction of a weak base (R-NH2) with a proton
(H+) to form R-NH3+ is competed by water reacting with H+ to form H30+. This competition makes it difficult
to detect the end point precisely for very weak acids or bases in aqueous solutions.

Non-aqueous titration provides a solution by using a solvent that doesn't interfere with the proton transfer,
and it also offers the ability to dissolve organic compounds that are insoluble in water. Many reactions in
non-aqueous titrations can be explained by the Bronsted-Lowry Theory, which defines acids as proton
donors and bases as proton acceptors. An acidic substance behaves as an acid only in the presence of a
base that can accept a proton, and similarly, a basic substance acts as a base only when an acid from
which it can accept a proton is present.

============================================================
PAGE 119
============================================================
III. 
Types of Non-Aqueous Solvents 
There are typically four types of solvents used in non-aqueous titration, categorized by their proton-
donating or accepting abilities: 
 
Aprotic Solvents: 
o Characteristics: These solvents are neutral in charge and chemically inert. They generally 
have a low dielectric constant. 
o Use: They can be used as catalysts. 
o Examples: Chloroform and benzene. 
 
Protophilic Solvents: 
o Characteristics: These solvents have a basic character and tend to react with acids by 
accepting protons, leading to the formation of solvated protons. They enhance the acidity of 
the substance being titrated. 
o Examples: Ammonia and pyridine. 
 
Proteogenic Solvents: 
o Characteristics: These solvents have a more acidic character and tend to have a donating 
effect on the bases they come in contact with. 
o Examples: Sulphuric acid and acetic acid. 
 
Amphiprotic Solvents: 
o Characteristics: These solvents possess both protophilic and proteogenic properties. This 
means they can either donate or accept a proton depending on the reacting species. 
o Examples: Acetic acid and alcohols. 
IV. 
Titrants for Non-Aqueous  
Commonly perchloric acid in acetic acid (for bases, amine salts) and potassium methoxide in toluene-
methanol (for acids)  
V. 
Indicators for Non-Aqueous Titration 
Visual indicators like crystal violet, methyl red, thymol blue, I-naphthol benzene, oracet blue B are used. 
Potentiometric methods can also accurately measure endpoints. 
VI. 
Advantages and Applications 
Non-aqueous titrations offer several advantages and applications: 
 
Elimination of Poor Solubility: Organic acids and bases that are insoluble in water can be 
effectively titrated because they are soluble in non-aqueous solvents. 
 
Enhancement of Reactivity: These solvents are useful for titrating very weak acids or bases that 
cannot be accurately titrated in water, as they enhance their reactivity. 
 
Selective Titration: By choosing suitable solvents and titrants, selective titration of acidic/basic 
components of physiologically active salts is possible. 
 
Efficiency: They maintain speed, precision, accuracy, and simplicity in analysis.

--- OCR TEXT ---

IV. Titrants for Non-Aqueous

Commonly perchloric acid in acetic acid (for bases, amine salts) and potassium methoxide in toluene-
methanol (for acids)

V. Indicators for Non-Aqueous Titration

Visual indicators like crystal violet, methyl red, thymol blue, |-naphthol benzene, oracet blue B are used.
Potentiometric methods can also accurately measure endpoints.

VI. Advantages and Applications
Non-aqueous titrations offer several advantages and applications:

¢ Elimination of Poor Solubility: Organic acids and bases that are insoluble in water can be
effectively titrated because they are soluble in non-aqueous solvents.

e Enhancement of Reactivity: These solvents are useful for titrating very weak acids or bases that
cannot be accurately titrated in water, as they enhance their reactivity.

e Selective Titration: By choosing suitable solvents and titrants, selective titration of acidic/basic
components of physiologically active salts is possible.

e Efficiency: They maintain speed, precision, accuracy, and simplicity in analysis.

============================================================
PAGE 120
============================================================
 
Weak Bases Analysis: Weak bases with Kb values less than 10 6 can be satisfactorily titrated. 
 
Karl Fischer Titration: The Karl Fischer titration, used for water content determination, is a non-
aqueous titration typically performed in methanol or ethanol, as water is the analyte and cannot be 
the solvent. 
VII. 
Compounds Titrated by Non-Aqueous Titration 
Acid halides, anhydrides, carboxylic acids, amino acids, enols, imides, phenols, pyrroles, sulfonamides (as 
acids). Amines, nitrogen-containing heterocycles, quaternary ammonium compounds, alkali salts of 
organic/inorganic acids, and some amine salts (as bases). 
5. Complexometric Titration 
Complexometric titration, also known as chelatometry, is a form of volumetric analysis that involves the 
formation of a colored complex to precisely determine the endpoint of a titration. This method is 
particularly useful for quantifying mixtures of different metal ions in solution. 
I. 
Principle 
Complexometric titration is a volumetric analysis technique where the formation of a colored complex 
indicates the end point of a titration. In this method, a metal atom, typically with an empty d orbital, is 
bound by polydentate ligands to form a complex. The completion of this complex formation signifies the 
end point. 
In theory, any complexation reaction can be used as a volumetric technique if certain conditions are met: 
 
The reaction must reach equilibrium rapidly after each addition of titrant. 
 
Interfering situations, such as the stepwise formation of multiple complexes, should not arise. 
 
A complexometric indicator capable of accurately locating the equivalence point must be 
available. 
II. 
Key Reagent 
EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic Acid) 
In practice, EDTA is the most widely established titrant for complexometric titrations. 
 
Structure and Binding: EDTA has four carboxyl groups and two amine groups, which can act as 
electron pair donors (Lewis bases). This enables EDTA to potentially donate its six lone pairs of 
electrons to form coordinate covalent bonds with metal cations, classifying it as a hexadentate 
ligand. However, in practical applications, EDTA is usually only partially ionized, forming fewer than 
six coordinate covalent bonds with metal cations. 
o 
 is commonly used to standardize aqueous solutions of transition 
metal cations. It forms only four coordinate covalent bonds with metal cations at pH values 
equal to or less than 12, because in this pH range, the amine groups remain protonated and 
cannot donate electrons for bond formation. 
o Complex Formation: EDTA forms an octahedral complex with most 2+ metal cations (M²⁺) 
in aqueous solution. The high formation constant for most metal cation-EDTA complexes 
means the equilibrium for the reaction M²⁺ + H Y  MH Y + 2H⁺ lies far to the right. 
Performing the reaction in a basic buffer solution removes H⁺ as it is formed, which further

Karl Fischer Titration: The Karl Fischer titration, used for water content determination, is anon-

the solvent.

acids). Amines, nitrogen-containing heterocycles, quaternary ammonium compounds, alkali salts of
organic/inorganic acids, and some amine salts (as bases).

5. Complexometric Titration

Complexometric titration, also known as chelatometry, is a form of volumetric analysis that involves the
formation of a colored complex to precisely determine the endpoint of a titration. This method is
particularly useful for quantifying mixtures of different metal ions in solution.

I.

Principle

Complexometric titration is a volumetric analysis technique where the formation of a colored complex
indicates the end point of a titration. In this method, a metal atom, typically with an empty d orbital, is
bound by polydentate ligands to form a complex. The completion of this complex formation signifies the
end point.

In theory, any complexation reaction can be used as a volumetric technique if certain conditions are met:

I.

The reaction must reach equilibrium rapidly after each addition of titrant.
Interfering situations, such as the stepwise formation of multiple complexes, should not arise.

Acomplexometric indicator capable of accurately locating the equivalence point must be
available.

Key Reagent

EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic Acid)

In practice, EDTA is the most widely established titrant for complexometric titrations.

Structure and Binding: EDTA has four carboxyl groups and two amine groups, which can act as
electron pair donors (Lewis bases). This enables EDTA to potentially donate its six lone pairs of
electrons to form coordinate covalent bonds with metal cations, classifying it as a hexadentate
ligand. However, in practical applications, EDTA is usually only partially ionized, forming fewer than
six coordinate covalent bonds with metal cations.

o Disodium EDTA (Na,H,Y) is commonly used to standardize aqueous solutions of transition
metal cations. It forms only four coordinate covalent bonds with metal cations at pH values
equal to or less than 12, because in this pH range, the amine groups remain protonated and
cannot donate electrons for bond formation.

o Complex Formation: EDTA forms an octahedral complex with most 2+ metal cations (M? 4
in aqueous solution. The high formation constant for most metal cation-EDTA complexes
means the equilibrium for the reaction M2 *+ H,Y > MH, + 2H “lies far to the right.
Performing the reaction in a basic buffer solution removes H “as it is formed, which further

============================================================
PAGE 121
============================================================
favors the formation of the EDTA-metal cation complex product. For most purposes, the 
formation of the metal cation-EDTA complex can be considered to go to completion, which is 
a primary reason for EDTA's extensive use in these titrations. 
III. 
Indicators for Endpoint Detection 
To perform metal cation titrations using EDTA, a complexometric indicator is almost always necessary to 
determine when the end point has been reached. 
 
Mechanism: Common indicators are organic dyes, such as Eriochrome Black T (EBT) or Fast 
Sulphone Black. They initially form a colored complex with the metal ion in solution. As EDTA is 
added, it binds more strongly to the metal ions, displacing the indicator from the metal cations. The 
end point is signaled when the free indicator, which has a different color than the metal-indicator 
complex, is released. For example, EBT typically gives a blue color initially, and the end point is 
noted when the solution changes to violet. 
 
Requirements for Ideal Indicators:  
o The color reaction must occur before the end point, when nearly all the metal ion is 
complexed with EDTA, and the solution is strongly colored. 
o The color reaction should be specific or selective. 
o The metal-indicator complex must have sufficient stability to ensure a sharp color change, 
but it must be less stable than the metal-EDTA complex so that EDTA can displace the 
metal ions from the indicator complex at the end point. 
o The color contrast between the free indicator and the metal-indicator complex must be 
easily observable. 
o The indicator must be very sensitive to metal ions so that the color change occurs as close 
to the equivalence point as possible. 
IV. 
Selectivity, Masking, and Demasking 
EDTA is a very unselective reagent as it complexes with numerous doubly, triply, and quadruply charged 
cations. To overcome this and allow for selective titration of specific ions in a mixture, several procedures 
can be used: 
 
Suitable Control of pH: By adjusting the pH of the solution, one can exploit the different stabilities 
of metal-EDTA complexes. For example, bismuth and thorium can be titrated in an acidic solution 
(pH = 2) without interference from most divalent cations. A mixture of bismuth and lead ions can be 
successively titrated by first titrating bismuth at pH 2, then raising the pH to about 5 to titrate lead. 
 
Use of Masking Agents: Masking is the process where a substance is transformed so it no longer 
participates in a particular reaction, without physical separation. Masking agents bind to certain 
cations, preventing them from reacting with EDTA or the indicator.  
o Cyanide ion is an effective masking agent, forming stable cyanide complexes with Cd, Zn, 
Hg (II), Cu, Co, Ni, Ag, and platinum metals, but not with alkaline earths, manganese, and 
lead. This allows for the determination of cations like Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺, Pb²⁺, and Mn²⁺ in the 
presence of these masked metals. 
o Ascorbic acid can mask iron (III) by reducing it to iron (II).

--- OCR TEXT ---
II,

favors the formation of the EDTA-metal cation complex product. For most purposes, the
formation of the metal cation-EDTA complex can be considered to go to completion, which is
a primary reason for EDTA's extensive use in these titrations.

Indicators for Endpoint Detection

To perform metal cation titrations using EDTA, a complexometric indicator is almost always necessary to
determine when the end point has been reached.

Mechanism: Common indicators are organic dyes, such as Eriochrome Black T (EBT) or Fast
Sulphone Black. They initially form a colored complex with the metal ion in solution. As EDTA is
added, it binds more strongly to the metal ions, displacing the indicator from the metal cations. The
end point is signaled when the free indicator, which has a different color than the metal-indicator
complex, is released. For example, EBT typically gives a blue color initially, and the end point is
noted when the solution changes to violet.

o Thecolor reaction must occur before the end point, when nearly all the metal ion is
complexed with EDTA, and the solution is strongly colored.

o Thecolor reaction should be specific or selective.

o Thecolor contrast between the free indicator and the metal-indicator complex must be

o The indicator must be very sensitive to metal ions so that the color change occurs as close
to the equivalence point as possible.

Selectivity, Masking, and Demasking

EDTA is a very unselective reagent as it complexes with numerous doubly, triply, and quadruply charged
cations. To overcome this and allow for selective titration of specific ions in a mixture, several procedures
can be used:

Suitable Control of pH: By adjusting the pH of the solution, one can exploit the different stabilities
of metal-EDTA complexes. For example, bismuth and thorium can be titrated in an acidic solution

(pH = 2) without interference from most divalent cations. A mixture of bismuth and lead ions can be
successively titrated by first titrating bismuth at pH 2, then raising the pH to about 5 to titrate lead.

Use of Masking Agents: Masking is the process where a substance is transformed so it no longer
participates in a particular reaction, without physical separation. Masking agents bind to certain
cations, preventing them from reacting with EDTA or the indicator.

o Cyanide ion is an effective masking agent, forming stable cyanide complexes with Cd, Zn,
Hg (Il), Cu, Co, Ni, Ag, and platinum metals, but not with alkaline earths, manganese, and
lead. This allows for the determination of cations like Ca? * Mg? % Pb? * and Mn? “in the
presence of these masked metals.

o Ascorbic acid can mask iron (Ill) by reducing it to iron (Il).

============================================================
PAGE 122
============================================================
o Triethanolamine can mask titanium (IV), iron (III), and aluminum. 
o Iodide ions can mask mercury. 
o Ammonium fluoride can mask aluminum, iron (III), titanium (IV), and tin (II). 
 
Selective Demasking: Demasking is the process by which a masked substance regains its ability to 
react. This allows for successive titrations of multiple metals in a single sample. For example, the 
cyanide complexes of zinc and cadmium can be demasked using formaldehyde-acetic acid solution 
or chloral hydrate, enabling their subsequent titration. 
V. 
Endpoint Detection Methods 
Beyond visual indicators, instrumental methods offer greater accuracy. 
 
Visual Methods:  
o Metallochromic or PM indicators: As described above (e.g., EBT). 
o pH indicators: Though complexometric titrations primarily involve metal ions, pH changes 
can occur due to the release or consumption of protons during complexation, allowing pH 
indicators to be used in some cases. 
o Redox indicators: If the complexation reaction is coupled with a redox change that affects 
an indicator's oxidation state. 
 
Instrumental Methods:  
o Photometry: Measures the absorption of light by the solution during titration, if the 
spectrum of the reactant, titrant, or product is known. 
o Potentiometry: Measures the electrode potential of the solution, which changes as the 
endpoint is reached. 
o Miscellaneous methods: Other techniques like conductivity measurements, or isothermal 
titration calorimetry can also be used. 
VI. 
Mechanism and Calculation 
In a typical complexometric titration: 
1. The solution containing the metal ion (analyte) is placed in a conical flask. 
2. A few drops of a suitable indicator (e.g., EBT) are added to the solution. 
3. The EDTA solution (titrant) is slowly added from a burette. 
4. As EDTA is added, it complexes with the metal ions, and the indicator changes color once all the 
metal ions are complexed by EDTA, signaling the end point. 
Calculations: The volume of titrant used is employed to calculate the concentration of the metal ion in the 
mixture. The formula used is: N (normality) of Analyte = [N (normality) of titrant × V (volume) of titrant] 
/ V(volume of analyte) The normality of the titrant is known as it's a standard solution. 
Titration Curves (Graphs) 
Complexometric titration graphs typically have an "S" shape.

The solution containing the metal ion (analyte) is placed in a conical flask.

Titration Curves (Graphs)

Complexometric titration graphs typically have an "S" shape.

============================================================
PAGE 123
============================================================
 
Initial Phase: A minute change in pH or potential value is observed as the titrant is added, due to 
the gradual formation of the complex. 
 
Steep Rise: A steep rise occurs when the complex formation is completed, and the value changes 
drastically with even a single drop of titrant. This steep part of the curve represents the equivalence 
point. 
 
Constant Phase: After the equivalence point, the value becomes constant again as all the metal 
concentration has been consumed in complex formation. Graphs can be plotted using instrumental 
methods, which often provide more accurate determination of the endpoint compared to manual 
observation. 
VII. 
Applications 
Complexometric titrations are widely used in various fields: 
 
Water Hardness Determination: Used to estimate the total hardness in water. 
 
Pharmaceutical Industry: Widely employed to determine metal concentration in drugs. 
 
Cosmetics Industry: For example, titanium dioxide, used in many cosmetic products, can be 
analyzed by complexometric titration.
